A man can argue with an accusation; he can hardly argue with a category that already contains his neighbours, his trade rivals and the ruins on the road north.

That quiet enrolment sets up what follows. The sūrah will now give the reason all six communities fell, in a single clause, and the clause will apply to whoever is reading it as directly as it applies to the dead.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:14

> Each rejected their messenger, so My punishment was justified

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Verb Covers Six Communities**

The verse compresses the histories of the previous two verses into a single sentence, and the compression is the argument. Six peoples, spanning many centuries, differing in wealth, technology, geography and sin, are given one verb between them: they denied. Not *some denied and others compromised*, not *they doubted* or *they delayed* — they denied, and the denial is presented as identical in kind across all of them.

That uniformity is a claim worth noticing, because it is not obvious. The people of Noah mocked; the people of Lot committed acts the Qur'an singles out as unprecedented; Pharaoh claimed divinity for himself; the Forest people cheated in the market. Their sins were not the same. What was the same, on this account, is the thing underneath: a warner was sent, and the warning was refused. The verse reduces a great variety of wrongdoing to one decisive act, and in doing so it tells the reader which act the whole Book considers decisive.

**"Was Justified": A Word From the Courtroom**

The verb translated *was justified* is ḥaqqa, from the root meaning to be true, to be established, to become due. It is the language of a claim that has been proven and a debt that has matured. The punishment is not described as inflicted, sent or decided; it is described as having become owed, and the owing is the result of the denial rather than of a decree that preceded it.

The legal register is consistent across the Qur'an's accounts of destruction. God does not punish arbitrarily and does not punish early. A term is set, a messenger is sent, evidence is produced, and only then does the sentence mature. The word ḥaqqa therefore does important theological work in this verse: it locates the cause of the punishment in the conduct of the punished. The verse could have said *so I destroyed them*; it says instead *My punishment was justified*, which places the burden of proof on the destroyed.

**Denying One Messenger Is Denying the Message**

The phrase *their messenger* is singular for each community, and the Qur'an elsewhere generalises the point: rejecting any one messenger is treated as rejecting the office itself. *“The people of Lot rejected the messengers”* (26:160), says al-Shuʿarāʾ, using the plural for a people who met only one man. The same plural is used for the residents of the Forest: *“The residents of the Forest rejected the messengers”* (26:176).

The grammar is making a substantive claim. A prophet does not arrive as an individual with a personal teaching; he arrives as the bearer of a message that has been delivered before, and to refuse him is to refuse the line he stands in. This is why the Qur'an insists that its own message is a confirmation of what came earlier, and why the charge of novelty — we have never heard this in the previous faith — is answered by pointing to Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ and the rest. The Quraysh were not facing a new claim. They were facing an old one delivered by a new voice.

**The Delay Was Part of the Justice**

Between the denial and the maturing of the punishment lay years, and the verse's structure makes those years part of the sentence rather than an absence of it. Nothing in the account suggests that the punishment could have arrived sooner and was withheld out of indifference; the consistent pattern is that a term was appointed and honoured.

This is what makes the verse a warning rather than a threat. A threat announces a power; a warning announces a schedule. The destroyed peoples had time in which the sentence had not yet matured, and they used it to harden. The audience of this sūrah is inside the same interval, and the verse tells them what the interval is for. The next verse will describe how the interval ends, in a single blast.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

Two absences in this sentence are as instructive as its contents. The first is any reference to the communities' strength, wealth or numbers — the categories in which the Quraysh measured themselves. None of it registers here. The second is any reference to the content of the messengers' preaching beyond the fact that they were rejected. No doctrine is restated, no sign is recalled.

The omissions narrow the verse to a single proposition: a messenger was sent and refused, and the refusal made the punishment due. Everything else in the histories is incidental to that proposition, which is why the sūrah can list six peoples in two verses and lose nothing essential. The reader who wants the detail will find it elsewhere in the Book; what this chapter needs is the pattern.

**The Sentence Applies Forward**

The verse is written in the past and read in the present. Its grammar belongs to the destroyed communities, but its logic belongs to whoever hears it, and the sūrah has already identified the current occupants of the position. The men of Makkah had received a messenger; the question the verse puts to them is not whether their city is stronger than Iram, but whether they have denied.

That is the whole force of the list. It converts a historical curiosity into a mirror, and it does so without a single word addressed directly to the audience. Nobody is named, nobody is accused, and nobody is told what to do. The sūrah simply lays out what happened to six groups of people who stood where the hearer is standing, states the reason in one clause, and moves on.

The restraint is itself a judgement on the audience. A sermon assumes the listener can be moved; this verse assumes only that he can read a pattern. The reader who does not see himself in it has already answered the question the verse is asking.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:15

> These ˹pagans˺ are awaiting nothing but a single Blast that cannot be stopped

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Blast, and Everything Reduced to One Sound**
The verse takes the whole of the preceding history and reduces it to a single acoustic event. The peoples listed before it were destroyed by flood, wind, an overturning of their towns and a blast, and this verse says that what remains for the current audience is the last of those means, in its simplest form: one cry, one blast, and nothing before it or after it that could change anything.

The word *nothing but* does the narrowing. It excludes negotiation, excludes postponement bought by wealth, excludes the intervention of allies. A people can defend itself against an army, negotiate with a rival, bribe an official, flee a famine. Against a sound there is no procedure. The verse is a statement about the exhaustion of options, and it arrives immediately after the histories that demonstrate the point.

The singularity of the event is a theological claim as much as a descriptive one. Six communities were destroyed by six different means, and the means varied because the circumstances varied; the final reckoning requires only one. There is no army to mobilise, no flood to gather, no wind to send, because the authority behind the event is not operating through intermediaries any longer.

That is the verse's quiet rebuke to the confidence of the Quraysh. They measured power in hosts and structures, and the answer given to them is a sound. The smallest thing in the created order — a vibration in the air — is sufficient to end the largest. Nothing they possess is relevant to the transaction, and nothing they lack can prevent it.

**Fawāq: The Space Between Two Milkings**
The phrase rendered *cannot be stopped* contains a rare word, fawāq, which in ordinary Arabic denotes the interval between one milking of a she-camel and the next — the shortest recognised unit of waiting in a pastoral society. Commentators explain it in three related ways: the blast admits of no delay, no return and no interval. Each reading uses the same image from a different angle.

The choice of a pastoral measure of time is not decoration. A Bedouin audience understood fawāq as the briefest practical pause, the moment you wait while the milk lets down. To say that the blast has no fawāq is to say that there is not even that much space. The verse thereby answers, in a single word, the most common human strategy for dealing with warnings: the assumption that there will be time to respond later.

**Which Blast Is Meant**
Two readings divide the exegetes, and both rest on who *these* refers to. If the pronoun points to the Quraysh alone, the blast is the first sounding of the trumpet, the one that ends the world as it stands. If it points to all the parties named in the previous verses, including the Quraysh, the blast is the second sounding, which raises the dead for the reckoning. The Qur'an describes both in one verse: *“The Trumpet will be blown and all those in the heavens and all those on the earth will fall dead, except those Allah wills ˹to spare˺. Then it will be blown again and they will rise up at once, looking on ˹in anticipation˺.”* (39:68).

The first reading fits the immediate context better. The verses before this one describe the Quraysh as a party awaiting defeat, and the destruction of a community is what the sūrah has been documenting. But the second reading is not excluded, since the two blasts are separated by an interval that no creature experiences as time. What both readings share is the essential claim: a single sound, with nothing in the audience's power to alter it.

**The Blast in the Qur'an's Own Vocabulary**
The Book returns to this image often enough that it becomes a technical term. *“All it took was one ˹mighty˺ blast, and they were extinguished at once.”* (36:29); *“They must be awaiting a single Blast, which will seize them while they are ˹entrenched˺ in ˹worldly˺ disputes.”* (36:49); *“It will only take one Blast, then at once they will see ˹it all˺.”* (37:19); *“But indeed, it will take only one ˹mighty˺ Blast”* (79:13).

The consistency of the wording across Meccan sūrahs is striking. The event is always one, always sudden, and always arriving while the audience is occupied with something else. The detail in Yā-Sīn — seized while they are entrenched in disputes — is the most pointed of these, because it names the occupation: arguing. The people destroyed by the blast were not asleep or distracted by pleasure; they were in the middle of a controversy, and the controversy is precisely what the preceding verses of this sūrah have recorded.

**The Sentence Leaves One Door Open**
Read strictly, the verse is a statement about what is coming and not about what is unavoidable by repentance. The destruction of a community in the Qur'an is always preceded by a term, and the term is always running while the warning is being delivered. The blast arrives for those who are still in dispute when it comes, and the sūrah has spent fourteen verses explaining what the dispute consists of.

The door is not described here, because describing it belongs to other verses. What this verse does is fix the end of the interval: one sound, no pause, no return. Everything between now and then belongs to the audience, and the sūrah will spend its remaining length showing them what the interval looks like from inside the lives of those who used it well.

That is why the histories give way to portraits at this point. A warning establishes a deadline; a portrait establishes a method. The chapter turns from what will happen to the pagans toward what happened to David, and the turn is not a change of subject but a change of address.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:16

> They say ˹mockingly˺, “Our Lord! Hasten for us our share ˹of the punishment˺ before the Day of Reckoning.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mockery Phrased as a Prayer**
The sentence is formally an invocation. It begins with *Our Lord*, uses the imperative, and asks for something to be brought forward. Every marker of piety is present, and every one of them is inverted, because the thing requested is the speaker's own destruction. The verse marks the tone with a single word — *mockingly* — and the marking is necessary, since the sentence read alone would sound like a petition.

That inversion is what makes the mockery more revealing than an insult would be. An insult admits that the object of the insult has weight; a parody of prayer claims that the addressee does not exist. The speakers are not merely rejecting the warning, they are performing their rejection in the vocabulary of the warned. The Qur'an preserves the performance verbatim, which is how the reader is able to hear the sneer rather than be told about it.

**Qiṭṭ: A Word That Means Both Share and Record**
The noun translated *our share* is qiṭṭ, and its range is wider than the English. It denotes a portion allotted to someone, and commentators extend it to three related senses: the book on which a person's deeds are inscribed, his account, and his decree. The connection between the senses is physical as well as semantic; the same root lies behind the idea of a piece cut off, and a written record in the ancient world was a cut piece of parchment or papyrus handed to its owner.

The ambiguity is productive here. The men asking for their qiṭṭ are asking for their portion of punishment, and the word they use is also the word for the document that will be handed to them on the Day of Reckoning. They are demanding, in effect, that the record be delivered early. A second reading takes the request as a demand for their share of the Garden rather than the Fire, on the assumption that they considered themselves righteous; on that reading the mockery is aimed at the promise rather than the threat. Both readings describe the same attitude, which is a certainty about one's own standing.

**Why They Can Afford to Be Funny**
The confidence behind the joke was diagnosed two verses earlier: *they have not yet tasted My punishment* (38:8). Nothing has happened to them. The warning has been issued for years, the rains have come, the caravans have returned, and the man who delivered it remains without power. Under those conditions a threat begins to look like a rumour, and a rumour can be laughed at.

The Qur'an treats this laughter as a symptom rather than an argument. It does not answer the mockery with a counter-threat of its own; it answers by pointing at the interval and explaining what the interval is. A delay granted for the sake of testing is indistinguishable, from inside, from a delay caused by indifference — and the only way to tell them apart is to be told, which is what the warning does. The mockery is therefore evidence of exactly what the warning predicted.

**A Long Line of Impatient Requests**
This is not an isolated outburst but a habit of speech the Qur'an documents across many sūrahs. *“They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ to hasten the torment rather than grace, though there have ˹already˺ been ˹many˺ torments before them.”* (13:6); *“They challenge you ˹O Prophet˺ to hasten the torment. And Allah will never fail in His promise. But a day with your Lord is indeed like a thousand years by your counting.”* (22:47); *“Do they ˹really˺ seek to hasten Our torment?”* (26:204); *“Do they ˹really˺ wish to hasten Our punishment?”* (37:176).

The recurrence has a purpose beyond recording an insult. It establishes that the demand was made, publicly and repeatedly, and that it was not granted on the schedule the demanders set. The Qur'an's answer to the request for haste is a lesson in scales of time: a day with God is a thousand years by human counting. The men asking for immediate settlement were operating on a calendar that the verse declares irrelevant.

**Delayed Forgiveness, and the Turn to the Portraits**
Al-Raʿd supplies the reply this verse leaves unstated, and the reply belongs beside the mockery: *“Surely your Lord is full of forgiveness for people, despite their wrongdoing, and your Lord is truly severe in punishment.”* (13:6). The two halves belong together. Forgiveness is available while the demand is being made in jest, and severity arrives when the jest has become a settled position.

That is the real answer to the request for haste. The delay they are laughing at is the space in which the offer stands, and the offer is not repeated after the interval closes. The men who asked for their portion early were asking to have the door shut on themselves, and the sūrah declines to oblige them until the term set for them has run.

Positioned where it is, this verse closes the section on the objectors. Their psychology has been described, their slogans quoted, their claim to ownership refuted, their precedent listed, their deadline fixed and their mockery recorded. There is nothing further to say to them, and the sūrah says nothing further.

The very next verse turns away from them entirely, and the turn is the point. A revelation that spent its length arguing with mockers would grant the mockers a standing they do not have. Instead the chapter gives the Prophet an instruction about patience and a name to remember, and the rest of the sūrah is given over to the lives of servants who were tested in earnest.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:17

> Be patient ˹O Prophet˺ with what they say. And remember Our servant, David, the man of strength. Indeed, he ˹constantly˺ turned ˹to Allah˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn to a Servant's Life, and Why David Comes First**
Three instructions arrive in one verse, and their order is a method. First, an address about endurance: be patient with what they say. Second, a command of recollection: remember Our servant David. Third, a characterisation of the man to be remembered: strong, and constantly turning. Nothing in the verse argues with the mockers of the previous verse; the argument is over and the chapter moves to consolation and instruction.

The pairing of patience with a prophet's biography is not incidental. Patience commanded in the abstract is a demand; patience shown in the life of a king who fell and rose is a demonstration. The Qur'an uses this device throughout, and it is the reason its narratives are never decorative. The story is the instruction, delivered in a form that can be carried and recalled under pressure.

Of the three servants the chapter will examine, David is placed first, and the placement follows the logic of the argument. The objectors of the opening verses were men of position who refused to submit their judgement to anyone; David is a man of greater position than any of them, holding both a throne and a revelation, who submitted and was corrected.

The comparison is the instruction. If a prophet-king could be tested, could err in a judicial matter, could recognise the error and fall prostrate, then the notables of Makkah have no standing from which to declare themselves beyond correction. The verse introduces him without mentioning the trial, which comes several verses later; it establishes the man's rank first, so that his fall will be read at its proper weight.

**Patience as an Order, Not a Mood**
The same injunction appears in nearly identical words in three other places: *“So be patient ˹O Prophet˺ with what they say. And glorify the praises of your Lord before sunrise and before sunset”* (50:39); *“So be patient ˹O Prophet˺ with what they say. And glorify the praises of your Lord before sunrise and before sunset, and glorify Him in the hours of the night and at both ends of the day, so that you may be pleased ˹with the reward˺.”* (20:130); *“Be patient ˹O Prophet˺ with what they say, and depart from them courteously.”* (73:10). In each case patience is joined to an act of worship, which tells the reader what kind of endurance is meant.

This is not stoicism. Stoic endurance is an internal fortification against an external world; Qur'anic ṣabr is endurance sustained by a practice, and the practice is prayer at fixed hours. Some jurists argued that verses of this kind were superseded by the later command to fight, but the argument is weaker than it looks: the injunction concerns the Prophet's inner bearing under insult, which the permission to fight never addressed. A man may be permitted to fight and still be commanded to bear speech patiently.

**"The Man of Strength": Strength in What**
David is introduced as possessing ayd, a word denoting physical power and, by extension, capacity. Commentators divide over the referent, and both readings are defensible. The first takes it as strength in worship: his capacity for long prayer, alternate-day fasting and unbroken devotion. The second takes it as strength in office, since David uniquely combined prophethood with kingship — he was given both the Book and the throne.

The two readings converge on the same point about the man's character. Strength in the Qur'an is never praised as an end; it is praised as an instrument that is turned toward God. A king's power that produces justice and a worshipper's endurance that produces devotion are the same virtue in different clothing. The verse names the strength and then immediately names what was done with it, which is the pattern the rest of the chapter will follow in the cases of Solomon and Job.

**Awwāb: The One Who Keeps Coming Back**
The closing phrase describes David as one who turns repeatedly, from awwāb, an intensive form built on the root for returning. The word is not used once of David but three times in this chapter — of him here, of Solomon at verse 30, and of Job at verse 44 — and its repetition across the three portraits is the chapter's central claim about its heroes.

What the intensive form adds is frequency. A man who repents once is a penitent; an awwāb is a man whose return is a habit, whose life contains many turns rather than one. The Qur'an applies the same word to those who guard themselves from sin, which suggests that returning is not a failure of the righteous but their normal operation. The strength of the verse's opening is thus qualified by its ending: the strong man is not the one who never slips but the one whose recovery is immediate and repeated.

**The Prayer and the Fast God Loves Best**
The tradition gives concrete form to David's strength, and the report is one of the most specific in the collections. The Prophet ﷺ told ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr that the fasting dearest to God is David's, and the prayer dearest to God is David's: he slept half the night, stood in prayer for a third of it, slept a sixth, fasted one day and broke the next (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3420; also recorded at Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1131 and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1159).

The arithmetic of the report is what makes it memorable. A night divided into halves, thirds and sixths; a calendar divided into alternating days. Neither schedule is maximal — the man sleeps more than half the night in total, and he fasts only half his days — and the same conversation records the Prophet ﷺ refusing ʿAbdullāh's request for more. The strength praised here is sustainable strength. A devotion that collapses under its own weight is not the model the Qur'an sets before a prophet who is being told to endure years of insult.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:18

> We truly subjected the mountains to hymn ˹Our praises˺ along with him in the evening and after sunrise

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mountains Given a Task**
The verb governing this verse is one of subjection: the mountains are made to do something, and what they are made to do is praise. The construction is unusual enough to require attention, because praise is not the sort of act normally assigned to geology. The Qur'an's claim is that the mountains joined David in an act of worship at fixed times, and the joining is described as something arranged rather than something that happened to occur.

Three readings of the arrangement are available. The mountains may have responded audibly, so that a traveller near David's prayer heard the hills answer him. They may have been inclined by God to a praise inaudible to human ears, with David alone aware of the joining. Or the expression may describe the effect of his voice in a landscape, where sound returns from rock and the praise seems to be answered. The first reading is the plain sense and the one the exegetes generally hold; the third is the weakest, since it turns a statement about divine action into a statement about acoustics.

**Everything Already Praises, and David Is Joined to It**
The praise of creation is not a peculiarity of this verse but a standing doctrine of the Qur'an. Whole sūrahs open with it: everything in the heavens and the earth glorifies God (59:1; 61:1; 62:1; 64:1). The praise is continuous and universal, and it does not depend on anyone hearing it. What this verse adds is not the praise but the coordination: the mountains praise *with him*.

The addition matters theologically. If all things praise at all times, then the mountains praising at David's hours is a special alignment, and alignment of that kind is a sign of office. Two parallel verses make the same arrangement: David is given the mountains and the birds as companions in praise elsewhere in the Book, and in Saba the command is addressed to both together — mountains and birds, returning with him (34:10). The consistent pairing suggests a role rather than an anecdote.

**At the Two Ends of the Day**
The times named are the evening and after sunrise, and the pairing covers the two transitions of the day — the closing of light and its return. Commentators read the phrase either as the two ends of the day, marking a routine, or as a way of saying *at all times*, using the extremes to include the middle.

The specific reference of the second time has been debated. Many take it as the period after the dawn prayer, when the sun has risen and the supererogatory prayer of the forenoon, al-ḍuḥā, is offered. That reading has the advantage of matching an established practice; it also explains why the verse mentions a time after sunrise rather than sunrise itself. A routine anchored at the two ends of the day is a discipline, and the verse is describing a discipline rather than a mood: fixed hours, repeated, with the world joining in.

**The Voice of a King and the Answer of a Landscape**
The tradition preserves a detail that makes the scene concrete rather than emblematic. David's voice was proverbial in the Islamic tradition for its beauty, and the phrase used to describe it is drawn from music: the Prophet ﷺ told Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī, on hearing his recitation, that he had been given one of the *mizāmīr* — the pipes or flutes — of the family of David (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 5048; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 793).

The comparison is informative about what the family of David was known for. A mizmār is an instrument of sustained, carried sound, and to say that a man's voice resembles one is to say that it carries across distance and holds a note. The next verse will describe the birds gathering, and the tradition of David's voice supplies the reason the gathering is plausible within the narrative: a voice that draws a crowd of birds is a voice that would carry into a valley.

The report is recorded in the two most authoritative collections. The Prophet ﷺ said to Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī, after hearing him recite, that he had been given one of the flutes of the family of David (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 5048; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 793). The comparison is to the psalms, and it treats the beauty of the voice as an inheritance rather than an accomplishment.

**Two Details, and a Vicegerent Whose Prayer the World Joins**
The theological weight of the verse is best read alongside the charge David will be given later in the chapter: he is appointed a representative on earth (38:26). The same idea appears in the general statement about human succession on the earth (6:165). A vicegerent is a stand-in, and a stand-in acts on behalf of the one he represents.

On that reading the mountains' joining is not a miracle granted to a favourite but a consequence of an office. The representative of God on earth prays, and the creation that belongs to God joins its representative's prayer. The arrangement shows what the office is for: not domination of the world but the leading of it in its proper act. David is strong, and the first use of his strength recorded in this chapter is worship, with the landscape as his congregation.

The verse gives only two facts — the subjection of the mountains and the fixed hours — and the two facts are chosen to make a single point. Strength, named in the previous verse, is shown here in operation, and its operation is worship rather than war or building. The chapter will give David a kingdom, a judgement and a fall; its opening description of him is of a man at prayer at dawn, with the hills answering.

That ordering is deliberate. Everything that follows in the story of David is to be read against this scene, including the error and the prostration. A king whose first recorded act in this chapter is worship is a king whose fall will be followed by return, and the return is already implied in the word used of him a verse earlier.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:19

> And ˹We subjected˺ the birds, flocking together. All turned to him ˹echoing his hymns˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**Birds Gathered, Not Scattered**
The verse continues the subjection begun in the previous one and changes the direction of the movement. Mountains are fixed; birds are not, and the natural response of a flock to a human voice is to scatter. What the verse describes is the opposite: they are gathered, the word used being one of assembling or mustering, the same term used elsewhere for a people brought together for judgement.

The reversal is the content of the miracle. A creature's instinct is its given law, and a bird's given law in the presence of a man is flight. To have the flock hold its position and close ranks is a suspension of the ordinary order, and the verse reports it in a single participle without elaboration. The absence of explanation is characteristic of the chapter's portraits, which state what happened and leave the mechanism to God.

**A Pronoun With Two Referents**
The final phrase contains a pronoun that commentators divide over. Read as referring to God, it says that all of them — David and the birds — turned repeatedly to Him, and the birds' turning is then a genuine praise in their own mode. Read as referring to David, it says that the birds turned toward him, following his lead and echoing his hymns. The grammatical form allows both, and the immediate context favours the second while the wider context favours the first.

Both readings cannot be dismissed, and the difference between them is instructive. On the first, the birds are worshippers in their own right and David is their imam; on the second, they are an audience arranged around a voice. The first reading fits the doctrine that all creatures glorify without exception, and it fits the intensive word for turning, which the chapter reserves for conscious agents — David, Solomon and Job. The second fits the plain sequence of the narrative, in which the birds are mustered for a purpose. The stronger position holds them together: the birds turn toward him because they turn to God, and his prayer is the occasion of theirs.

**A Voice That Drew a Crowd**
The Islamic tradition consistently explains the gathering by the beauty of David's voice, and the explanation is supported by the phrase used elsewhere in the collections. When the Prophet ﷺ heard Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī recite, he told him he had been given one of the *mizāmīr* of the family of David (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 5048; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 793) — a pipe, an instrument whose sound carries and sustains.

The comparison explains the verse without diminishing it. A voice that carries across a valley will reach birds on the wing, and the miracle lies not in the birds hearing but in their staying. The tradition also tells the reader something about the character of David's worship: it was sung, and its beauty was part of its function. The Qur'an's account of praise repeatedly treats sound as an instrument of devotion, and this verse is the clearest case in the Book of a non-human congregation assembling for it.

**The Gathering, and the Word Shared by Birds and Prophets**
The intensive noun applied to those who turn appears in the previous verse of David, in verse 30 of Solomon and in verse 44 of Job, and here it is extended to the birds. The extension is not a casual reuse. The word describes a repeated turning, an act performed many times and by disposition, and applying it to a flock attributes to the birds a settled pattern rather than a momentary reaction.

That attribution is what makes the scene a congregation instead of a spectacle. A spectacle is watched; a congregation participates. The chapter's insistence on the word across its three portraits binds the birds into the same category as its prophets, and the binding is a claim about the world: praise is the natural activity of creation, and a human being who performs it well is not alone in performing it.

Two things follow from the scene, and both matter for the argument the chapter is making. The first concerns David's office. A man whose prayer is joined by mountains and birds is functioning as a representative of creation, and the representation is visible in a way no decree could make it. The second concerns the objectors of the opening verses.

The men of Makkah measured worth in followers, wealth and rank. The verse answers with a different measure: what gathers around a man when he stands to pray. Nobody can be recruited into that assembly, nobody can pay for a place in it, and it contains creatures that no chief of Quraysh could command. The comparison is quiet, and it is devastating to the values the chapter has been dismantling since its second verse.

**Sound, Not Force**
Read beside the previous verse, this one completes a picture of power that runs against every assumption in the opening of the sūrah. David's strength was named in verse 17, and the first two demonstrations of it involve no coercion of any human being. Mountains are inclined and birds are gathered, in both cases for an act of worship rather than for a task of labour or war.

The chapter will soon show the same man sitting as a judge, and later it will show Solomon commanding builders, divers and the wind. Both portraits are set against this scene deliberately. Authority in the Qur'an is legitimate when it leads creatures toward their proper act, and the proper act of everything that exists is praise.

That is the standard against which the chapter measures every ruler it names, including the rulers who rejected the message. A chief of Quraysh could command men by wealth and lineage, and the verse does not dispute the fact. It disputes the value of the command, by showing a king whose authority reaches a flock of birds that owe him nothing and can leave at any moment — and stay.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:20

> We strengthened his kingship, and gave him wisdom and sound judgment

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Kingdom Strengthened From Outside**
Three gifts are listed, and the grammar of the first is the key to all three. The kingship is *strengthened*, not created or granted outright, and the strengthening is done by God to something David already held. The tradition records that his rule was the most powerful of its time, but the verse's construction attributes the power to an external source: a kingdom is as strong as the aid behind it.

That attribution answers the assumption the opening verses of the chapter were built on. The notables of Makkah treated position as an asset a man owns and defends. The verse describes position as a deposit that is held up by someone else, and the holding up can be withdrawn. Solomon's kingdom later in this chapter is described in the same way, as something subjected to him rather than achieved by him. Nothing in the Qur'an's portraits of power allows a king to say that he built his own strength.

**Wisdom: Prophethood or Judgement**
The second gift, ḥikmah, is read by commentators in two directions. The first identifies it with prophethood, since ḥikmah in the Qur'an is frequently paired with the Book and given to prophets. The second takes it as understanding and sound reasoning — the practical intelligence that lets a ruler decide correctly. The verse gives no gloss, and the ambiguity is functional rather than accidental.

The two readings converge in the case of David, who held both offices, and the convergence is the reason he is introduced here rather than in the narrative that follows. A judge who is not a prophet judges by evidence and inference; a prophet judges by evidence and revelation. The chapter's account of David's trial turns precisely on the difference, and the mention of wisdom in advance tells the reader that whatever goes wrong in the story is not a failure of intelligence.

**Faṣl al-Khiṭāb: Speech That Cuts Clean**
The third gift is the most specific and the most discussed. The phrase translated *sound judgment* is faṣl al-khiṭāb, literally the separation of speech, and the noun faṣl carries the senses of division, decision and discrimination. Commentators explain it as clarity and truthfulness of speech, as the knowledge required to issue decrees and administer justice, and as the ability to separate a plaintiff's case from a defendant's and settle between them.

The image is of a cut. A judge's sentence divides what was tangled: this belongs to you, that to him, and the line is drawn once and for all. Speech that does this is not merely eloquent; it is decisive in the strict sense that it ends a dispute. The Qur'an's description of David as possessing faṣl al-khiṭāb is therefore a professional qualification as much as a virtue, and it prepares the reader for the courtroom scene that occupies the next several verses.

**Three Gifts in Order, and the Qur'an's Own Method**
Faṣl is related to faṣṣala, to set out in detail or to expound, and that verb is used of revelation itself: the Qur'an is described as a Book whose signs have been set out clearly (41:3; 7:52; 11:1). The connection between the two uses is not etymological curiosity. Both describe an act of making distinctions visible — separating truth from falsehood in a case, or separating guidance from error in a text.

On this reading the gift given to David is a share in the activity that defines revelation. God expounds; the prophet expounds; the judge decides. Each performs a separation that others cannot perform, and each is dependent on the one above. The verse's three gifts therefore form a chain rather than a list: a kingdom held up, an understanding given, and a speech that cuts.

The sequence is not arbitrary. Power comes first, then wisdom, then decisive speech, and the order describes how authority actually operates: a man is given a position, then the capacity to use it, then the instrument through which it is exercised. Each gift is useless without the one before it. Power without understanding is tyranny; understanding without the ability to articulate a decision is paralysis.

The ordering also carries a warning that the chapter will activate shortly. The man who holds all three can still err, and when he does, the error will occur in the exercise of the third gift — the judgement he issues before hearing both sides. The verse establishes the endowment so that the failure can be located precisely, and the location of the failure is what makes the repentance that follows meaningful.

**A Portrait of Power That Points Away From Itself**
Everything in this verse is given, and the giving is explicit in each clause. The kingdom is strengthened by God, the wisdom is bestowed, the speech is granted. David contributes nothing to the endowment, and the chapter is careful to say so before it shows him at work.

That care is aimed at the audience of the sūrah. The men who asked why the reminder had come to one man out of all of them are shown a king whose throne, mind and voice are all loans. The comparison is not stated, because it does not need to be. A reader who has followed the chapter from its second verse will supply it himself.

The gifts also define the terms on which the trial will be judged. A man given decisive speech is held to a standard of decision-making that an ordinary litigant is not, and the severity of his self-reproach later in the chapter is proportionate to the endowment recorded here. Nothing in this verse excuses what is coming; everything in it explains why the coming events matter.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:21

> Has the story of the two plaintiffs, who scaled the ˹wall of David’s˺ sanctuary, reached you ˹O Prophet˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Opens a Case File**
The verse is a question about whether a report has arrived, and the question is a device rather than a doubt. Nobody is being asked for information; the audience is being placed in the position of someone about to hear a case. The form — *has the news of this reached you* — is used elsewhere in the Qur'an to introduce a narrative the listener will be held to, and its effect is to make the telling an event rather than a recital.

What follows is the chapter's longest continuous narrative, and it is introduced in a single clause that gives no name, no date, no place and no outcome. The two men are unnamed; the location is only *the sanctuary*; the nature of the dispute is not stated. Every detail that a historian would supply is withheld, and what remains is the shape of the encounter. That withholding is the method of the Qur'an's storytelling throughout: the particulars are stripped until only the moral geometry is left.

**Two Plaintiffs, and the Address to the Prophet**
The question is directed to the Prophet, and the direction matters for two reasons. The first is the claim it carries: he is being told something he did not witness, about a prophet who lived a thousand years before him, in a society that had no access to the records of that life. The Meccan audience knew this, and the Qur'an elsewhere makes the argument explicit — that news of the unseen is being given to him without his having been present (3:44).

The second reason is pastoral. The sūrah has just told him to be patient and to remember David, and this verse supplies the remembering. The instruction and its content are joined without a transition, which is how the chapter treats every command: the command is followed immediately by the material that makes it bearable.

The Arabic word for the pair, khaṣm, is grammatically singular and is used here with a dual verb, a construction the language permits for nouns that denote a party or a class. Commentators note the usage because it affects how the scene is read: a khaṣm is not simply an opponent but a litigant, a person who has a case and has come to have it heard.

The choice of a legal noun over a word for intruder or attacker is significant. From the first mention, the two men are defined by what they want — a judgement — and not by what they did to get there. The verse therefore establishes the genre of the scene before the reader knows its content, and the genre is a courtroom.

**Scaling the Wall of a Sealed Chamber**
The detail the verse chooses to give is the strangest one available. The two men did not request an audience, did not wait their turn and did not come through a door. They climbed over. The word used for the sanctuary is miḥrāb, which in the usage of the period denotes a private chamber or an elevated place of worship rather than the architectural feature the word later came to name.

The commentators explain the fear that follows by reference to this breach: David's chamber was difficult of access and he had ordered that no one be admitted. Two strangers appearing inside a sealed room would reasonably be taken for assassins. The Qur'an does not explain the fear and does not resolve how the entry happened, which leaves the scene suspended between a legal proceeding and a supernatural intrusion — a suspension the next verse will hold for one line before dissolving it.

**Why the Story Begins With Its Oddity**
Every narrative has details that could be omitted, and the ones a text keeps are the ones doing work. This verse keeps the climbing and drops everything else, which tells the reader that the irregularity of the entry is the point of the opening.

Two readings follow from that. On one, the breach signals urgency: the plaintiff's case cannot wait for protocol, and a man who believes he is being ruined will not queue. On the other, the breach signals that these are not ordinary litigants, and the scene is being arranged by someone other than the two men. The chapter does not choose between them here. It gives the odd detail, notes the king's alarm, and lets the following verses disclose what the intrusion was for.

The withholding of names deserves a word as well, because it is a consistent feature of Qur'anic narrative and it has a purpose. A named man belongs to his century, his tribe and his circumstances, and the reader can file him away as an instance. An unnamed man is a type, and a type is something the reader can become. The two litigants in this story have no biography, which is what allows the account to be read as a case rather than as an anecdote.

**The Case Is Set Down Before the Reader**
By the end of the verse the reader has been given a room, a king, two claimants and an unresolved entry. Nothing has been explained and nothing has been judged. The Qur'an's method here is to make the reader a party to the case before the facts arrive, so that the judgement — when it comes — is measured against the reader's own expectation rather than against a summary.

That arrangement is what gives the following verses their force. The dispute will be presented in the plaintiff's own words, and the reader will hear only one side, exactly as David did. He will form an opinion before the evidence is complete, and the opinion will be correct — and premature. The verse is designed to produce that error in the reader as well as in the judge, because a reader who has made the mistake is better placed to understand the repentance that follows it.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:22

> When they came into David’s presence, he was startled by them. They said, “Have no fear. ˹We are merely˺ two in a dispute: one of us has wronged the other. So judge between us with truth—do not go beyond ˹it˺—and guide us to the right way

**Expanded Commentary**

**Fear Before Words**
The verse records the king's reaction first and the visitors' speech second, and the order is deliberate. David is startled — the verb denotes the physical jolt of alarm — before anyone has said anything. The reader is given the fear without its explanation, and only then given the reassurance that answers it.

That sequence makes the fear available to the reader as evidence. A man in a sealed chamber, alone, confronted by two strangers who have arrived over a wall, reacts as anyone would. Nothing in the verse suggests his alarm was unreasonable or that it reflected a guilty conscience; it is the natural response to a breach of security, and the visitors' first word is an acknowledgement of it.

The recording of the fear also does something unusual for a prophetic portrait. Scripture generally presents its prophets in command of their situations; here one is shown off balance, in a private room, needing to be calmed by two men who have entered without leave. The portrait gains credibility from the exposure, and the chapter needs that credibility, since the man who is frightened here is the same man who will be forgiven a few verses later.

**The First Words of a Litigant, and Three Requests in One Sentence**
The reassurance comes before the case, which is how a person behaves when he knows his entrance has been wrong. The two men begin by removing the obstacle their own conduct created, and only then state who they are: two parties to a dispute, one of whom has wronged the other.

The self-description is remarkably economical. No names, no tribe, no history — a legal relationship stated in a clause. The Qur'an keeps the anonymity of the previous verse intact, and the anonymity is functional: a case about two unnamed men can be recognised by anyone, whereas a case about two named men belongs to its own century.

The litigant asks for three things, and each is distinct. Judge between us with truth. Do not go beyond it. Guide us to the right way. The first is a request for a decision; the second sets a limit on the decision; the third asks for something beyond the decision altogether.

The second request is the most unusual. A plaintiff rarely instructs a judge on the ceiling of his authority, and the request reads less like a courtesy than like a reminder — as though the speaker knows that a judge in this position is capable of exceeding what is due. Commentators connect it to the charge David will be given several verses later, to judge with truth and not follow desire, and the connection suggests that the phrase is placed here as a marker rather than as a pleasantry.

**Litigants Who Instruct the Judge**
The third request — guide us to the right way — goes beyond adjudication. A judge settles a claim; a guide directs a life. The two men are asking for both, and the combination tells the reader what kind of authority they recognise in David: not merely a magistrate but a prophet.

This matters for what happens next. A man who asks a judge for guidance has submitted to something larger than a verdict, and the verse quietly raises the stakes of the proceeding. The case about sheep is about to become a case about the judge, and the litigants have prepared the ground without appearing to.

There is a further consequence in the request not to exceed. Judges in the ancient world were expected to decide by their own judgement, and a party who asks for restraint is implying that restraint may be needed. Whether the implication is aimed at David or is a formula of pleading, its presence in the text draws attention to the possibility of excess, and the chapter will make use of that attention when it charges him to judge with truth and not follow inclination.

**Why the Fear Was There at All**
Commentators offer two explanations for David's alarm, and both rest on the sealed chamber. The first is practical: his miḥrāb was difficult of access and entry had been forbidden, so the appearance of strangers was a threat. The second is that he supposed they had come to harm him, since kings are the natural objects of assassination. Neither explanation requires anything unusual about the visitors beyond the fact that they were there.

A third reading, which some exegetes prefer, holds that the alarm itself is the beginning of the test — that David's first movement in the story is a suspicion of men who meant him no harm, and that his later request for forgiveness includes that suspicion. On this reading the verse is not merely scene-setting; it is the first item in an account that the story will settle at the end.

**A Courtroom Established in Three Clauses**
By the close of the verse the reader has a judge, two parties, an admission of wrong by one of them, and a set of instructions addressed to the bench. The scene is complete as a legal proceeding, and everything that follows is testimony.

The verse's economy is what makes the next one so effective. Nothing has been said about the substance of the claim, so the plaintiff's account will arrive without preparation, exactly as a judge hears it — one voice, one version, no cross-examination.

Notice too what the litigants concede at the outset. One of us has wronged the other. The wrong is admitted before the facts are given, which is unusual in a dispute and removes the usual contest about whether an injury occurred at all. What remains for the judge is not the fact but the remedy, and a case narrowed to remedy is a case that invites a quick decision. The speed of the verdict in the following verse is prepared here, in a sentence that sounds like an introduction.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:23

> This is my brother. He has ninety-nine sheep while I have ˹only˺ one. ˹Still˺ he asked me to give it up to him, overwhelming me with ˹his˺ argument.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ninety-Nine Against One**
The testimony is given in numbers, and the numbers do the arguing. Ninety-nine to one is not a marginal disparity; it is a monopoly beside a subsistence holding. The plaintiff does not allege violence, fraud or breach of contract. He alleges that a man who already owns almost everything asked for the last thing his neighbour has.

The disproportion is what makes the case morally obvious, and its obviousness is the trap. A judge hearing this account can hardly reach any conclusion but one, and the verse is constructed so that the reader reaches it too, quickly and without reserve. Everything that follows in the story depends on how fast the reader arrives at the verdict.

The numbers also carry an economic fact that a pastoral audience would have grasped at once. A single ewe is not a surplus asset; it is a household's milk, its wool and its next year's lamb. To take it is not to adjust a portfolio but to end a way of living. The plaintiff's grievance is therefore about survival, and the defendant's demand — whatever its legal form — is a demand that his brother cease to keep animals at all.

**Kinship as Aggravation, and the Plaintiff's Eloquence**
The first words of the testimony establish a relationship, and the relationship is doing legal work. The Arabic allows two readings, and both have been held: brothers by blood, or brothers in faith. Either way, the term imports an obligation. A stranger's demand is aggressive; a brother's demand is a betrayal.

The choice to open the testimony with kinship is a rhetorical act by the plaintiff, and it is the first sign that this man is a skilled speaker. He begins not with the fact in dispute but with the relationship that makes the fact intolerable. A judge listening carefully would note the technique; a judge listening to the substance would simply feel the wrong.

Read on the plain sense, the testimony is a study in advocacy. Every element is chosen: the kinship term first, the numbers second, the manner of the injury last. Nothing is false, and nothing is neutral. The speaker is presenting his case in the order that will produce the strongest reaction, and he does it well.

That skill is what makes the scene a test of a judge. A weak advocate produces a weak case, and a weak case invites caution. A brilliant advocate produces a case that feels self-evident, and self-evidence is precisely the condition in which a judge is most likely to rule before the other side has spoken. The verse supplies the eloquence; the next verse supplies the ruling.

**Overpowered in Speech, Not in Force**
The closing clause is the most carefully worded part of the testimony: he overwhelmed me *in speech*. The plaintiff does not claim he was robbed. He claims he was argued down — that the other man's case was better put, more fluent, more persuasive, and that fluency prevailed over justice.

Commentators explain the phrase as the stronger party's clearer and more eloquent argument. That explanation is important, because it identifies the injury as procedural rather than physical. A man who loses his property to a thief has been robbed; a man who loses it to a better speaker has been defeated in a forum where the rules favoured the articulate. The verse names a specific kind of injustice, and it is the kind a king is appointed to correct.

**The Case Is Not Only About Sheep**
A long tradition reads the ninety-nine ewes as a figure for something else, and the reading goes back to the earliest generations of exegetes. On that account the story corresponds to the account in 2 Samuel of David's desire for the wife of one of his officers, and the ninety-nine to one is taken as the many wives of a king beside the single wife of a soldier. The two visitors are then understood as angels sent with a case that is really about the judge.

The reading has real support and real problems, and both should be stated. Its support is structural: the plaintiff's numbers fit the pattern of a king's household against a commoner's, and David's immediate conviction of the defendant mirrors a self-condemnation. Its problem is that the Qur'an supplies none of the particulars the reading requires — no woman, no husband, no battlefield — and that it must be imported from a text outside the revelation. Ibn Kathīr observed that the connection is not grounded in sound reports, and al-Rāzī argued that even a purified version of the story conflicts with what the Qur'an says of David here and elsewhere. The stronger position is the one that stays inside the text: a case about property and eloquence, in which the test is judicial rather than moral in the narrower sense.

**One Voice in the Room**
The most striking feature of this verse is what it does not contain. There is no reply from the defendant. The man with ninety-nine sheep is present, named as a brother, described as overbearing in argument — and silent throughout. The reader never hears him.

That silence is the whole case. A judgement requires both parties, and the verse gives one. Whether the reader notices the absence before David does is the question the chapter is asking, and the answer determines how the following verses are read.

The silence is also the reason the story can be applied to anyone. Every reader has been the persuasive party and every reader has been the silent one, and the verse makes the reader occupy the plaintiff's position first. Persuasion feels like justice from the inside. That is the discovery the account is engineered to produce.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:24

> David ˹eventually˺ ruled, “He has definitely wronged you in demanding ˹to add˺ your sheep to his. And certainly many partners wrong each other, except those who believe and do good—but how few are they!” Then David realized that We had tested him so he asked for his Lord’s forgiveness, fell down in prostration, and turned ˹to Him in repentance˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Ruling Issued on One Testimony**
David decides, and the decision is sound on the facts presented. A man with ninety-nine who demands the hundredth from his brother has wronged him, and no defence is likely to alter that. The defect is not in the reasoning but in the procedure: the defendant has not spoken. The verse marks the ruling with a word meaning *eventually* or *he judged*, and the marking has been read as an indication that the judgement came after a process in David's own mind rather than after a hearing.

The absence of the second party is what turns a correct verdict into a failing. A judge who rules on the strength of a compelling plaintiff has done what the office forbids, however accurate the outcome. Al-Rāzī identifies this as the most probable reason for David's request for forgiveness, and the following verses support the reading, since the charge given to him concerns judging with truth and not following inclination.

**"Many Partners Wrong Each Other"**
The generalisation that follows the verdict widens the case beyond the two men in the room. Partners — those who share property, trade or an arrangement — routinely wrong one another, and the verse states it as a fact about the world rather than as a complaint. The word used for partners carries the sense of those who have mixed their holdings, and the mixing is what generates the dispute.

The generalisation is also the moment at which the ruling stops being a decision and becomes a sermon. A judge's sentence should end with the parties; this one continues into a reflection on human nature. That continuation is characteristic of a prophet-judge, and it is also the sign that David is speaking from something other than the record in front of him.

**"But How Few Are They"**
The exception is faith and right action, and the comment attached to it — how few — is one of the Qur'an's recurrent laments. The same observation appears in various forms across the Book: most people do not give thanks (2:243; 40:61), most people do not believe (11:17). The rarity is never offered as a surprise but as a settled finding.

The phrase does two things in this verse. It completes the moral picture by naming what restrains the tendency to exploit a partner, and it lowers the estimate of the human condition in a way that the reader is meant to apply to himself. The partners who do not wrong each other are not the strong or the clever; they are the ones who believe and act well, and there are few of them.

**The Realisation of the Test, and What the Story Refuses to Say**
The turning point of the verse is a single clause: David understood that We had tested him. Nothing external is described — no voice, no sign, no rebuke. The understanding arrives from within, and its arrival is the test's completion.

Commentators offer two accounts of how the realisation came. Those who take the visitors to be angels say that one of them glanced at the other and smiled, and then both rose into the sky, leaving David to see what the proceeding had been. On the plainer reading, no such signal is needed: a man who has just ruled without hearing the defence recognises, in the silence that follows his own sentence, what he has done. The second reading requires nothing beyond the text and is the stronger for it.

The Qur'an does not name David's fault. It says he was tested, that he understood it, and that he sought forgiveness; it never states the offence, and it never identifies the defendant or explains his silence. Every attempt to fill the gap has come from outside the text, and the attempts contradict one another.

The refusal is a choice about what the story is for. A narrative that named the sin would be about the sin; a narrative that withholds it is about the recovery. The chapter wants the second, and it gets it by leaving the first unstated. The reader is given a prophet's error in outline and his repentance in detail, and the proportions tell him which half of the account he is meant to carry away.

**Four Movements in a Single Recovery**
What follows the realisation is described in four verbs, and their sequence is the chapter's model of repentance. He asked his Lord's forgiveness. He fell down. He prostrated. He turned.

The order is instructive. The request comes before the bodily act, and the turning comes last, as though the physical prostration were the hinge between admission and return. The final verb is from the same root as the intensive noun applied to David in verse 17 — the one who turns repeatedly — and its appearance here confirms that the characterisation was not decoration. A man described as constantly returning is a man who knows how to return, and the verse shows the mechanism at work.

The mechanism is worth naming because the chapter will need it later. Its audience had spent years refusing the message, and the refusal had become habitual. The verse supplies a counter-model: a sequence of four movements that a person can perform at any point, regardless of how far the interval has run.

**The Prostration Is Part of the Verse's Inheritance**
The scene ends with a bodily act that the Islamic tradition has kept alive in its liturgy. This is one of the fifteen places in the Qur'an where a prostration is prescribed on recitation, and the practice is reported from the Prophet ﷺ himself, who prostrated here and distinguished the act from the obligatory prostrations of prayer. The verse therefore carries its own continuation: every reader who recites it repeats, in the body, the movement with which the story closes.

Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said that the prostration of Ṣād is not among the obligatory ones, but that he saw the Prophet ﷺ prostrating when reciting it (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1069). The report fixes the act's status: recommended rather than required, and established by practice rather than by command.

The status suits the verse. David's prostration was not an obligation discharged but a return performed, and the liturgical act preserves that character. A reader who prostrates here is not completing a requirement; he is joining a movement the verse describes.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:25

> So We forgave that for him. And he will indeed have ˹a status of˺ closeness to Us and an honourable destination

**Expanded Commentary**

**Forgiveness Stated in Five Words**
The verse disposes of the entire affair in a clause. What the previous verse described in four verbs — request, fall, prostration, return — is answered here with a single act of pardon, and no condition is attached to it. No penance is imposed, no restitution is ordered, no period of probation is mentioned. The forgiveness is complete on the spot.

The absence of a condition is theologically important. Repentance in the Qur'an is not a transaction in which the sinner supplies a payment and God supplies an acquittal; the sinner supplies a turning, and the pardon is given in response to the turning rather than purchased by it. The verse's brevity is therefore not a compression of a longer process but an accurate report of how the process works. A man who turned was forgiven, and that is the whole of it.

**Zulfā: Nearness as a Rank**
The promise that follows uses a noun denoting nearness — zulfā — and the Qur'an uses the same word elsewhere as a term of rank rather than of feeling. The foremost of the resurrected are those brought near: *“They are the ones nearest ˹to Allah˺”* (56:11); *“So, if the deceased is one of those brought near ˹to Us˺”* (56:88); and the record of the righteous is *“witnessed by those nearest ˹to Allah˺”* (83:21).

The Qur'an is equally clear about how that rank is reached. It is not bought with wealth or family: *“It is not your wealth or children that bring you closer to Us. But those who believe and do good—it is they who will have a multiplied reward for what they did, and they will be secure in ˹elevated˺ mansions.”* (34:37). And it cannot be obtained through intermediaries, which is the specific error the Book condemns: *“As for those who take other lords besides Him, ˹saying,˺ ‘We worship them only so they may bring us closer to Allah,’ surely Allah will judge between all regarding what they differed about.”* (39:3). Nearness is a position granted to obedience, and it is the position David is promised here.

**An Honourable Destination, and a Sentence the Chapter Reuses**
The second promise is a good place of return, and the phrase carries two directions at once. It can mean the outcome of a life — where a man ends up — or the place to which he comes back, since the same root governs both arriving and returning. Applied to David, it covers his standing in this world after the affair and his station in the next.

The pairing of the two promises is deliberate. Nearness describes a relationship; the destination describes a place. Together they answer the two things a fall threatens: a man's standing before God and his future. Neither is diminished by what happened, and the verse says so in the future tense, which makes the promise permanent rather than a restoration of a previous state.

The promise made here is not unique to David. The identical formula will be given to Solomon at the close of his own account, and its repetition across the two portraits is the chapter's summary of what its heroes have in common.

Both men were tested, both fell short in some respect, and both received the same two gifts at the end. The chapter is not a collection of exemplary lives so much as a single argument restated: the servants God praises are the ones who return. The next verses will show what David was told to do with the standing he has just been confirmed in.

**The Prostration This Verse Carries**
This is one of the places in the Qur'an where a prostration is prescribed upon recitation, and its status has been settled by a report from Ibn ʿAbbās preserved in the most authoritative collection: the prostration of Ṣād is not one of the compulsory prostrations, but he saw the Prophet ﷺ prostrating while reciting it (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1069). The act is therefore established by practice and left open by law, and it is classed among the prostrations of gratitude rather than the obligatory ones.

Ibn ʿAbbās is also reported to have explained the difference between the two prostrations in the story: David prostrated in repentance, and those who recite prostrate in thanks, because his repentance was accepted. The distinction is worth keeping. The verse is not a place of mourning in the liturgy; it is a place of thanksgiving for a pardon that was granted, and the bodily act is the reciter's way of joining the scene at its conclusion rather than at its crisis.

**Rank Restored, Not Rank Reduced**
The most important claim in this verse is what it does not say. Nothing in it suggests that David's fall cost him his standing. He is not demoted, not reproved in the text of the promise, not required to make amends to anyone. The two gifts announced here — nearness and a good return — are of the same order as the gifts he held before the trial, and the chapter will shortly give him a charge greater than the one he had: to judge among the people with truth as God's authority on earth (38:26).

That sequence answers a question the story might otherwise raise. If a prophet can err in judgement, does his office survive the error? The verse answers that it does, and the answer is not a concession to David's dignity but a statement about the nature of the office. Prophethood is not a reward for infallibility; it is an appointment that includes the possibility of correction, and the correction is part of the appointment.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:26

> ˹We instructed him:˺ “O David! We have surely made you an authority in the land, so judge between people with truth. And do not follow ˹your˺ desires or they will lead you astray from Allah’s Way. Surely those who go astray from Allah’s Way will suffer a severe punishment for neglecting the Day of Reckoning.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Charge After the Pardon, and an Appointment That Warns**
The charge is given in four movements, and only the first of them is a favour. David is appointed an authority on earth; he is told to judge with truth; he is told not to follow desire; and he is told what awaits those who stray. Three of the four movements are cautions, and they are addressed to a man who has just been forgiven and promised nearness.

The proportion tells the reader how the Qur'an understands office. Authority is not a reward that can be enjoyed; it is a trust that can be lost, and the one who holds it is more exposed than those he governs. The verse does not congratulate David on his appointment. It arms him against the specific failure that his appointment makes possible.

The sequence of the two verses is the most instructive thing about them. Forgiveness is granted, nearness is promised, and then the appointment is issued with its warnings. A reader expecting a narrative of restoration might expect the charge to come first and the pardon last.

The actual order makes a different point. David is not restored *to* his office as though the office had been suspended; he is confirmed *in* it and given a sharper understanding of what it requires. The trial has not disqualified him, and it has not left him unchanged. The man who ruled on one testimony is now told, in so many words, to judge between people with truth — and the instruction lands differently on a man who has just discovered what it costs to get that wrong.

**Khalīfah: What the Word Obliges**
The term translated *authority* is khalīfah, the same word used of the human being placed on earth at the beginning: *“˹Remember˺ when your Lord said to the angels, ‘I am going to place a successive ˹human˺ authority on earth.’”* (2:30). The word denotes a successor or a deputy — someone who acts in the place of another, and whose acts are measured against the standard of the one he represents.

The general form of the appointment is stated elsewhere with its condition attached: *“He is the One Who has placed you as successors on earth and elevated some of you in rank over others, so He may test you with what He has given you.”* (6:165). Rank is a test, not a prize. David's kingship is therefore a case of the general rule, and the verse applies the rule to him by name: you have been made an authority, so judge with truth.

**"Do Not Follow Desire": The Judge's Named Danger**
The prohibition is not general but professional. A judge's failure is rarely a collapse into open wickedness; it is usually a tilt — toward the eloquent party, the powerful one, the relative, or simply the reading of the case that feels most satisfying. The verse names the mechanism and the consequence in a single clause: desire will lead you astray from the way of God.

Ibn ʿArabī read the word for desire at its widest, as everything a person loves, and glossed the instruction as *follow not what you love but what I love*. On that reading the prohibition is not about appetite in the narrow sense but about the whole orientation of the will. The narrower reading is the more likely in a verse addressed to a judge, and the wider one is not inconsistent with it: the man who cannot govern his preferences will not govern a case.

**Forgetting the Day of Reckoning Is the Cause**
The final clause gives the reason for the severity of the punishment, and the reason is not the act of injustice but the forgetting that permitted it. Those who stray suffer severely *because* they neglected the Day of Reckoning. The Qur'an elsewhere describes the same forgetting in starker terms: *“And do not be like those who forgot Allah, so He made them forget themselves. It is they who are ˹truly˺ rebellious.”* (59:19).

The causal claim is precise and worth stating plainly. A judge who believes he will be called to account weighs his decision differently from one who does not, and the difference is not in his intelligence but in his horizon. Forgetting is therefore not a venial lapse behind the injustice; it is the condition that made the injustice possible. The verse treats the forgetting as the more serious matter, and it locates it in the heart of the man on the bench.

The location is what makes the warning transferable. A judge is not the only person with authority over another's case, and the forgetting is not confined to a courtroom. Anyone who decides a matter while out of sight of the Day of Reckoning is in the position the verse describes, and the chapter addresses him through the king.

**A Portrait That Ends on a Rule**
The David of this chapter is not primarily a singer, a king or a penitent. He is a judge, and the chapter gives him one detailed case, one error, one repentance and one standing instruction. Everything else in his portrait — the mountains, the birds, the strengthened kingdom, the decisive speech — is equipment for that function.

The instruction is addressed to a prophet and applies to anyone with authority over another person's case. The verse does not soften it for the difficulty of the task. It states the appointment, the requirement, the danger and the consequence, and leaves the man to hold all four at once.

Notice what the verse does not provide. No procedure is given, no list of evidences, no rule about witnesses. A judge is told to be truthful and warned against his own inclinations, and the technical apparatus is left to him. That distribution of concern is characteristic of the Qur'an's legislation: the inner condition is specified in detail because it cannot be supervised, while the outward method is left to circumstances because it can.

The chapter's interest in David's heart rather than in his court procedure is also what makes the story portable. Legal systems change; the temptation of a man who holds power over another's case does not.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:27

> We have not created the heavens and earth and everything in between without purpose—as the disbelievers think. So woe to the disbelievers because of the Fire

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Purpose Asserted Against a Conjecture**
The verse makes a claim about the whole of creation and then names the belief it is answering. The heavens, the earth and everything between them were not created in vain — the Arabic term is bāṭil, meaning empty, false, or without consequence. The denial is absolute, and it is directed at a specific alternative view, which the verse identifies as the opinion of those who disbelieve.

The same assertion appears repeatedly in the Qur'an, in almost identical wording: *“We did not create the heavens and the earth and everything in between for sport.”* (21:16; 44:38); *“We have not created the heavens and the earth and everything in between except for a purpose. And the Hour is certain to come, so forgive graciously.”* (15:85); *“We only created the heavens and the earth and everything in between for a purpose and an appointed term.”* (46:3). The repetition across Meccan and Medinan sūrahs shows that this is a foundational claim rather than a passing remark, and the addition of *an appointed term* in one of them identifies what the purpose consists in.

**"That Is the Conjecture of Those Who Disbelieve"**
The verse does not merely contradict the opposing view; it classifies it. The belief that creation is purposeless is described as a ẓann — a conjecture, an opinion held without knowledge. The word choice matters, because it denies the opposing position the status of a considered conclusion. It is not a rival theory; it is a guess that has been mistaken for a finding.

Al-Rūm and al-Jāthiyah describe the same condition in the same terms. The disbelievers say: *“There is nothing beyond our worldly life. We die; others are born. And nothing destroys us but ˹the passage of˺ time.” Yet they have no knowledge ˹in support of this ˹claim˺. They only speculate.* (45:24). The verdict in both verses is identical: the position rests on speculation, and its holders are unaware that they are speculating.

**Purposelessness and Injustice, and Woe Because of the Fire**
The verse's placement is not accidental. It follows immediately on the instruction to a judge, and the connection between them is the chapter's argument. A man who believes that creation has a purpose judges as someone accountable; a man who believes it has none judges as someone who will not be called to answer.

This is why the Qur'an treats the denial of the Hereafter as a practical doctrine rather than a metaphysical opinion. It has consequences in the courtroom, in the market and in the treatment of the weak.
