The charge to David was to judge with truth and not follow desire, and the verse that follows explains what makes that possible: the conviction that the case is not the last word. Remove the conviction and the charge becomes unenforceable.

The closing sentence is structured as a lament followed by its cause: woe to them, and the cause is the Fire. The construction is common in the Qur'an's warnings, and its effect is to present the punishment as something that already exists rather than something that will be produced. The Fire is not created in response to their disbelief; their disbelief is what delivers them to it.

The lament is worth noticing as well. The word carries sorrow as well as condemnation, and its use here — immediately after a verse about the purpose of creation — gives the warning a specific tone. These are not enemies being destroyed but creatures being lost, and the loss is described in the vocabulary of mourning rather than of victory.

**The Denial in Their Own Words**
The Qur'an quotes the position it is refuting more than once, and the quotations are consistent. *“There is nothing beyond our worldly life. We die, others are born, and none will be resurrected.”* (23:37). The formula has three parts: the world is all there is, life is a succession of generations, and there is no return. Each part follows from the one before it, and together they describe a closed system.

The chapter's answer does not engage the system on its own terms. It states the fact of purpose and identifies the alternative as conjecture, which is a different kind of move: it does not argue within the disbeliever's frame but refuses the frame. The reason is that the frame cannot be entered. A man who holds that nothing is real beyond what he can see cannot be shown a purpose, because purpose is not an object of sight.

**The Verse That Holds the Chapter Together**
Read in sequence, the passage from the trial of David to this verse forms a single argument. A judge is appointed and warned. He is warned because he will be called to account. He will be called to account because creation is not purposeless. Those who deny the purpose are the ones who will not be warned by anything.

The chapter does not state that chain explicitly. It lays the pieces down in order and lets the reader assemble them, which is how its argument usually proceeds.

There is also a claim about the world in the phrase *everything in between*. The heavens and the earth are the two extremes of the created order, and the space between them contains everything a human being can act upon — the air, the seas, the land, the living things. To say that none of it was made in vain is to say that no part of the human domain is exempt from meaning, including the parts that look accidental.

That claim is the ground of the moral seriousness the chapter has been insisting on since the trial of David. If a single ewe matters enough to bring two men before a king, and a king low enough to need forgiveness, then nothing in the space between heaven and earth is trivial. The verse states the principle; the story demonstrated it.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:28

> Or should We treat those who believe and do good like those who make mischief throughout the land? Or should We treat the righteous like the wicked

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Questions, the Second Narrowing the First**
The previous verse denied that creation is purposeless; this one demonstrates what purposelessness would mean in practice. Two rhetorical questions are posed, and neither can be answered affirmatively by anyone who retains a sense of justice. Should the believer who does good be treated like the man who spreads corruption? Should the righteous be treated like the wicked?

The questions are the moral argument for the Hereafter in its shortest form. If the two groups receive the same end, then the difference between them meant nothing, and a universe in which the difference meant nothing is a universe without purpose. The verse therefore does not prove the resurrection by cosmology; it proves it by the intuition that the reader already has about fairness.

Two questions are asked, and the second is not a repetition of the first. The first contrasts those who believe and do good with those who make mischief; the second contrasts the righteous with the wicked, using terms that describe character rather than conduct. The movement is from action to disposition.

The narrowing matters because it closes an escape. A man might argue that his acts of mischief were incidental to a life that was on balance decent, or that his corruption was the product of circumstance rather than character. The second question removes that defence by asking about the man himself rather than his deeds: shall the righteous be treated as the wicked? Two levels of accounting, and both must be answered.

**Corruption Is Not Simply the Opposite of Righteousness**
The verse pairs belief and good action against the making of mischief, and the pairing is looser than it looks. The ordinary Qur'anic opposite of ifsād, the working of corruption, is iṣlāḥ, the setting of things right, as in the command *“Do not spread corruption in the land after it has been set in order.”* (7:56). Here the opposition is drawn between the corrupter and the believer who acts well, which widens the contrast from a pair of acts to a pair of lives.

That widening is deliberate. The verse is not comparing two deeds but two careers. A life of belief and good action is set against a life of mischief, and the comparison is about totals rather than incidents. The Qur'an's interest in the accounting is always at this scale: what a person's years add up to, not what any single hour contained.

The word for corruption also carries a specific weight in the Qur'an, which treats it as a spreading thing rather than a private fault. Corruption has consequences beyond the corrupter: *“Corruption has spread on land and sea as a result of what people's hands have done, so that Allah may cause them to taste ˹the consequences of˺ some of their deeds and perhaps they might return ˹to the Right Path˺.”* (30:41). A mischief-maker is therefore not merely a sinner but a source, and the verse measures him by what he generated.

**Equality as the Real Injustice**
The verse's force lies in a single idea: to treat unequal things equally is itself an injustice. This is not a claim about reward but about truth. If the corrupter and the righteous receive the same end, then the universe has recorded a falsehood about them, and the falsehood is not repaired by any subsequent event.

The Qur'an states the same point elsewhere without the rhetorical form: *“Is the one who is a believer equal ˹before Allah˺ to the one who is rebellious? They are not equal!”* (32:18), and *“Should We then treat those who have submitted like the wicked?”* (68:35). The flat denial in al-Sajdah — *they are not equal* — is the answer that the questions here require, and the three verses together make clear that the inequality is a fact about reality rather than a decision God might have made otherwise.

**The Verse Answers the Complaint of the Opening**
Everything in these two verses speaks back to the first ten verses of the sūrah. The notables of Makkah objected that a prophet had been appointed over their heads and that their own standing had been ignored. The verse now puts the question of standing in its proper terms: what does a person's rank mean at the accounting?

Nothing, on this account, except what it was used for. The chiefs who felt themselves slighted by the choice of a messenger are told that the distribution they cared about was never the significant one. The significant distribution is between those who believed and did good and those who made mischief, and it will be carried out with an exactness that the worldly one lacked.

**Patience Rests on This Verse**
The instruction given earlier in the chapter — be patient with what they say (38:17) — is made bearable by the claim in this one. Endurance under injustice is possible when the injustice is temporary and the accounting is certain; it is impossible when both are uncertain. The verse supplies the second term.

That is why the passage sits where it does. The histories of the destroyed peoples established that denial has consequences in this world. This verse establishes that the consequences do not end there, and without that assurance the histories would be nothing more than a record of who happened to win.

The verse also answers a difficulty that every reader of the histories eventually meets. The destroyed communities were not always destroyed for cruelty; some were destroyed for mere refusal, and some of the cruel were not destroyed at all in their lifetimes. A moral order that operates only inside history cannot account for either case. This verse supplies the missing half of the ledger, and it does so as a question that the reader answers himself.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:29

> ˹This is˺ a blessed Book which We have revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ so that they may contemplate its verses, and people of reason may be mindful

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Book Revealed For a Stated Purpose**

The verse gives the reason for revelation in a purpose clause, and the reason is not obedience in the first instance but contemplation. The Book was sent down *so that they may contemplate its verses*, and a second group is named after it: so that people of reason may take heed. Neither clause mentions belief, law or ritual; the primary function of the revelation is described as an act of thought.

The claim is unusual enough to be worth stating plainly. A scripture whose stated purpose is contemplation cannot be treated as a manual to be applied without being understood. The verse makes thinking part of the obligation, and it makes the failure to think a failure toward the Book rather than merely a personal shortcoming.

**Blessed: What the Word Adds**

The Book is described as blessed, from barakah, a term denoting increase that is stable and productive rather than merely large. A blessed thing yields more than its size suggests and keeps yielding; the word is used in the Qur'an of water, of olive trees, of nights and of places.

Applied to a book, the term describes what the text does over time. A blessed book is one that continues to produce understanding in readers who did not exist when it was revealed, and the verse's own history is evidence for the claim: it has been read for fourteen centuries in languages its first audience did not speak, and it is still doing what this verse says it was sent to do.

The word also excludes a reading of blessing as prestige. A thing can be widely known and yield nothing. Barakah is measured by what comes out of a thing rather than by how far it travels, and the verse attaches it to a book whose purpose is stated in the same breath: that its verses be reflected upon.

**Contemplation Is Named as the Work**

The verb used for contemplating denotes a sustained, deliberate looking-at, and the Qur'an elsewhere demands it in the form of a rebuke: *“Do they not then reflect on the Quran? Or are there locks upon their hearts?”* (47:24). The alternative offered there is not ignorance but obstruction — a heart that has been locked, which is a condition the person has participated in producing.

That rebuke tells the reader what the failure to contemplate looks like from the outside. It is not a lack of ability; it is a refusal that has hardened. The verse here states the same requirement positively, and the two together define the reader's task: the verses are there to be thought about, and the person who does not think about them has not been unable to but unwilling.

The same idea is put in terms of the heart elsewhere: *“Surely in this is a reminder for whoever has a ˹mindful˺ heart and lends an attentive ear.”* (50:37). Two conditions are named there, and both are active — a heart that is present and an ear that is listening. Contemplation in the Qur'an is never a passive reception; it is an act performed on the text with attention, and the verse makes that act the purpose of the revelation itself.

**People of Reason: Those Who Hold the Kernel**

The phrase translated *people of reason* is ulū al-albāb, literally those who possess the lubb — the kernel or innermost part of a thing, as opposed to its shell. The expression is used throughout the Qur'an of those who see to the inside of matters, and it appears in contexts of observation, memory and reflection rather than of argument.

The word choice carries an implication about what reasoning means here. A lubb is what remains when the husk is removed; a person of lubb is one who has done the removing. The verse is therefore not addressing the clever but the attentive, and the distinction is important, since cleverness can be used to defend a husk indefinitely.

The chapter has already shown the husk being defended. The notables of Makkah argued from rank, wealth and tribal inheritance, which are shells rather than kernels, and they argued with some skill. The verse names the quality that would have let them see past the shells, and the naming is an invitation rather than a rebuke.

**Two Verbs, Two Stages**

The verse names two activities and it is worth keeping them distinct. Contemplating the verses is the act of working on the text: reading it, holding two of its statements together, asking what follows. Being mindful is the act of being changed by it: taking heed, adjusting a life.

The order is not accidental. Mindfulness without contemplation is sentiment, and contemplation without mindfulness is scholarship. The verse requires both, and it puts the work on the text first, which means that the transformation it promises is not available to anyone who skips the reading. This is the strongest statement in the chapter about what the revelation expects of its audience, and it comes at the point where the chapter is turning from argument to narrative.

**Why the Verse Sits Between the Argument and the Stories**

Structurally, this verse closes the first half of the sūrah and opens the second. The argument with the objectors is finished, the histories of the destroyed have been listed, the moral basis of judgement has been established — and now the reader is told what the Book itself is for.

The placement gives the portraits that follow their instruction manual. David, Solomon, Job and the prophets named after them are not presented as exempla to be admired but as material to be contemplated, which is the activity the verse has just defined. The stories are the verses, and the verses are to be worked on.

There is a further point in the verse's own self-reference. A book that says it was revealed so that its verses may be contemplated is making a claim about how it should be read, and the claim is checkable. A text that repays sustained attention will produce different results at different depths; a text that does not will flatten out on the second reading. The verse invites the test in the same breath as it defines the task, and the chapter's remaining fifty-nine verses are the material offered for it.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:30

> And We blessed David with Solomon—what an excellent servant ˹he was˺! Indeed, he ˹constantly˺ turned ˹to Allah˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Gift Phrased as the Bestowal of a Person**
The verse describes God's giving of Solomon to David, and the phrasing is unusual: the gift is not a possession but a son, and the verb is the same one used for conferring favours. David is blessed *with* Solomon, as one might be blessed with rain or with victory, and the construction treats the child as a benefit conferred on the father rather than as an heir produced by him.

The framing sets the tone for everything that follows in Solomon's portrait. His kingdom, his command of the wind, his authority over the jinn — all of it is presented in the same register as his birth: given. Nothing in the chapter allows Solomon to be read as a man who achieved his position, and the verse establishes that before he does anything at all.

**The Inheritance That Was Not Property**
David had many sons, and the Qur'an singles out the one who succeeded him: *“And David was succeeded by Solomon, who said, ‘O people! We have been taught the language of birds, and been given everything ˹we need˺. This is indeed a great privilege.’”* (27:16). The inheritance is described elsewhere with a word that in ordinary usage means property, and commentators are careful about what it denotes here: what Solomon inherited was prophethood and sovereignty, not estates.

The distinction matters for the chapter's argument. The notables of Makkah measured standing by wealth and lineage, and the verse answers with a succession in which the transmitted goods were an office and a revelation. Solomon's own summary of what he received is a list of capacities — the language of birds, sufficiency — and his reaction is gratitude rather than pride.

Both father and son are described in the Qur'an as having been given knowledge and judgement, and their acknowledgement is recorded in the same breath as the gift: *“Indeed, We granted knowledge to David and Solomon. And they said ˹in acknowledgment˺, ‘All praise is for Allah Who has privileged us over many of His faithful servants.’”* (27:15). The verse is careful to place the praise immediately after the privilege, and the placement is a lesson about how a servant speaks about what he has been given.

**"What an Excellent Servant"**
The exclamation is the highest commendation the chapter gives to any man, and it is given before any of Solomon's deeds are narrated. The word used for servant, ʿabd, is the same term the Qur'an applies to the Prophet in the account of the night journey, and it denotes a person defined by belonging to God rather than by any achievement of his own.

The commendation is therefore not a rating of performance. It identifies a relation: this man was a servant, and being a servant is what makes him excellent. The same exclamation will be used of Job at the end of his account (38:44), which shows that it is not a reward for success — Job's story is one of loss — but a recognition of the same quality in two very different lives.

**Awwāb Again, and Why Solomon Follows David**
The verse closes on the intensive noun for one who turns repeatedly, the word used of David in verse 17 and of Job in verse 44. Its third appearance in the chapter is now clearly a signature rather than a description, and it tells the reader what the three portraits have in common.

The repetition also prepares the account that follows. Solomon will be tested, will lose something he held, and will return; the verse announces the returning before it narrates the loss. A reader who has met the word twice already knows that whatever happens to this man, the movement of his life is toward God and back again.

The chapter could have taken its prophets in any order, and the order it chooses is instructive. David's account ends with a charge about judging and a warning about desire; Solomon's begins with a horse review, a distraction, and a prayer for a kingdom that cannot be taken from him.

The sequence sets the son against the father deliberately. Both are kings, both are prophets, both are tested, and both return. The differences are equally important: David's trial came through a case brought to him, Solomon's through a possession that held his attention. The chapter is showing that the danger to a servant of God takes more than one form, and that the recovery is the same in each case.

**A Portrait Introduced by Its Ending**
Everything in this verse is a conclusion stated at the beginning. Solomon is called an excellent servant and a man who turns before he has done anything in the narrative, and the narrative that follows will show what those two titles cost him.

The method is the same one the chapter used with David, whose strength and returning were named before his trial was described. By establishing the character first, the Qur'an ensures that the failures it goes on to record are read as the failures of a servant rather than as evidence against the office. The man is fixed before the test begins.

That ordering has a pastoral purpose as well. A reader who meets a prophet already described as excellent will read his stumbling as news about himself rather than as a scandal about the prophet. The chapter wants the first reaction, because its subject throughout is not the biography of great men but the operation of returning. The commendation is placed early so that the account can be honest later.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:31

> ˹Remember˺ when the well-trained, swift horses were paraded before him in the evening

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Review Held at the Wrong Hour**
The verse opens on a scene of display: horses brought out and passed before a seated king in the last light of the day. Nothing in the sentence is criticised, and nothing in it is praised. The reader is given a time, a spectacle and an owner, and is left to notice what the combination implies.

The time is the point. A horse review is a military exercise, and military exercises are conducted in daylight for practical reasons; a review held in the evening is a review that has continued past the hour at which it should have ended. The Qur'an does not say that anything was neglected, and the following verse supplies the information the reader needs. What this verse does is set the clock, and setting the clock is enough to make the scene legible as a problem.

**Two Adjectives Doing the Work**
The horses are described with two qualities, and each carries meaning. The first denotes a stance — animals drawn up and standing with the poise of trained stock, the posture that in Arabian usage describes a horse resting on three legs with the toe of the fourth touching the ground. The second denotes breeding and speed: fine, noble, swift animals.

The pairing tells the reader what kind of property this is. These are not working animals or livestock for trade; they are a king's cavalry, bred, trained and presented. Such animals represent wealth, military capacity and status simultaneously, and in a pastoral society they are the single most visible form of power a ruler can display. The Qur'an elsewhere lists fine horses among the things made attractive to human beings: *“The enjoyment of ˹worldly˺ desires—women, children, treasures of gold and silver, fine horses, cattle, and fertile land—has been made appealing to people.”* (3:14). The verse places Solomon's horses inside that list without comment.

**Why Horses in Particular**
The choice of subject is not arbitrary. Horses in the Qur'an are associated with war and with beauty at the same time, and the sūrah that opens with them makes the double character explicit: *“By the galloping, panting horses, striking sparks of fire ˹with their hoofs˺”* (100:1–2). They are also created for a purpose that includes display: *“˹He also created˺ horses, mules, and donkeys for your transportation and adornment.”* (16:8).

A possession that is both useful and beautiful is the most dangerous kind for a servant of God, because it can be loved for two reasons at once and defended on either. Solomon's horses are exactly such a possession: they serve the state and they please the eye. The verse presents them without qualification, which is how the chapter treats every test — the object of the test is never a bad thing in itself.

**The Evening and What It Cost**
The commentators' standard reading of the scene rests on the time of day. The review is said to have occupied Solomon until the sun had set and the time for the prescribed prayer had passed, which is why the following verse speaks of loving good things *over* the remembrance of his Lord. On that reading the fault is not the horses but the hour they consumed.

The reading has the merit of explaining everything that follows: the confession, the recall, and the violent or tender act described in the next verse. It also explains why the chapter places this account immediately after David's trial. Both are failures of attention in men of great rank, and both are followed by an immediate return. Neither man is accused of a grave sin; each is shown losing track of something more important than the thing in front of him.

The evening also carries a weight of its own in the Qur'an's devotional language. The two ends of the day are the appointed times of remembrance, and David's praise in this same chapter is placed at the evening and after sunrise (38:18). To be absorbed in a display at the evening is therefore to be occupied at precisely the hour the chapter has already identified as belonging to God.

**A Possession Displayed, and What the Verse Refuses to Say**
There is a detail in the scene worth pausing on: the horses are brought *to him*. Solomon is seated and the animals pass before him, which makes the scene a display for one viewer rather than a manoeuvre for an army. A king inspecting his cavalry is performing a public function; a king watching his horses is enjoying a private pleasure.

The distinction matters for what the chapter is doing. It is not criticising military preparedness, which the Qur'an commands elsewhere. It is describing the moment at which a duty becomes an attachment — when the inspection stops being about the army and starts being about the animals. The verse gives no interior account of that shift; it gives only the seating arrangement and the hour, and lets the reader recognise the shift himself.

Three things are conspicuously absent. The verse does not state that Solomon sinned. It does not say how long the review lasted. It does not mention prayer at all. Everything that would allow a reader to reach a verdict is withheld, and the withholding is the method of the chapter's portraits throughout.

The effect is to place the reader in Solomon's position rather than in a judge's. A man watching beautiful animals at the end of a long day does not experience himself as failing; he experiences himself as enjoying something good. The verse reproduces that experience exactly, and the next verse supplies the sentence in which the man notices what he has done.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:32

> He then proclaimed, “I am truly in love with ˹these˺ fine things out of remembrance for Allah,” until they went out of sight

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Confession Spoken Aloud, and the Sentence That Explains What Follows**
The verse gives Solomon's own words, and they are the words of a man taking stock. He states that he has loved the love of good things, and the phrasing is a confession in the strict sense: an admission addressed to no one in particular, made at the moment of recognition. There is no accusation in the scene, no visitor, no rebuke. The man looks at what has been occupying him and names it.

The first-person form is significant. The Qur'an does not narrate Solomon's fault from outside; it lets him announce it. That choice preserves the man's dignity while recording his failure, and it establishes the pattern the chapter uses for every one of its servants: the fault is acknowledged by the servant before it is dealt with by God.

Whatever the preposition means, the verse's function in the narrative is fixed: it is the moment of recognition, and everything that follows depends on it. The recall of the horses, the act performed on their legs and necks, and the prayer for forgiveness in the verse after next all presuppose that Solomon has identified a fault in himself.

That sequence is the chapter's model of the spiritual life, and it is worth stating plainly. Nothing external intervenes. No angel appears, no sign is given, no rebuke is delivered. A man notices, names what he has noticed, and acts. The trial that comes two verses later will be imposed by God, but this one is self-detected, and the chapter treats the self-detection as the more impressive of the two.

**"The Love of Good Things"**
The expression translated *these fine things* is a single Arabic word, al-khayr, which means the good or the wealth, and in the usage of the Qur'an and of the Arabs it commonly denotes property — horses, cattle, gold. The word appears in the same sense elsewhere: *“and they are truly extreme in their love of ˹worldly˺ gains”* (100:8), where the context is the horses of the sūrah's opening oath.

The naming is exact and unsentimental. Solomon does not say he loved the horses; he says he loved the love of good things, which is a statement about his own attachment rather than about the animals. The object of the sentence is his affection, not his property, and the formulation shows a man who has correctly identified where the problem lies. Nothing about the horses was wrong; his love of them had taken a place it should not have held.

**Two Prepositions, Two Verdicts**
The clause translated *out of remembrance for Allah* has been read in two directions, and the difference between them is the whole interpretive question of this passage. The standard reading takes the preposition as one of preference: I loved the love of good things *more than* the remembrance of my Lord. On that reading the sentence is an admission of having allowed a good thing to displace a better one.

Al-Rāzī proposed the alternative: I loved the love of good things *on account of* the remembrance of my Lord — that is, his love of anything in this world exists within his devotion and is governed by it. The reading has a motive, which is the defence of prophetic impeccability, and it produces an internally consistent account that requires no unreliable reports. Its weakness is that it makes the following verse inexplicable: a man who has just declared his love properly ordered would have no reason to recall the horses and act upon them. The preference reading remains the stronger, because it is the only one that explains what happens next.

**"Until They Went Out of Sight"**
The final clause is ambiguous in the Arabic, and the ambiguity is productive. The pronoun can refer to the horses, giving the sense *until they passed out of view*, or it can be read with the sun as the unstated subject, giving *until it was veiled* — the ordinary expression for sunset. The first reading describes a spectacle continuing until the animals disappeared; the second describes a man sitting until the day ended.

Both readings have been held, and they are not mutually exclusive. If the sun is meant, the clause supplies the cost of the display: the prayer time passed. If the horses are meant, the clause describes the depth of the absorption: he watched them until there was nothing left to watch. The commentators who prefer the second reading do so because it explains the urgency of the command in the next verse, and their reasoning is sound.

**The Real Subject of the Verse Is Attention**
Stripped of its particulars, the verse is about the economics of a heart. There is a finite amount of attention in a person, and every object that occupies some of it occupies it at the expense of something else. Solomon's horses were legitimate, useful and beautiful, and they still cost him something, because attention spent on them was attention not spent elsewhere.

The chapter is not moralising about wealth. It is describing a mechanism, and it locates the mechanism in one of the most privileged men who ever lived. If a prophet-king with a kingdom, a revelation and a command over creatures could be displaced from remembrance by the sight of fine animals, then the mechanism operates independently of rank, wealth and piety. The verse records its operation once, in a single sentence, and moves on.

The honesty of the record is what makes it usable. A text that concealed the distraction of its hero would be offering a standard nobody could meet and a portrait nobody could recognise himself in. This verse says the thing plainly, in the hero's own voice, and leaves the reader to do what Solomon did.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:33

> ˹He ordered,˺ “Bring them back to me!” Then he began to rub down their legs and necks

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command With Two Possible Objects**
The order is short and its object is a pronoun, which is why the verse has generated so much discussion. The pronoun can refer to the horses, and the command then means *bring the animals back to me*. It can also be read with the sun as its object, since the same pronoun can mean *it*, and the command then means *bring it back to me* — restore the day so that the prayer missed can be prayed in its time.

Both readings have defenders and both have consequences. On the first, Solomon recalls the animals in order to deal with the thing that distracted him. On the second, he asks for something no human being can obtain, and the request becomes an expression of grief rather than a practical instruction. The tradition reports that the sun was indeed returned for him, and the report is the reason the second reading persists. Neither reading can be established from the text alone, and the honest position is to say so.

**Masaḥa: Between Stroking and Striking**
The verb describing what Solomon did to the horses' legs and necks is masaḥa, which means to pass the hand over something, to wipe or to rub. In ordinary usage it is a gesture of tenderness or of grooming. It is also used idiomatically with the sword, in the expression *he stroked them with the sword*, meaning he struck them.

The lexical range is therefore wide enough to support opposite conclusions, and the choice between them determines the entire moral reading of the passage. A man caressing his horses is a man making peace with a pleasure he has ordered correctly. A man cutting them down is a man destroying the occasion of his own distraction. The verse gives the verb and the body parts — legs and necks — and nothing more.

The body parts themselves are a small clue that cuts both ways. Legs and necks are where a groom works and where a sword falls, and the pairing is exactly as ambiguous as the verb. A reader determined to find a slaughter will find it here; a reader determined to find a caress will find that too.

**The Sacrifice Reading and Its Difficulty**
The majority of the classical commentators prefer the sword reading, and their account is coherent: Solomon slaughtered the horses as an atonement for having been distracted from the remembrance of God, and gave the meat to the poor. The reading explains the recall, explains the choice of legs and necks as the places a sword falls, and provides a moral for the story.

Its difficulty is equally clear. The animals did nothing wrong, and the Qur'an is emphatic elsewhere about the seriousness of killing a living thing without cause. To slaughter a hundred horses because one's own attention wandered is a disproportionate act, and it sits uneasily beside a prophet's character. Some exegetes noted this and preferred the gentler reading for that reason alone, arguing that it would be unjust for Solomon to kill the horses for his own slip.

**The Branding Reading and the Hand Reading**
On the alternative reading, Solomon called the horses back and passed his hand over their legs and necks — an act of care, or of inspection, or of both. Those who hold this view note that a cavalry commander's hand on an animal's legs is a professional gesture, checking soundness, and on its neck is a gesture of affection.

The reading has a further advantage that its defenders did not always state: it makes the sequence coherent without requiring a miracle or a slaughter. Solomon notices his distraction, recalls the animals, examines them, and turns the pleasure back into the duty it was meant to serve. The horses return from being an object of absorption to being an instrument of the state, which is the correction the previous verse called for.

A third interpretation is recorded and rarely followed: that Solomon branded the legs and necks of the horses and dedicated them to the service of God. On this account the act is neither destruction nor caress but consecration — the animals are marked as belonging to a purpose higher than their owner's enjoyment.

The reading deserves mention because it identifies the actual issue in the passage, which is ownership. The problem was never the horses; it was that they were held for pleasure rather than for use. A branding is a statement about who holds what and for what end, and it answers the fault described in the previous verse without requiring the reader to accept either a miracle or a massacre.

**What the Text Commits To**
Four readings, all defensible on the language, and the text chooses none of them. That is worth stating as a finding rather than as a difficulty. The verse commits to three things and no more: the horses were recalled, something was done to their legs and necks, and the act followed immediately on a confession.

The indeterminacy is not a defect in the text but a feature of its method. The chapter is interested in the movement of the servant — recognition, recall, response — and not in the mechanics of the response. Readers who insist on one reading usually do so because they have already decided what the story must mean, and the verse declines to make that decision for them.

There is a practical consequence for the reader as well. Because the act is unspecified, every reader must supply his own equivalent, and the equivalent will differ from person to person. One man's recall is a resignation; another's is a sale, an apology or a change of hours. The verse gives the shape of the correction and leaves its content to the life it is applied to, which is why it has remained useful for fourteen centuries.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:34

> And indeed, We tested Solomon, placing a ˹deformed˺ body on his throne, then he turned ˹to Allah in repentance˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Trial Named but Not Described**
The verse states three facts: Solomon was tested, a body was placed on his throne, and he turned. It does not say what the test consisted in, who placed the body, whose body it was, or how long the situation lasted. Every one of those questions has been answered at length by later writers, and none of the answers is in the text.

That silence is not an oversight. The chapter's other portraits work the same way: David's fault is never named, Solomon's distraction is described in a single sentence, and Job's suffering is summarised in a phrase. The Qur'an's method is to record the test and the response, and to leave the circumstances to the reader's imagination. What is withheld cannot be argued about, and what remains is the part that matters.

**A Body on a Throne, and Why the Elaborate Readings Fail**
The image is deliberately strange. A throne is the symbol of a king's authority, and a body placed upon it is either an impostor sitting in the king's place or a corpse occupying the seat of the living. The word used for the body denotes a lifeless form rather than a person, which rules out the ordinary sense of a rival claimant and pushes the image toward the symbolic.

Read as an image rather than as an event, it says something precise: Solomon's authority was, for a time, not in his own hands. The thing that sat on his throne was not him. Whether the loss was literal or figurative, the verse is describing a man separated from his own power, and the separation is the test.

Beyond their mutual contradiction, the ring stories have a structural problem: they import a plot from outside the revelation and then treat it as the key to the verse. The method is the same one criticised earlier in this chapter, where the case of the ninety-nine ewes was read as a cipher for a Biblical narrative. In both instances the Qur'anic text supplies none of the particulars the reading requires.

The more economical reading needs nothing beyond the verse. Solomon was tested; his authority was, in some manner, taken from him or made unavailable; he recognised the situation as a test and turned. Whether the mechanism was political, physical or supernatural is not stated, and the chapter does not need it to be. The test is the point; the machinery is not.

**The Stories of the Ring**
The commentaries are full of explanations, and they share a common structure. Solomon's authority was attached to a ring; an evil jinn obtained the ring by deception; the jinn sat on the throne and ruled in Solomon's place for a period; the body was that of the jinn, or of Solomon's son, or of Solomon himself reduced to a corpse-like state by illness. Each version supplies names, motives and a mechanism.

The versions contradict one another in ways that cannot be reconciled. If the body was the jinn, it cannot also have been Solomon's son; if Solomon was ill, he was not displaced. Al-Rāzī observed that these accounts are fanciful and rest on unsubstantiated sources, and his observation remains the most useful thing said about the passage. The stories are interesting as a record of what readers have wanted the verse to mean, and they are worthless as evidence of what it does mean.

**"Then He Turned"**
The final clause is the shortest in the verse and the only one the chapter is interested in. The verb is from the same root as the intensive noun used of Solomon in verse 30 — the one who turns repeatedly — and its appearance here confirms what the earlier verse announced. The man described as constantly returning is shown returning.

The sequence is also informative. The turning comes after the trial and not before it. Nothing in the verse suggests that Solomon's turning caused the trial or shortened it; the trial happened, and the response followed. That order protects the account from becoming a formula in which repentance is a technique for escaping difficulty. The turning is a response to being tested, not a means of avoiding the test.

The word also allows a second sense, since the same verb can mean simply to return. Some commentators read it as Solomon's return to his throne or his recovery of health after the trial, and the two senses are not in conflict. A man who turns to God returns to his place, and the chapter is content to let the spiritual and the practical meanings stand together.

**The Trial as a Lesson About Desire**
Read together with the three verses before it, this one forms a single movement, and the connection between them is the reason both are here. The first trial was self-detected: a man noticed his own attachment and corrected it. The second was imposed: a man lost what he held and discovered that he had never owned it.

The two together describe the education of a servant who rules. Attachment to what one holds is the danger named in the horse review; the loss of what one holds is the lesson supplied by the body on the throne. The chapter does not connect them explicitly, but it places them in sequence and gives them the same ending, and the ending is the point: in both cases the servant turns.

That ending is also what separates this account from the histories earlier in the chapter. The destroyed peoples were tested by their prophets and failed; Solomon is tested by God and returns. The difference is not in the severity of the test but in the direction of the response, and the chapter measures its people by nothing else.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:35

> He prayed, “My Lord! Forgive me, and grant me an authority that will never be matched by anyone after me. You are indeed the Giver ˹of all bounties˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Forgiveness First, and a Kingdom After a Theft**
The prayer has two requests, and their order is the most important thing about it. Forgiveness comes first, and the kingdom comes second. A man who has just lost his throne asks to be forgiven before he asks to be restored, and the order tells the reader how Solomon understands what has happened to him.

The sequence is the same one that closed David's account: request for forgiveness, then the gift. In both cases the pardon is not a reward for the asking and not a condition of the gift; it is the first thing a servant reaches for when he has been tested. The chapter establishes the pattern twice, and the repetition makes it a rule rather than a coincidence.

The connection between the trial and the request is the key to the verse. Solomon has learned, in the most direct way available, that a kingdom held without divine support can be taken by a creature. His prayer is therefore not an escalation of ambition but a correction of it: he asks for a rule whose security does not depend on his own grip.

That correction is what makes the prayer acceptable. The same words spoken by a man who had never been tested would have been the words of a tyrant. Spoken by a man who has just watched a body sit on his throne, they are the words of a servant who has understood the terms of his office.

**"Not Matched by Anyone After Me"**
The second request is for a sovereignty that will not befit anyone after him, and the phrase has been read in two ways. The ordinary reading is temporal: a kingdom such as no later ruler will equal. The alternative takes the preposition as meaning *besides* or *other than*, giving the sense of a kingdom that will not be given to anyone in addition to him.

The difference matters. On the first reading Solomon is asking for a supremacy in history; on the second he is asking for an exclusivity in the present. The first is the more natural sense of the Arabic and the one the following verses satisfy, since the gifts enumerated after this prayer — the wind, the jinn, the divers — have no parallel in the accounts of any later king. The chapter answers the request in the terms in which it was made.

**Why the Request Is Not Greed**
A man asking for unmatched power immediately after a test is, on the surface, a strange figure, and the strangeness has produced a spiritualising reading: some commentators hold that Solomon sought dominion over his own soul rather than over a territory. The reading is attractive, but the following verses refuse it, since what is granted is unmistakably external — wind, builders, divers, chains.

The better answer is that the request is not about enjoyment at all. A kingdom in the Qur'an is a trust, and the man asking for it has just experienced what it is to lose one. He knows what the office costs and how little it belongs to him. His request is for a rule that cannot be taken from him by deception, which is a request about the security of a trust rather than about the pleasures of power. The verse's own logic supports this: the trial was a theft, and the prayer is for a kingdom that cannot be stolen.

**The Name That Makes the Request Possible**
The prayer closes on a divine name, and the choice of name is the argument. Al-Wahhāb, the Giver of all bounties, denotes one who gives repeatedly and without a return being expected. Solomon does not appeal to God's justice, his covenant or his promise; he appeals to his generosity, and he does so at the moment of asking for something unprecedented.

The appeal is well formed, because the request is not something that can be earned. No amount of service entitles a man to a kingdom unmatched in history, and Solomon does not claim that it does. He names the attribute under which such a gift could be given and asks under it. The same name appeared earlier in the chapter, attached to the question about the treasuries of mercy (38:9), and the echo is deliberate: the treasury belongs to the Giver, and the Giver gives to whom He will.

**The Request Was Answered, and in Full**
The three verses that follow are the reply, and they answer the prayer literally rather than symbolically. The wind is subjected to him. The jinn are given to him as builders and divers. Others are bound in chains. Nothing comparable is recorded of any later king, and the Qur'an's own account of Solomon's successors contains no such gifts.

The answer is given without commentary, and the absence of commentary is instructive. A lesser text would explain why the request was granted or attach conditions to it. The verse simply records the prayer and then records the gifts, leaving the reader to see that a servant who asks for forgiveness first is a servant whose second request can be trusted.

The prayer also stands as a model for the form a request should take. It opens with the need that must be met before any other, states the want without disguise, and closes on the attribute under which the want can be granted. Nothing is hedged, nothing is pretended, and nothing is demanded as a right. The structure is available to any reader who has something to ask for.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:36

> So We subjected to him the wind, blowing gently at his command to wherever he pleased

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer Arrives as a Gift, Not a Verdict**

The verse begins with the conjunction of consequence, and what follows is the reply to the prayer of the previous verse. There is no preamble, no condition and no comment on the request. Solomon asked for a kingdom unmatched after him, and the first instalment is the wind.

The absence of comment is significant. The chapter does not evaluate the request, does not qualify the grant and does not warn the recipient. It records the asking and the giving, and leaves the reader to observe the shape of the exchange.

The shape is the chapter's model for prayer. A person turns, asks, and receives — and the receiving is described in the same plain register as the turning. Nothing in the verse suggests that the gift was earned by the turning; the two are simply placed beside each other.

The manner of the answer matters as much as its content. A request made after a trial could have been refused, or granted with a rebuke, or granted partially as a lesson. The verse does none of those things. The forgiveness asked for first is taken as settled, and the second request is answered in full and immediately. The chapter thereby establishes a principle about prayer: the order of the requests determines the reception of them.

**The Wind Subjected, Not Tamed**

The verb is the same one used of the mountains in David's case (38:18) — a making subservient, a placing under command. The wind is not persuaded, negotiated with or harnessed by technology. It is placed under a man's direction by an act of God, and its obedience is a consequence of that act rather than a property of the wind.

The distinction is worth holding onto, because it separates the Qur'anic account from any reading that would make Solomon a kind of engineer. Nothing in the verse describes a device, a sail or a method. The subjection is a gift of authority over a created force, and the authority belongs to the giver, who has lent it. The same verb governs the jinn in the next verse, which confirms that this is a single category of favour rather than two different kinds of achievement.

**"Blowing Gently": The Quality of the Gift**

The adjective applied to the wind is one of softness and ease, and it stands in deliberate contrast to the description given elsewhere: *“And to Solomon We subjected the raging winds, blowing by his command to the land We had showered with blessings. It is We Who know everything.”* (21:81). The two verses describe the same gift from two angles — a force that is violent in itself and gentle in its service.

The combination is the point. A power that could destroy is placed under a servant's command and made to carry rather than to break. Saba supplies the measure of the result: *“its morning stride was a month's journey and so was its evening stride”* (34:12). The gentleness is not weakness; it is a force held in reserve, and the holding is what the subjection consists in.

**Better Than the Horses**

The commentators connect this gift to the horse review, and the connection is a natural one. Solomon was absorbed by animals that carried men at speed, and he is given a force that carries his whole retinue farther and faster without feeding, stabling or resting. The exchange is recorded without comment, and the reader is left to make the comparison.

On that reading the verse is not merely a reward but a correction. The thing that displaced remembrance is replaced by something that serves the same end without the same danger, and the replacement is given rather than earned. The chapter's economy of grace is precise: a servant who gives up an attachment for God's sake is not left with a hole where the attachment was.

**Command Without Coercion**

The phrase *at his command* describes a relationship that has no human analogue. A king commands soldiers, who can disobey; he commands subjects, who can flee. The wind can do neither, and its obedience is total and immediate.

The immediacy is what distinguishes the gift from ordinary power. A ruler who must persuade, negotiate or enforce holds something contingent; Solomon held something that responded. The Qur'an uses the same construction for the subjection of the jinn and the birds in the verses that follow, and in each case the response is instant.

That instant response is also the measure of the gift's size. The wind is not merely available to him; it is available without delay, without condition and without residue of resistance. Nothing in the verse suggests a limit on the frequency or the extent of the command.

That totality is what makes the gift a sign rather than a convenience. A man whose word is obeyed by a force of nature is exercising a borrowed authority, and every exercise of it is a reminder that the authority is borrowed. The verse notes that the direction of travel was wherever he pleased, which places the choice entirely in his hands — and the choice is exactly where a servant's danger lies.

**Sovereignty Over the Air**

Read with the two verses that follow, this one gives Solomon command of three domains. The wind is the air; the builders are the land; the divers are the sea. No other figure in the Qur'an is given authority over all three, and the enumeration is clearly intentional.

The triple sovereignty answers the prayer in the terms in which it was made. A kingdom unmatched after him is not merely larger than other kingdoms; it is of a different kind, extending into realms no human ruler has ever governed. The chapter is careful to present the gifts one at a time and in this order, beginning with the least substantial of the three — a moving air — and ending with beings bound in chains.

There is also a theological point in the order. The wind is the gift furthest from human technology; no one can breed it, buy it or build it. By placing it first the verse establishes that the whole series is of one kind — conferred, not acquired — and prevents the reader from sliding into the assumption that the later gifts were earned by the wise use of the earlier ones.

**A Gift Measured Against Its Recipient**

The final observation about this verse concerns the man receiving it. Ten verses earlier Solomon was absorbed by the sight of horses until the day closed, and two verses before that he watched a body sitting on his throne. The same man is now given the wind to carry him wherever he pleases.

The sequence is the chapter's argument in miniature. A turning is followed by a return, the return by a request, and the request by a gift that exceeds anything the earlier possessions represented. The horses were a distraction; the wind is a means of travelling toward what he was distracted from.

The measurement is therefore not between two amounts of wealth but between two conditions of the heart. The chapter has been insisting on that distinction since the account of David, and the verse supplies its clearest illustration.

The chapter does not resolve the tension between the man's fragility and the man's power, and the refusal is instructive. Great capacity is not given to people who have proven they cannot be distracted; it is given to people who have shown what they do when they are. The verse records the gift without a word of caution, because the caution has already been delivered and received.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:37

> And ˹We subjected to him˺ every builder and diver of the jinn

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Domains, and Every Builder and Diver in Them**
The verse names two occupations and attaches a word meaning *every* to both, and the comprehensiveness is the claim. Solomon is not given some labourers from among the jinn; he is given the class — every one of them capable of building and every one capable of diving. The formulation leaves no residue, and it is the strongest statement of the gift in the chapter.

The two occupations are chosen for their opposition as much as for their utility. Building is work performed upward and outward on dry land, requiring strength and precision. Diving is work performed downward into water, requiring endurance and courage. Between them they cover the whole range of heavy labour available in the ancient world, and the verse is saying that all of it was at this man's disposal.

Read with the verse before and the verse after, this one completes a picture of total sovereignty. The wind carries him, the builders raise his monuments, the divers bring up the sea's wealth, and a further group is held in chains. Saba describes the arrangement in organisational terms: *“Solomon's forces of jinn, humans, and birds were rallied for him, perfectly organized.”* (27:17).

The chapter's purpose in the enumeration is not wonder but instruction. A kingdom of this extent was given to a man who, ten verses earlier, had been distracted by the sight of horses and had lost his throne to a body placed upon it. The disproportion between the man's weakness and the man's power is the lesson, and the verse supplies the second term of it without comment.

**Who the Shayāṭīn Are**
The beings named here are the shayāṭīn, a term the Qur'an uses for the rebellious among the jinn as well as for the rebellious among human beings. The jinn are a created order distinct from humanity: *“As for the jinn, We created them earlier from smokeless fire.”* (15:27). They are addressed by revelation alongside humans, and they are capable of belief and of rebellion.

The word shayṭān describes a character rather than a species, and its application here is informative. The beings subjected to Solomon are not the obedient jinn but the recalcitrant ones, which means the gift is a gift of control over adversaries. That is why the following verse adds a third category — others bound in chains — since some of the same beings required restraint as well as direction.

The same assembly is described in al-Naml as a military body rather than a workforce: jinn, humans and birds rallied and organised (27:17). Solomon's authority over them was therefore not a private arrangement but a public order of rank, which is what makes the gifts that follow intelligible as a kingdom rather than as a set of personal favours.

**Builders on the Land**
The building work is described in detail elsewhere, and the detail confirms that this was labour of the highest order: *“They made for him whatever he desired of sanctuaries, statues, basins as large as reservoirs, and cooking pots fixed ˹into the ground˺. ˹We ordered:˺ ‘Work gratefully, O family of David!’ ˹Only˺ a few of My servants are ˹truly˺ grateful.”* (34:13).

Two things in that account bear on this verse. The first is the scale — basins the size of reservoirs, pots immovable once set, which implies construction beyond human capacity. The second is the instruction attached to the work: gratitude. The jinn are told to work gratefully, and the family of David is addressed in the same breath, which places the whole enterprise under a religious obligation rather than under a commercial contract.

**Divers in the Sea**
Diving is the second occupation, and al-Anbiyāʾ confirms it with the same economy: *“And ˹We subjected˺ some jinn that dived for him, and performed other duties. It is We Who kept them in check.”* (21:82). The closing clause of that verse identifies the real agent of the subjection, and it is worth carrying across to this one: the divers are held in check by God, not by Solomon.

The work itself was understood by the commentators as the extraction of riches from the sea — pearls, coral and whatever else the depths held. What matters for the verse is that the deepest and least accessible part of the created order was made available to a servant. The sea in the Qur'an is repeatedly a sign of divine power, and a man whose labourers work on its floor is exercising a privilege that has no parallel in the Book.

**The Limits Built Into the Gift**
The subjection is not unconditional, and the Qur'an states the condition plainly: *“And ˹We subjected˺ some of the jinn to work under him by his Lord's Will. And whoever of them deviated from Our command, We made them taste the torment of the blaze.”* (34:12). The jinn work under Solomon, but they answer to God, and deviation is punished by the one who lent the authority.

That clause protects the account from any reading in which Solomon becomes a master of independent powers. The jinn are not his creatures and his command is not final. He holds a delegated authority over beings who remain under a higher jurisdiction, and the arrangement is a model of how power is held in the Qur'an: completely in practice, and never in ownership.

The arrangement also has a warning attached for the reader. The Qur'an records that human beings sought refuge with jinn and were led further astray by them: *“And some men used to seek refuge with some jinn—so they increased each other in wickedness.”* (72:6). Solomon ruled the jinn and was not harmed by them; ordinary people who traffic with them are harmed. The difference is the authority, and the authority was a gift to one man, not a possibility for all.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:38

> and others bound together in chains

**Expanded Commentary**

**"And Others": A Third Category**

The verse adds a group to the two named before it, and the addition changes the character of the whole account. The builders and the divers were subjected for work; this third group is subjected for restraint. The word *others* is deliberately unspecific, and the absence of a task attached to them is the information the verse is giving.

The distinction is not a matter of degree but of kind. A labourer under command is an agent with a function; a prisoner in chains is an agent with his function removed. The chapter's list of Solomon's powers therefore includes both the direction of willing service and the neutralisation of active opposition, and the second is the harder of the two.

The verse's grammatical dependence on the one before it is also worth noting. It has no verb of its own and continues the sentence begun in the previous verse, which binds the restrained to the employed in a single act of subjection. One gift, three groups, two treatments.

**Bound in Chains**

The image is concrete: beings fastened together, restrained by physical bonds. The word used denotes fetters in which the hands are joined to the neck or to one another, and the plural form indicates that the restraint was applied to a number of them.

The physicality of the image is worth noting, because jinn are elsewhere described as invisible to human beings and as incapable of being held by ordinary means. To describe them as chained is to describe a subjection that operates at a level below the physical — an authority that reaches whatever they are. Al-Anbiyāʾ states the same fact in plainer terms: *“It is We Who kept them in check.”* (21:82). The chains are the visible form of a restraint whose agent is God.

The plural also tells the reader something about the scale of the opposition. A single chained rebel would be an incident; a group of them, bound together, is a suppressed faction. The verse does not say how many there were or what they had attempted, and the omission leaves the image to carry the weight of an unnamed history.

**Why Some Were Employed and Others Bound**

The two treatments imply two dispositions among the beings subjected. Those who could be directed were directed; those who could not were restrained. The Qur'an does not explain the division, and the commentators supply the obvious explanation: recalcitrance. The ones in chains are the ones who would not work.

That explanation is consistent with the rest of the Book's account of the jinn, which describes them as divided between belief and rebellion just as human beings are. The verse therefore presents a political reality in miniature: a ruler's subjects include both the cooperative and the hostile, and the two require different treatment. Solomon is shown exercising both forms of authority, and neither is presented as more admirable than the other.

The distinction also removes any suggestion that the jinn were converted. They were not persuaded, taught or reconciled; they were held. The chapter is honest about the limits of what power can achieve with an unwilling creature, and it does not pretend that the chains produced anything other than compliance.

**The Image of a Rebel Held**

The chained figure carries a weight beyond its function in the narrative. A being who opposed a prophet-king and is now fastened in fetters is the clearest possible image of defeated rebellion, and the Qur'an uses that image here without elaboration.

The image also speaks back to the earlier parts of the chapter. The parties of the destroyed peoples were routed, the Quraysh were called a host bound for defeat, and the objectors were asked whether they could climb to heaven. Now a king is shown holding the enemies he could not persuade. The chapter's treatment of opposition has moved from argument to restraint, and the movement is not a change of view but a change of case.

**Power That Includes Restraint**

The inclusion of the chained in a list of gifts is a statement about the nature of authority. A ruler who can only employ the willing is not sovereign; a ruler who can also hold the unwilling is. The verse treats the second capacity as part of the favour conferred, and it does so without moralising about punishment.

That treatment is deliberate. The chapter has been careful throughout to present Solomon's power as a trust rather than a possession, and the chains fit the pattern: they are a responsibility before they are a privilege. A man who holds rebels in fetters is answerable for what he does with them, and the following verse addresses that answerability directly.

**The Shortest Verse Carrying the Heaviest Claim**

Three words in translation, and they complete the account of a sovereignty without parallel. The verse's brevity is not a lack of content but a refusal to elaborate. The reader is given the fact and left with the image, and the image does the work that description would otherwise do.

That economy is characteristic of the chapter's final movement. The gifts are listed without praise, the restraint without apology, and the account closes by naming what all of it amounts to in the next two verses: a gift, and a standing before God.

There is a further reason for the brevity. Any elaboration on the chained jinn would invite speculation, and the commentaries show how quickly speculation multiplies when a text offers an image without a context. By saying nothing more, the verse forecloses the invention of names, motives and plots, and leaves the reader with exactly what was revealed: a group held, a king given the holding, and a giver who did the holding first.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:39

> ˹Allah said,˺ “This is Our gift, so give or withhold ˹as you wish˺, never to be called to account.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Gift Declared as a Gift, and Why the Exemption Was Given**
The verse interrupts the list of powers with a statement about their origin, and the interruption is the point. Wind, builders, divers, chains — each has been described in terms of what Solomon can do with it, and the verse now names what all of it is: a gift, from God, given without price.

The declaration is necessary because of the danger the list creates. A reader moving through an enumeration of powers can easily arrive at an impression of the man's greatness rather than the giver's generosity. The verse corrects the impression before it settles, and it does so in the divine voice, which makes the correction authoritative rather than editorial.

The context supplies the reason, and it is the trial of the previous verses. Solomon has just experienced the loss of his throne, and the loss taught him what the gift now confirms: nothing he holds is his by right. A man who has learned that lesson does not need to be audited, because he will not treat the gift as a possession.

The verse therefore completes the education begun in the horse review. Attachment was the fault; loss was the lesson; discretion is the result. The chapter does not spell out this progression, but the order of the verses is the argument, and the exemption sits at its end as the mark of a completed instruction.

**"Give or Withhold"**
The permission that follows is unusually wide. Solomon is told to bestow or to withhold as he sees fit, and no criterion is supplied for the choice. A ruler given that discretion over powers of this magnitude is being trusted in a way that has no parallel elsewhere in the chapter.

The trust is intelligible only in light of what has just been narrated. This is a man who recognised his own distraction without being told, who recalled the object of it, and who asked for forgiveness before he asked for anything else. The discretion granted here is the chapter's answer to a question it has been building toward: what kind of person can be given power without being corrupted by it? The answer is the one who has already shown that he knows where the power comes from.

**"Never to Be Called to Account"**
The final clause is the most discussed in the verse, and it must be read precisely. Solomon is told that he will not be held to account for his giving and withholding. Some have read this as a suspension of accountability in the general sense, and the reading cannot be right: the Qur'an holds every soul accountable, and it holds its prophets to a higher standard than others rather than a lower.

The clause is best understood as applying to the matter in hand. The gifts are God's, given to Solomon without obligation, and his disposal of them is not something he will be required to justify as a debtor justifies an expenditure. He is not a steward who must render accounts for another's property; he is a recipient deciding what to do with what he has been given. The exemption is from the reckoning of a trustee, not from the reckoning of a soul.

The two reckonings are different in kind, and the distinction runs through the whole Book. A person is answerable for what he did with his life, and that accounting is never waived. He is not answerable for the size of the gift he received, or for choosing to give part of it away rather than keep it. The verse removes the second sort of question from Solomon's case and leaves the first entirely intact, which is why it can promise an exemption without contradicting the chapter's insistence that everyone will be judged.

**The Gift Answering the Opening Verses**
The verse also answers the objectors of the sūrah's beginning, and the answer is precise. They asked whether they held the treasuries of God's mercy and whether the kingdom of the heavens and the earth belonged to them (38:9–10). Here is a man who was given a treasury to dispose of and a kingdom beyond any other, and the verse says in terms that it is a gift.

The contrast is between two attitudes toward power. The notables of Makkah assumed that rank is owned and defended; Solomon is told that everything he holds was given and may be given away. The chapter does not draw the comparison explicitly, but it has placed the two statements in the same sūrah and left the reader to hold them together.

**A Sentence That Defines the Chapter's View of Power**
Every portrait in this sūrah is governed by the claim in this verse. David's kingdom was strengthened by God. Solomon's powers were subjected to him. Job's family was returned to him. In each case the possession is described as conferred, and in each case the recipient's response is gratitude or return rather than ownership.

The verse states the principle in its purest form, and it states it about the most powerful man in the Book. If a king with the wind, the jinn and the sea at his command is told that all of it is a gift, then the claim applies with greater force to everyone with less.

The instruction attached to the building work elsewhere makes the same point from the other side: *“˹We ordered:˺ ‘Work gratefully, O family of David!’ ˹Only˺ a few of My servants are ˹truly˺ grateful.”* (34:13). A gift is met with gratitude, and gratitude is rare. The verse leaves the rarity standing beside the generosity, and the pairing is the chapter's summary of the human condition.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:40

> And he will indeed have ˹a status of˺ closeness to Us and an honourable destination

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Promise in the Same Words, and Nearness After Power**
The verse repeats, almost word for word, the promise made to David fifteen verses earlier (38:25). Nearness to God, an honourable destination — the two gifts are given to the son in the same formulation in which they were given to the father, and the repetition is the chapter's clearest statement of what its portraits have in common.

The effect of the repetition is to make the promise a formula rather than a personal reward. A sentence used twice, of two men with very different lives and very different failures, is no longer a comment on either of them. It is a statement about the category they belong to: the servant who returns.

The order of the two gifts is worth attending to. Solomon has just been given the wind, the builders, the divers and the chained, and the verse that follows describes his reward as nearness to God. The greatest accumulation of power in the Book is followed by a promise that contains no power at all.

That juxtaposition is the chapter's judgment on the value of the gifts. Nearness is a relation, and a relation cannot be commanded, spent or lost through mismanagement. The kingdom Solomon asked for was granted in full, and the verse then identifies what he actually has that matters. A reader who remembers the prayer will notice that nearness was not part of it.

**An Honourable Destination for a Man Who Lost His Throne**
The second promise has a particular force in Solomon's case. He watched a body sit on his own throne and experienced the loss of the thing that defined his public standing. The promise of a good place of return answers that loss directly, and it answers it in the same terms: a destination, a place to come back to.

The word carries the sense of a returning as well as an arriving, and the double sense suits the account. Solomon returned to his throne after the trial, and the verse promises a return of a different kind at the end. The chapter is careful to give both, and to give the second in the same words it gave to David, who had never lost a throne but had lost something harder to name.

**The Closing of Solomon's Account**
Ten verses are given to Solomon, and the last of them contains no reference to his kingdom. The account closes on the same two words with which it opened — servant, and one who turns — and everything in between is treated as incidental to them.

The proportion is the chapter's method. David received seven verses of narrative and one of promise; Solomon received nine of narrative and one of promise. In both cases the narrative is the material and the promise is the finding, and the finding is identical. The chapter is not comparing two men; it is demonstrating twice over that the same conclusion follows from very different premises.

The closing also reverses the direction of the account. It began with what Solomon was given — a son to David, an excellent servant — moved through what he lost and regained, catalogued what was placed under his command, and ends on what he will have. The last term of the series is not a possession but a position, and the position is described in relation to God rather than to any creature.

**Nearness as the Highest Category the Book Offers**
The word used for closeness here is the same one the Qur'an applies to the highest of the resurrected. Wāqiʿah divides humanity into three groups and names the first of them those brought near: *“They are the ones nearest ˹to Allah˺”* (56:11), and it describes their end: *“So, if the deceased is one of those brought near ˹to Us˺”* (56:88). Al-Muṭaffifīn places the same group at the centre of the highest record: *“witnessed by those nearest ˹to Allah˺”* (83:21).

The Qur'an is equally explicit that this rank is reached by belief and action rather than by wealth or connection: *“It is not your wealth or children that bring you closer to Us. But those who believe and do good—it is they who will have a multiplied reward for what they did, and they will be secure in ˹elevated˺ mansions.”* (34:37). Applied to Solomon, the promise therefore names the highest category available to a human being, and it gives it to a man whose chapter-length record includes a distraction and a theft of his throne. The giving is the argument.

**What the Repetition Establishes**
Two portraits, two trials, two failures, one promise. The chapter has now shown a judge who ruled without hearing both sides and a king who lost his throne, and it has answered both with the same sentence. The repetition forecloses the reading that either man's restoration was a special favour.

That foreclosure matters for the audience the sūrah was revealed to. The men of Makkah measured people by what they held, and the chapter answers with two men who held everything and were commended for something else. The verse places the answer at the end of the second portrait, where it cannot be missed, and the third portrait — a man who held nothing at all — begins in the next verse.

The repetition has one further effect on the reader. A promise stated once can be taken as a reward for a particular achievement; stated twice, in identical words, it becomes a standing offer with stated terms. The terms are visible in both accounts: the man was a servant, and he turned. Nothing else in either life was decisive, and nothing else is required.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:41

> And remember Our servant Job, when he cried out to his Lord, “Satan has afflicted me with distress and suffering.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Servant Introduced by His Complaint**
The third portrait in the chapter opens differently from the other two. David was introduced by his strength and Solomon by his excellence; Job is introduced by his suffering, and the first thing the reader hears from him is a complaint.
