The verse does not describe his condition, does not list his losses and does not characterise his endurance. It quotes his cry and lets the cry do the describing.

The effect is immediate and human. A reader meets Job in the middle of his affliction rather than before it, which means the account begins where the suffering is already old. Nothing is said about how long it has lasted, and the omission preserves the weight of the cry: this is not a man reporting a misfortune but a man at the end of his capacity, speaking to God about it.

**Blame Placed Where It Belongs, and Why Job Comes Third**
The complaint names an agent, and the naming is theologically careful. Job does not say that God has afflicted him, and he does not say that no one has. He attributes the harm to Satan, and the attribution allows the verse to record a man's distress without recording an accusation against God.

The naming also identifies the nature of the test. This is not a random misfortune but an assault, and an assault implies an adversary with an aim. The Qur'an's account of Satan throughout is consistent: he works on human beings through loss, fear and suggestion, and he is permitted to operate within limits set for him. Job's cry places his suffering inside that framework, and the framework is what makes his endurance meaningful rather than merely impressive.

The order of the three servants is not accidental, and the third place is the most instructive. David held a throne and a revelation; Solomon held more than David, including the wind and the jinn. Job held nothing at all. The chapter moves from power to greater power to total loss, and it gives each man the same commendation at the end.

That arrangement dismantles any reading in which a person's standing with God is a function of his circumstances. The two kings were tested through what they had; the sufferer was tested through what he lost. The chapter measures all three by the same standard, and the standard is stated in the same word each time.

**Distress and Suffering: What the Two Words Cover**
The cry names two things, and the commentators take them as covering the whole of Job's loss. The first denotes weariness, exhaustion, the fatigue of a body and a mind worn down over time. The second denotes punishment or pain — the acute, positive hurt that accompanies the exhaustion.

Together they describe a condition that is both chronic and severe. Weariness alone can be borne if there is relief in prospect; pain alone can be borne if it is short. The combination is what breaks people, and the verse's two words are chosen to describe it exactly. The tradition adds the particulars: loss of property, loss of family, and a painful disease, endured for years — seven years and seven months in one report. The verse itself needs none of those details, since the two nouns carry them.

**The Qur'an's Job Is Not a Treatise on Evil**
The Book's longest account of Job is four verses, and the shortest is two. Al-Anbiyāʾ records the same cry in shorter form: *“And ˹remember˺ when Job cried out to his Lord, ‘I have been touched with adversity, and You are the Most Merciful of the merciful.’”* (21:83), and the answer follows at once: *“So We answered his prayer and removed his adversity, and gave him back his family, twice as many, as a mercy from Us and a lesson for the ˹devoted˺ worshippers.”* (21:84).

What the Qur'an does not do is turn the story into a philosophical inquiry. There is no council of friends, no debate about why the righteous suffer, no divine speech from the whirlwind. The account is a record of endurance and its outcome, and the omission is a choice about what the story is for. Job is listed among the prophets to whom revelation was given (4:163) and among those God guided (6:84); he is presented as a model, not as a case study.

**The Shortest Prayer in the Chapter**
David's prayer is recorded at length elsewhere, and Solomon's is given here in full with two requests and a divine name. Job's is a single sentence, and it contains no request at all. He states his affliction and names its agent, and stops.

The absence of a petition is the most remarkable feature of the verse. A man in that condition could have asked for relief, for an explanation or for death, and the Qur'an's other account of him ends with an implicit appeal to God's mercy. Here there is only the statement, and the statement was enough: the answer comes in the next verse without any request having been made. The chapter is showing what a servant's honest report of his own state is worth.

That honesty is worth distinguishing from complaint. A complaint is addressed to an audience and looks for sympathy; a report is addressed to the one who can act and looks for nothing in particular. Job's sentence has the form of the second. It accuses no one, excuses nothing, and asks for nothing, and it is the most complete statement of need in the chapter.

The distinction matters for the reader as well as for the prophet. A person in prolonged distress usually drifts toward the first form, and the drift is understandable. The verse offers the second form as available, and it shows the outcome that followed it.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:42

> ˹We responded,˺ “Stomp your foot: ˹now˺ here is a cool ˹and refreshing˺ spring for washing and drinking.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Cure Delivered as a Task**
The answer to Job's cry is not a healing but an instruction. He is told to strike the ground with his foot, and the water appears at the place he strikes. Nothing is done to him; something is required of him, and the relief follows the action rather than preceding it.

The structure is unusual for an account of miraculous healing and it is worth stating plainly. A man exhausted by years of disease is asked to perform a physical act before he is given anything, and the act is trivial in itself — a stamp of the foot. What it establishes is that the servant remains an agent. Affliction had reduced Job to a state in which almost nothing was in his power; the first thing God gives back to him is a small piece of agency.

**Stomp Your Foot: Washing and Drinking, Outside and Inside**
The command is concrete and physical, and its specificity has been read in two ways. The literal reading takes it at face value: the ground was struck, and the spring came out of it. The second treats the striking as the occasion rather than the cause, since a foot cannot open a spring, and reads the command as a way of directing Job's attention to where the water was already about to appear.

Both readings preserve the same point, which is that the water was given in connection with an act of obedience. The Qur'an's other account of Job does not mention the foot at all — it says simply that God answered him and removed his affliction (21:84) — and the addition here is the detail the chapter wants: the servant participates in his own relief.

The two purposes correspond to two kinds of affliction, and the correspondence is the reason the verse names both. Washing addresses what is external — the sores, the skin, the visible marks of the disease. Drinking addresses what is internal — the organs, the blood, the damage no one could see.

The pairing is medically coherent and theologically pointed. Job's suffering was total, and the cure is total. Nothing is left untreated, and the verse is careful to say so by naming both routes of application. A reader who knows what chronic illness does to a body will recognise the completeness of the description, and the completeness is what the word *mercy* in the next verse refers to.

**Cool Water, and Whether There Were Two Springs**
The water is described as cool, and the adjective carries more than temperature. After a disease traditionally described as burning and painful, cool water is the exact opposite of the affliction, and the Qur'an's account of the punishments of Hell will describe boiling liquid as one of its drinks (38:57). The contrast is deliberate, and it places Job's relief in the register of mercy rather than mere remedy.

The commentators divide over whether one spring or two is meant. The majority hold that there were two: one for washing and one for drinking. The reading is supported by the two purposes named in the verse, and by the natural inference that different water was required for the two uses. The single-spring reading is simpler and requires no addition to the text, but it leaves the pairing of purposes unexplained. Neither reading changes the substance, which is that both routes of healing were provided at once.

**The Rain, the Fields and the Golden Locusts**
The tradition adds two details to the account, and both are recorded in the most authoritative collection. Rain is reported to have fallen on Job's fields until they were full, restoring the property he had lost. And the Prophet ﷺ reported that while Job was bathing, locusts of gold began falling on him, and he started gathering them in his garment; his Lord said to him, *have I not made you rich enough not to need what you see*, and Job answered, *yes, my Lord, but I cannot do without Your blessing* (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3391; also 7493).

The second report is the more instructive of the two, because it answers a question nobody asked. A man who has been stripped of everything and is now being given gold does not stop collecting it, and his explanation is not greed but gratitude — he cannot bear to refuse a gift from God. The chapter's portrait of Job ends where the report ends: a man whose relationship to what he has is defined by who gave it.

**Why the Healing Required an Act**
The whole verse could have been a single sentence: We healed him. The Qur'an chose instead to record a command, an action and a result, and the choice tells the reader something about how relief is given.

A cure that arrives without any participation from the sufferer is a fact about the sufferer's condition. A cure that requires him to strike the ground is a fact about his standing. Job is not treated as an object of mercy but addressed as a servant, and the address is the first sign that the affliction is ending. The chapter gives the restoration of the family in the next verse; it gives the restoration of agency here.

The same pattern runs through the chapter's other portraits. David was not forgiven until he had fallen down and turned; Solomon's kingdom was not returned until he had asked for it in a particular order. Nothing in this sūrah is granted to a passive recipient, and Job's foot is the smallest and clearest instance of the rule.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:43

> And We gave him back his family, twice as many, as a mercy from Us and a lesson for people of reason

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Restoration in Two Parts, a Mercy and a Lesson**
The verse describes a single gift with two components, and the second component is what makes the account distinctive. Job is given back his family — the same family, restored — and then given their like along with them. The doubling is not a replacement of the lost by the new but an addition to the returned.

Commentators differ on the mechanics of the first part. Some hold that the family was brought back to life; others that they were healed of what had afflicted them; others that they had been absent and were returned. The differences matter less than the shared claim, which is that what was lost was not compensated but recovered. The verse is explicit that the same people came back, and the explicitness is what gives the doubling its force.

The two reasons given are a mercy from God and a reminder for people of understanding. The first is directed at Job, the second at everyone who hears the story, and the pairing tells the reader that the account has two audiences.

The mercy is the fact of the restoration; the lesson is what the restoration demonstrates. What it demonstrates is not that patience is always repaid in this life — the Qur'an does not promise that anywhere — but that God's dealings with a patient servant end in increase rather than in mere survival. The lesson is available to anyone in affliction, and it does not depend on receiving the same reward Job received.

**"Twice as Many": What the Doubling Signifies**
An increase of the same kind is a different thing from a substitute. A man who loses a child and later has another has not been repaid; a man whose child is returned and who is given more besides has been given something that exceeds the original state. The verse's arithmetic is doing this work, and it is doing it deliberately.

The doubling also answers a question the story raises without asking it. Suffering endured for years is not balanced by a return to the starting point, because the years are gone. The Qur'an's answer is that the restoration exceeds the loss, not in the currency of time but in the currency of what remains. The verse does not attempt to justify the years; it simply reports that the outcome was greater than the beginning.

**A Contrast With the Other Account of Job**
The Qur'anic ending differs from the Biblical one in a detail worth noting. In the Book of Job, the servant's livestock is doubled at the end, but the number of his children is given as the same as before — seven sons and three daughters — which the tradition reads as indicating that the first children were not lost but await him. The Qur'an states the doubling of the family itself, without qualification.

The difference is not a contradiction so much as a different emphasis. The Biblical account is concerned with the integrity of a man who kept faith through argument and silence; the Qur'anic account is concerned with the generosity of the restoration. The verse makes the generosity explicit in a number, and it attaches two reasons to it, neither of which appears in the other account.

**"People of Reason" Again**
The phrase used here is the same one that appeared in verse 29, where the Book was said to be revealed so that people of reason may be mindful. The word denotes those who possess the kernel of a thing, its inner substance rather than its shell, and its reappearance at the close of Job's account connects the two verses.

The connection is precise. The chapter said that its material exists to be contemplated, and it now names the contemplation it wants: a reader who sees the story of Job and draws the conclusion from it. The people of reason are not those who admire Job's endurance from a distance; they are those who understand what the outcome means for their own afflictions.

The phrase appears repeatedly across the Qur'an in exactly this function, always at the end of a sign or a story, always naming the audience that has understood it (2:179; 3:190; 12:111; 14:52; 39:9). Its use here closes the account of Job the way a full stop closes a sentence, and it marks the reader who has followed as one of them.

**What Restoration Does Not Undo**
The verse is generous and it is also honest, because it does not claim that the suffering was erased. The years of disease and loss are not returned, and the account does not suggest that the memory of them was removed. What is restored is the family, increased; what is added is the mercy and the lesson.

That proportion is the chapter's final word on affliction. It does not offer a theodicy and it does not minimise the pain. It reports that the servant was patient, that he was found to be so, and that his end was better than his beginning. The verse leaves the reader with those three facts and asks nothing more of him than understanding.

The restraint is what makes the account usable. A story in which the sufferer is compensated exactly would suggest that patience is a transaction with a fixed rate; a story in which he is not compensated at all would suggest that it is pointless. This verse stands between the two, and the position is the one most readers of it actually occupy — relieved of something, still carrying something, and told that the carrying is not the last word.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:44

> ˹And We said to him,˺ “Take in your hand a bundle of grass, and strike ˹your wife˺ with it, and do not break your oath.” We truly found him patient. What an excellent servant ˹he was˺! Indeed, he ˹constantly˺ turned ˹to Allah˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Instruction Arriving Without Its Context**
The verse opens on a command whose background is not given. A bundle of grass is to be taken, a strike is to be delivered, and an oath is not to be broken. The reader is not told what the oath was, why it was made, or what the strike is meant to accomplish, and the omission has generated centuries of explanation.

The Qur'an does this repeatedly with its narratives, and the reason is consistent: the circumstances of a command belong to its occasion, while the command itself belongs to everyone. What the verse commits to is that a vow existed, that keeping it literally would have caused harm, and that God supplied a way of keeping it without the harm. The mechanism is a legal device; the principle is that an oath is binding and that a merciful fulfilment is better than a literal one.

**The Third Use of the Word, and the Story of the Wife**
The standard explanation is that Job's wife, under pressure from Satan, urged him to say or do something that would bring relief, and that Job, angered by her having listened to the enemy of God, swore to punish her. God then permitted him to strike her with a bundle of grass so that the oath would be fulfilled without injury.

The story is widely repeated and it is coherent, but it has no support in the Qur'an or in the Prophetic tradition. Every element of it — the prompting, the specific urging, the vow of punishment — has to be supplied from outside the text. The honest position is to report the story as the traditional reading while noting that it is not established, and to prefer an account that requires nothing beyond the verse itself.

The verse closes with the intensive noun for one who turns repeatedly, the word used of David in verse 17 and of Solomon in verse 30. Its third appearance completes the chapter's triptych, and the completion is the point of the three portraits.

Three men, three very different lives, one description. A king who erred in judgement, a king who was distracted by his possessions and lost his throne, a man who lost everything and kept his tongue from complaint. The chapter does not rank them and does not compare their failures. It gives them the same two titles — excellent servant, and one who returns — and leaves the reader to see that the titles are earned in different ways by all three.

**The Reading That Stays Inside the Text**
The alternative reading takes the bundle of grass as part of Job's treatment — herbs or leaves used in washing or as a remedy — and takes the injunction not to break the oath as either a general command or an assurance that using the remedy did not violate any vow he had made to God.

This reading has the advantage of requiring no invented narrative. It also fits the preceding verses, in which Job has just been given water for washing and drinking as the means of his healing. A bundle of plant material belongs naturally in that sequence. Its weakness is that it makes the mention of striking harder to explain, since striking is an odd word for applying a remedy. Neither reading resolves every difficulty, and the verse's own brevity is the reason.

**"We Truly Found Him Patient"**
The commendation uses a verb of finding, and the verb is significant. God did not make Job patient or appoint him patient; He found him so, which means the patience was exercised before it was recognised. The recognition is a verdict on conduct already performed.

The word for patience here is the same root the chapter used in its opening instruction to the Prophet — be patient with what they say (38:17). The connection is deliberate. The man told to endure is shown a servant whose endurance was examined and confirmed, and the confirmation is stated in the divine voice rather than in a narrator's summary. Job's patience is not the reader's impression of him; it is a finding.

Al-Anbiyāʾ makes the same finding in different words, listing Job with Ishmael and Ⱬul-Kifl as men who were steadfast (21:85). The two chapters agree on the verdict and differ only in the company they name, which suggests that the patience is the quality being measured in both places rather than the suffering that occasioned it.

**A Legal Principle Delivered in a Narrative**
Buried in this verse is a ruling that the Islamic legal tradition drew out and applied: an oath may be fulfilled by a symbolic act when its literal fulfilment would cause disproportionate harm. The bundle of grass became a precedent, and the precedent rests on the fact that God Himself prescribed the substitution rather than annulling the vow.

That is why the verse does not simply cancel the oath. The oath is honoured, and the harm is removed, and both are achieved in a single gesture. The chapter gives the principle in the form of a story, which is how it gives most of its law, and the story has outlived the circumstances that produced it.

The principle also carries a limit that is easy to miss. The substitution was authorised, not improvised. A man who decides for himself that his oath can be discharged symbolically has not kept the principle; he has broken the oath. What the verse permits is a fulfilment prescribed from above, and the difference between that and a self-serving reinterpretation is the whole content of the ruling.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:45

> And remember Our servants: Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob—the men of strength and insight

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Names Given Without a Story**

The verse names three prophets and describes them in four words. No narrative follows, no incident is recalled, no trial is mentioned. After the detailed accounts of David, Solomon and Job, the chapter shifts to a list, and the shift is abrupt enough to require explanation.

The explanation is that the chapter has made its point and no longer needs narrative to make it. Three portraits have shown what a servant does under test; the list that follows shows that the pattern is not confined to three men. Abraham, Isaac and Jacob are named because they are known, and their being known is the entire content of the verse. A reader who knows their stories supplies the evidence; a reader who does not is told that these men belong to the same class.

The choice of these three is also a choice about audience. They are the ancestors of the Israelites and, through Ishmael, of the Arabs, which means the verse names a lineage that both sides of the Qur'an's first audience claimed. To describe them as servants possessed of strength and insight is to define the inheritance in terms that neither party could contest and neither had been practising.

**"Servants", in the Plural**

The word used is the plural of ʿabd, the same term applied individually to David, Solomon and Job in their own verses. Its plural form here is the first time the chapter speaks of servants as a group, and the change of number is significant.

Servanthood in the Qur'an is not a rank attained by individuals in isolation; it is a condition shared by a line. The three men named are a father, a son and a grandson, and their being described together as servants makes the point that the condition is transmitted and sustained across generations. Al-Anʿām states the same fact in the form of a genealogy: *“And We blessed him with Isaac and Jacob. We guided them all as We previously guided Noah and those among his descendants: David, Solomon, Job, Joseph, Moses, and Aaron. This is how We reward the good-doers.”* (6:84).

**"Men of Strength and Insight"**

The description is a pairing, and the pairing covers two distinct capacities. The first word denotes strength — the same noun used of David in verse 17 — and in the usage of the commentators it refers to strength in worship and in obedience rather than in body or in arms. The second denotes sight or insight, and it is explained as the vision of the heart: perception of religious reality rather than of physical objects.

The pairing is not a list of two virtues but a description of one integrity. Strength without insight is zeal without direction; insight without strength is understanding without effect. The three men are described as possessing both, and the description is the chapter's definition of what a mature servant looks like.

**Insight as a Kind of Seeing**

The word for insight is worth pausing on, because the Qur'an uses it elsewhere for a perception that goes beyond evidence. To have insight into a matter is to see through to what it is, and applied to religious reality it means seeing the Hereafter as present rather than as promised.

That sense explains the verse that follows, which describes the three men as having been chosen for the remembrance of the Abode. Insight and remembrance are the same capacity from two sides: a man who sees the next world clearly cannot help remembering it, and a man who remembers it constantly acquires the sight. The verse names the capacity here and its exercise in the next verse, and the two belong together.

The pairing also explains why these three are named after the three portraits. David, Solomon and Job were each tested through something they could see — a case, a herd of horses, a disease. The men named here are described by their capacity to see what is not visible, and the contrast sets up the destination the chapter is about to describe.

**Why These Three, After Job**

The placement of the list immediately after Job's account is not arbitrary. Job's story ends with a restoration of his family — the same people returned and doubled — and the three men named here are a family: Abraham, his son, his grandson. The chapter moves from a family restored to a family chosen.

The connection answers a question the Job account might leave. A reader could conclude that Job's reward was material and therefore exceptional. The list corrects that impression by naming a line whose distinction consists in nothing material at all: strength in worship and insight into the unseen. The chapter is telling its audience which kind of inheritance it considers worth having.

**A Chapter That Has Become a Roster**

From this verse onward the sūrah stops narrating and begins listing. Three names here, three more in verse 48, and then the two destinations. The change of mode is a sign that the argument is complete and the chapter is now assembling its witnesses.

The method has an advantage that narrative does not. A story can be admired from a distance; a list cannot. Three names in a row, described in the same words, ask the reader to ask which category he belongs to, and the question is more direct than any narrative could make it.

The roster also does something for the portraits that came before it. David, Solomon and Job could each be read as exceptional — a king, a greater king, a sufferer of unusual endurance. Placed beside Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, they become instances of a type, and the type is defined by the four words this verse uses. Strength and insight are available to anyone; the chapter has simply shown three men who had them and three more who are about to be named.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:46

> We truly chose them for the honour of proclaiming the Hereafter

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Choice Made for a Purpose, Remembered Through Him**
The verse explains the previous one. The three men were described as possessing strength and insight; this verse says what those qualities were for. God chose them, and the choosing had an object: the remembrance of the Abode. Nothing in the sentence suggests that the choice was a reward for something already achieved, or that the qualities were possessed independently of the purpose.

The construction is important because it reverses the ordinary way of thinking about religious distinction. A man is not first excellent and then selected; he is selected for a function, and the excellence is the equipment for the function. The chapter's three portraits followed the same logic — each man was given something and then tested with it — and the verse generalises the pattern to the whole line of prophets.

Al-Tustarī is reported to have drawn a further distinction from the phrase, and it is worth recording because it identifies the highest form of the remembrance described. God purified them for the remembrance of God for God alone, not for the attainment of a reward; they remembered Him through Him and for Him.

The distinction separates two kinds of devotion that look identical from outside. One remembers the next world because it wants the next world; the other remembers it because the One who made it is worthy of remembrance. The first is a calculation and the second is a relation, and the verse's word for purity applies strictly only to the second. Al-Tustarī's gloss is not in the text, but it names a difference the text assumes.

**"The Honour of Proclaiming the Hereafter"**
The phrase translated here has been rendered in several ways, and the range of meaning is narrow but real. It can be read as a purification *by means of* something pure — the remembrance of the Abode being the pure thing through which the men were made pure. It can be read as a purifying of them *with* a pure quality. And it can be read, as here, as an honour or distinction conferred, consisting in the constant remembrance of the next world.

All three readings converge on a single claim: what these men were given was an orientation rather than a possession. The Abode — the Dār, the next world as a place of arrival — was made present to them, and its presence in their minds is what the verse calls their distinction. No wealth, no victory and no office is mentioned, and the omission is the point of the verse.

**Purified by What Is Pure**
The commentators explain the purification in concrete terms: God removed the love of this world from their hearts and made them constantly mindful of the Hereafter. The two halves of that explanation are one action, since a heart cannot hold both orientations at full strength. What is removed is not the use of the world but the love of it, and what replaces it is not contempt for the world but attention to what comes after it.

The explanation is worth stating carefully, because the verse is easily misread as asceticism. Nothing in it praises poverty, and the chapter has just described two kings who held great wealth. What is praised is the location of the heart's attachment. A rich man whose attachment is elsewhere is pure in the sense this verse means; a poor man whose attachment is fixed on what he lacks is not.

**A Purpose Stated, Not a Virtue Praised**
The verse's grammar is unusual in the Qur'an's descriptions of prophets. It does not say that these men were righteous, or truthful, or patient — all of which are said of prophets elsewhere. It says that they were chosen for something, and the something is named.

That choice of formulation is the chapter's answer to the values of its first audience. The notables of Makkah measured a man by what he had and what he commanded. The verse measures three of the greatest men in the Book by what they remembered. The measure is available to anyone, costs nothing, and requires no position, and the chapter places it immediately after the account of a man who lost everything.

The formulation also protects the men it praises. A statement that a prophet was righteous invites the reader to compare himself and conclude either that he is close or that the standard is unreachable. A statement that he was chosen for a function invites a different question — what am I chosen for — and that question can be answered by anyone.

**Why the Abode and Not the Garden**
The word used is the Abode — a general term for the dwelling place to come — rather than the Garden or the Fire specifically. The generality is significant. A man who remembers the Garden wants something; a man who remembers the Abode knows that this world is not where he lives, and the knowledge governs everything else.

That is the quality the verse identifies as the distinction of the three men, and it is the quality the chapter has been describing in its portraits from the beginning. David's kingdom, Solomon's wind, Job's restoration — each is presented as a loan, and each recipient is praised for treating it as one.

The Abode also connects this verse to the destination the chapter is about to describe. Verses 49 to 54 will set out what the Abode contains for the righteous, and verses 55 to 64 what it contains for the transgressors. The three men named here were distinguished by keeping both halves of that description in view, and the chapter moves from their remembrance of it to the thing itself.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:47

> And in Our sight they are truly among the chosen and the finest

**Expanded Commentary**

**"In Our Sight": The Standard Named**
Three words carry the whole verse, and the first of them is the important one. The judgement about these men is located in God's sight, and the locating is not decoration. A reader who has just been told that the notables of Makkah measured men by wealth and rank is given a verdict issued in a different jurisdiction, and the difference between the two is the verse's argument.

The phrase also closes off an objection. If the three men are among the finest, someone might ask: by whose standard? The verse answers before the question is asked. Not by the standard of their century, not by the estimation of their peoples, and not by the reader's. In Our sight — and the sight is the only one that will still be operating when the others have closed.

The same formula governs the chapter's earlier portraits without being stated. David's nearness, Solomon's nearness, Job's commendation as an excellent servant — each was a verdict issued from the same place, and each was issued over the head of an audience that would not have agreed. The verse makes the jurisdiction explicit for the first time.

**Chosen and Finest, and Election That Is Not Exemption**
Two descriptions are given, and they are not synonyms. The first denotes being selected or set apart, which is a relation: these men were chosen rather than having chosen. The second denotes being among the best, which is a quality: they occupy a rank within a class.

The pairing covers both aspects of their standing. Selection is not a statement about merit, since a thing can be selected for reasons belonging to the selector; excellence is a statement about the thing itself. The verse asserts both, and in doing so it refuses the two easy reductions — that the prophets were merely appointed, or that they merely happened to be outstanding. They were appointed, and they were excellent, and the two facts stand together without either explaining the other.

The verse is a high commendation, and the chapter is careful to place it where it cannot be misused. Nothing in the two preceding verses exempts these men from trial, and nothing in the surrounding portraits exempts anyone from accountability. The men are the finest, and they were also tested, and the two facts are held together without tension.

That holding is the answer to a common misunderstanding of election. To be chosen is not to be removed from the conditions of the choice; it is to be placed inside them with a heavier weight. The chapter has shown what the weight does to a man who rules, a man who owns and a man who has lost everything, and it now names three more who carried it.

**Why the Verse Follows the Last**
The previous verse said what the three men were chosen for; this one says what they are. The order is deliberate, and it prevents a misreading of the previous verse. A reader could conclude that the remembrance of the Abode was simply the job description, with no bearing on the men's standing. This verse removes that conclusion.

The connection is also a model of how the chapter describes its servants throughout. Each portrait states a function and then a verdict: David was made an authority on earth and will have nearness; Solomon was given a kingdom and will have nearness; Job was found patient and is an excellent servant. The function comes first and the verdict second, and the pattern reaches its most compressed form here, in a single sentence about three men at once.

**A Rank Given Without Comparison**
The verse does not say that these three are better than the others named in the chapter, and the silence is meaningful. David, Solomon and Job each received an individual verdict; these three receive a collective one, and the collective form makes comparison impossible.

The chapter is not building a hierarchy. It is building a class, and the class is defined by the qualities named in the previous verse — strength in worship, insight into the unseen, remembrance of the Abode. Anyone who meets the definition belongs to it, and the verse's refusal to rank its members is an invitation rather than an exclusion.

The Qur'an elsewhere warns against precisely the habit of ranking prophets, and the warning is consistent with this verse's method. What matters in the Book is not which prophet was greater but what each of them was for, and the chapter has answered that question for six of them in the same terms.

**The Verse That Prepares the Destinations**
Structurally this is the last verse about people before the chapter turns to places. The next verse adds three more names, and then the sūrah describes the honourable destination of the righteous and the evil destination of the transgressors. The verdict here is the bridge between the two halves.

The bridge is a claim about what survives. Wealth, kingdoms, health and family are all described in the preceding verses, and every one of them is temporary. The verdict *in Our sight* is the only thing in the chapter that is not subject to time, and the chapter places it immediately before describing where the two groups end up.

The placement also gives the descriptions that follow their meaning. A garden and a fire are only destinations if something arrives at them, and the verse has just said what arrives: a standing, fixed in a sight that does not change. The reader is told who the travellers are before he is shown the two places they travel to.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:48

> Also remember Ishmael, Elisha, and Ⱬul-Kifl. All are among the best

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ishmael, and Three More Names Under One Description**
The verse adds three prophets to the three named before it, and it describes all three with a single phrase: each is among the best. No individual characterisation is given, no incident is recalled, and no distinction is drawn between them. The verse is a list with a verdict attached.

The economy is the point. After six named prophets, the chapter has established what it means by excellence, and it no longer needs to explain the term. A reader who has followed the chapter knows that *among the best* refers to the qualities named in verse 45 and the orientation named in verse 46, and the phrase can therefore be applied to three men about whom the chapter says nothing else.

The first name is the most familiar to the chapter's original audience, and the familiarity is why he is placed first. Ishmael is the ancestor of the Arabs and the son of Abraham, named in the previous verse; his story is told in al-Baqarah, where he and his father raise the foundations of the House (2:125–128), and he appears throughout the Qur'an as a man of his word and a prophet.

His inclusion here does two things at once. It completes the genealogy begun in the previous verse — father, son, grandson, and now the other son — and it places the ancestor of the audience inside the class being described. The men of Makkah claimed descent from Ishmael, and the verse tells them what the claim requires.

**Elisha, Mentioned Twice in the Whole Book**
The second name is al-Yasaʿ, and it occurs only here and in al-Anʿām, where he is listed with Ishmael, Jonah and Lot among those favoured above the worlds: *“˹We also guided˺ Ishmael, Elisha, Jonah, and Lot, favouring each over other people ˹of their time˺.”* (6:86). No narrative is attached to him anywhere in the Qur'an.

The identification is with the Elisha of 2 Kings, the disciple of Elijah who succeeded him as a prophet in Israel. The Qur'an's silence about his story is not a gap but a characteristic of its method: not every prophet is narrated, and the naming alone is enough to place a man in the line. The reader is told that he existed, that he was a prophet, and that he was among the best.

**Ⱬul-Kifl: A Title Rather Than a Name**
The third figure is the most obscure in the Qur'an, and the obscurity is built into the name. Ⱬul-Kifl means the possessor of a portion or share, and the word kifl is used elsewhere of a double portion of mercy: *“˹And˺ He will grant you a double share of His mercy”* (57:28). The title has been read as describing a man who bore a double share of prophetic labour and reward.

The same root lies behind the verb meaning to assume responsibility or to pledge, and the commentators developed the reading accordingly: a man who pledged himself to a prophet, or who pledged to fast by day, pray by night and judge without anger. Attempts to identify him with a known prophet — Elijah, Joshua, Zechariah, Ezekiel — rest on these etymologies rather than on evidence, and the majority of commentators hold that he was not a prophet at all but a righteous man. Al-Anbiyāʾ groups him with Ishmael and Enoch as men who were steadfast (21:85), which is the most the texts commit to.

**Why an Obscure Figure Is Included**
The inclusion of Ⱬul-Kifl in a list of prophets is deliberate and instructive. Every other name in the chapter is familiar to the reader; this one is not, and no story is attached to it anywhere in the Book. A man known only by a title and a verdict stands beside Ishmael and Elisha without any explanation.

The effect on the reader is exact. If a man about whom nothing is recorded can be described as among the best, then the class is not confined to the famous. The chapter has been assembling witnesses, and this one is a witness to the possibility of excellence without renown.

The witness is needed because of who the chapter is arguing with. The notables measured a messenger by his standing in Makkah and found him insufficient (38:4). The verse answers by placing a man with no recorded standing in the same list as the patriarchs and calling him one of the best. The list is the argument, and the obscure name is its most useful member.

**"All Are Among the Best"**
The closing phrase is a collective verdict, and it is the same verdict given to the three named in the previous verse. Six prophets are now described in the same terms, and the descriptions differ in no respect.

That uniformity is the chapter's summary of its portraits. Three men were narrated in detail and three were listed in a phrase, and the verdict is identical. What separates them in the text is the amount of space they are given, not their standing, and the amount of space is a matter of what the audience needed to hear.

The phrase also carries a comparison that is left unstated. Among the best implies a class with members, and the chapter has spent its first sixteen verses describing people who are not in it — the arrogant, the oppositional, the chiefs who canvassed for their gods. The verdict is inclusive in one direction and exclusive in the other, and it makes both statements in four words.

**A Roster That Ends With a Reader**
The verse closes the chapter's list of people, and the following verse turns to destinations. Six names, three with stories and three without, and one verdict applied to all of them.

The turn is prepared by the last name in the list. A man known only by a title and a description stands as the final witness, and he is the one a reader can most easily imagine himself beside. The chapter is about to describe where two groups of people end up, and it has just shown that the criterion for belonging to the better group is not fame.

That placement does real work. A reader arriving at the description of the two destinations has just been given a roster in which the last name carries no history at all. The destinations are then assigned by a quality the reader has been shown is independent of renown, and the ordering makes the assignment harder to dismiss.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:49

> This is ˹all˺ a reminder. And the righteous will certainly have an honourable destination

**Expanded Commentary**

**"This Is a Reminder"**
The verse opens by naming what the chapter has been. Three readings of the demonstrative are available, and each is defensible. It can refer to the Qur'an as a whole: this Book is a reminder. It can refer to the narratives just completed: these stories are a reminder. And it can refer to the sūrah itself, at the point where its second half begins.

The three readings are not rivals, because the Qur'an uses the same word — dhikr — in all three senses, and the opening verse of this chapter swore by the Book as one *full of reminders* (38:1). The verse therefore closes a circle that was opened forty-eight verses earlier, and it closes it with the same noun.

Calling the material a reminder also makes a claim about the audience. A reminder addresses people who already know what they are being reminded of, and the chapter has been arguing exactly that: the message is not new, the prophets are not new, and the demand for one God is the oldest demand on record. The verse names the genre of everything that has preceded it, and the genre determines how the reader should receive it.

**A Promise Given to a Class, and the Destination Before the Description**
The second half of the verse repeats, almost exactly, the promise made to David in verse 25 and to Solomon in verse 40. An honourable destination — the same two words, the same construction, now applied not to an individual but to the righteous as a group.

The generalisation is the most important move in the verse. What was given to two prophets as a personal promise is now stated as the standing outcome for a category of people, and the category is defined by conduct rather than by office. The righteous are not prophets; they are those who believed and did good, and the destination promised to the greatest servants of God is promised to them without qualification.

The verse promises the destination and then the following verses describe it, and the order is worth noting. A reader is told first that there is an honourable destination for the righteous, and only then is shown what it contains. The promise precedes the particulars.

That order protects the description from being read as an inducement. If the Garden were described first and the promise attached at the end, the account would function as an advertisement. Placed the other way round, the particulars illustrate a promise that has already been made on other grounds — the grounds established in verses 27 and 28, that creation has a purpose and that the righteous and the wicked cannot receive the same end.

**An Honourable Destination, a Third Time**
The phrase has now appeared three times in one chapter, and the repetition does specific work. The first use followed a repentance; the second followed a trial and a restoration; the third follows a list of six prophets and precedes a description of the Garden.

By the third occurrence the phrase has become a technical term within the sūrah, and its meaning is fixed by the two cases that came before it. It is what a servant receives at the end, it is not diminished by what he did in the middle, and it is described in relation to God rather than in relation to any creature. The reader arrives at the description of the Garden already knowing what the destination is worth.

The repetition also prepares the contrast that follows in verse 55, where the transgressors are given the exact opposite phrase: an evil destination. The two formulas are matched word for word, and the matching is what makes the chapter's ending an argument rather than a pair of pictures.

**The Hinge Between People and Places**
Structurally the verse is a hinge, and its two halves face in different directions. The first half looks back over everything the chapter has narrated — the argument with the objectors, the histories of the destroyed, the three portraits, the two lists. The second half looks forward to the six verses describing the Garden and the four describing the Fire.

The hinge is a single word, and the word is the chapter's own name for itself. A reminder is something that calls to mind what is already known, and the chapter is claiming that everything in it has been a calling to mind rather than a disclosure of novelty. What follows is not new information but the consequence of what has already been recalled.

**A Verse That Answers the Chapter's First Objection**
The men who opened this sūrah objected that a reminder had come to one man out of all of them. This verse answers the objection in its final form. The reminder is here, it has been given, and it is now being applied: the righteous have a destination, and the destination is not allocated by rank.

The answer is not an argument but a statement of fact, and the statement is the chapter's last word on the dispute. Everything after it is description, and the description belongs to the two groups rather than to the argument between them.

The verse also disposes of the question about the treasuries of mercy that occupied verses 9 and 10. Those verses asked who controls the distribution of God's gifts; this verse shows the distribution in operation, and it shows that the criterion applied is conduct. Nobody in Makkah controlled it, and nobody in Makkah is excluded from it except by his own doing.

That is the whole of the chapter's reply to its opening objection. The notables asked whether they held the treasuries; the verse answers that the treasuries are open and that the gate is conduct. The answer required no argument, and the chapter offered none.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:50

> the Gardens of Eternity, whose gates will be open for them

**Expanded Commentary**

**Gardens of Eternity, and Arrival Without an Obstacle**
The destination is named, and the name carries two claims. The first is plurality: gardens, not a garden. The Qur'an speaks of Paradise as containing gardens of different kinds, and the same title for the abode recurs across many sūrahs, from al-Tawbah to Ghāfir. The second claim is permanence: the gardens are gardens of Eden, of abiding, and the word denotes a settled dwelling rather than a visit.

The combination answers the two objections a reader might raise about any promised reward. Plurality answers the fear of monotony; permanence answers the fear of loss. The chapter has spent its first half describing possessions that could be taken — a throne, a kingdom, a family, a herd of horses — and it now describes a possession defined by the fact that it cannot be.

The scene the verse describes has no barrier in it, and the absence is deliberate. Elsewhere the Qur'an describes the arrival of the other group in terms of doors that close and a keeper who reproaches, and the contrast between the two arrivals is one of the chapter's central devices.

Raʿd gives the believers' arrival in fuller form, and the fullness makes the point: they enter the Gardens of Eternity together with the righteous of their parents, spouses and descendants, and the angels come to them from every gate with a greeting of peace for their perseverance (13:23–24). Every element of that scene — company, greeting, welcome from every direction — is the opposite of what the chapter will describe in verses 55 to 64.

**Gates Already Open**
The second clause is the more striking of the two, and its detail is precise: the gates will be open for them. Not opened on their arrival, not opened by request, but open. Al-Zumar describes the same arrival with the same emphasis: the mindful are led to the Garden in groups, and when they reach it its gates are found already open, and its keepers greet them with peace and tell them to enter and stay forever (39:73).

The openness is not a convenience but a statement. A gate that must be opened implies a decision made at the moment of arrival; a gate already open implies that the decision was made earlier and is not in question. The verse places the judgement behind the arrival, and the arrival is therefore not a hearing but an entry.

**The Eight Gates and What They Imply**
The tradition supplies a detail that the verse assumes, and it is recorded in the most authoritative collections. The Prophet ﷺ said that Paradise has eight gates, and that each is named for a deed: the one who belonged to the people of prayer is called through the gate of prayer, the faster through the gate of fasting, the giver of charity through the gate of charity, and the one who strove through the gate of striving (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1897; Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 1027). Another report names one of the eight al-Rayyān, and says that none enter by it but those who fasted (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 3257).

The reports explain what the plurality of gardens implies: the reward is differentiated by the deed. A person is not admitted through a general entrance but called by name from the door that corresponds to what he actually did. The verse's open gates are therefore not eight identical openings but eight recognitions.

**What the Open Gates Answer**
The chapter has been concerned throughout with who is admitted and on what grounds. The notables of Makkah objected that the reminder had been given to the wrong man, and the whole sūrah has been answering the objection by describing what God gives and to whom. This verse is the final answer, and it is given in an image rather than an argument.

Nobody stands at these gates deciding who belongs. The gates are open, and the people who arrive are the people who were expected. The chapter's dispute about rank ends in a scene where rank is not mentioned, and the only thing that determines which door a person is called from is what he did.

The same image is turned against the other group later in the Book, and the turning sharpens both. The Fire is described as having seven gates, with a portion assigned to each (15:44), and the difference between assigned portions and open doors is the difference between the two destinations described in this chapter.

**A Description Given in Two Details**
The verse is short, and it gives only two facts: gardens, and open gates. Everything else — the reclining, the fruit, the drink, the companions — comes in the following verses, one detail at a time. The method is characteristic of the Qur'an's descriptions of the Garden, which are built from particulars rather than from generalities.

The two particulars chosen here are the setting and the entry, and both are structural rather than sensual. Before the reader is told what the Garden contains, he is told that it is permanent and that he can get in. The chapter supplies the assurance before the inventory.

That ordering is also an answer to the chapter's opening audience. The men of Makkah asked whether they possessed the treasuries of mercy and the kingdom of the heavens and the earth (38:9–10). The verse answers with a kingdom whose gates stand open to anyone who qualifies, and whose qualification is stated in the previous verse in two words. The treasuries they claimed to hold are shown to be something else entirely, and the doors to the real one are not locked.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:51

> There they will recline, calling for abundant fruit and drink

**Expanded Commentary**

**Reclining: A Posture With a Meaning**
The verse begins with a bodily position, and in the world that produced this text the position carries information. Reclining at a meal was not the ordinary posture of eating; it was the posture of a host at leisure, of a man who has finished his work and is being served. To say that the inhabitants of the Garden recline is to say that nothing is required of them.

The Qur'an uses the same image repeatedly and consistently. The inhabitants are described as reclining on thrones lined up and facing one another (52:20), as facing each other on thrones (37:44), and as resting in shade with their spouses on canopied couches (36:56). The repetition is not decorative. A posture repeated across many sūrahs is a claim about the condition of the people described, and the claim is that the labour of life has ended.

The posture also carries a social meaning that the first audience would have caught at once. In their world, the man who reclined was the man who had no one above him at that moment, and the servants were standing. The verse places every inhabitant of the Garden in the reclining position, which means it places none of them in the standing one.

**Calling, Not Fetching**
The second detail is more informative than it looks. The inhabitants call for fruit and drink, and the calling is the whole of their contribution. They do not rise, do not go, do not carry and do not prepare. The desire is expressed and the thing arrives.

The Qur'an states the same arrangement elsewhere with equal plainness: *“And We will ˹continually˺ provide them with whatever fruit or meat they desire.”* (52:22). The provision is continuous and the desire is the only mechanism required. The verse is describing a state in which the gap between wanting and having has closed, and the closing of that gap is what the word *provision* in the next verse will formalise.

The calling is also a social act, and the chapter is aware of it. A person who calls for something is speaking to someone, and the scene therefore contains speech that is not argument, negotiation or defence. The Qur'an notes elsewhere that the drink of the Garden leads to no idle talk and no incitement to sin (52:23), which describes the same conversation from the other side.

**Abundant Fruit**
The fruit is described with a word denoting abundance — much fruit, fruit without stint. The quantity matters because the experience of the world the audience knew was one of scarcity. Fruit in the Hijaz was seasonal, expensive and perishable, and a promise of unlimited fruit was not a vague image of luxury but a specific reversal of a known condition.

Muḥammad's description of the Garden elsewhere in the Qur'an is equally concrete: rivers of fresh water, of milk that does not change in taste, of wine delicious to drink and of pure honey, with all kinds of fruit and forgiveness from their Lord (47:15). The pairing of fruit with forgiveness in that verse is instructive — the material and the spiritual provision are listed together, as if both were equally part of the promise.

**A Scene of Effortlessness, and Drink Without Its Consequences**
The drink is named without qualification here, and the qualification is supplied elsewhere. Al-Ṭūr describes the cup that passes between them as containing no idle talk and no incitement to sin (52:23), which identifies what the drink is by naming what it lacks. The pleasures of the Garden are given without the harms that accompany their counterparts in this world.

The distinction is theologically careful. The Qur'an prohibits wine in this life and promises drink in the next, and the difference is not a contradiction but a description: what is promised is the pleasure without the damage. A reader who knows what drink does to a person in this world will understand what the description is claiming, and the claim is the point of the verse.

Every element of the verse is chosen to remove effort. The inhabitants are already reclining; they call rather than go; the fruit is abundant so no one need economise; the drink arrives without consequence. Nothing in the scene requires exertion, planning or restraint.

That removal is the answer to the chapter's earlier portraits, in which effort was the whole of the matter. David had to judge, Solomon had to return, Job had to strike the ground with his foot. The verse describes the end of all three efforts, and it describes it in the smallest possible terms — a posture, a call, an arrival.

**Why the Description Is So Plain**
The verse could have been ornate, and the Qur'an's descriptions of the Garden elsewhere are richer. Here the description is reduced to three elements and stated in ordinary language, and the plainness suits its position in the chapter.

The sūrah has spent its first half arguing with men who measured everything in terms of possession and rank. The description given to them is not an argument but a picture, and the picture is of people who have stopped acquiring. The verse's plainness is what makes it legible to the audience it was revealed to, and the audience is the reason it is plain.

There is a further reason, and it belongs to the chapter's argument. A description that lavished detail on luxury would confirm the very values the sūrah has been attacking — that the good is the abundant and the impressive. This verse describes the good as effortless, and effortlessness is not something the chiefs of Makkah were in the habit of praising. The plainness is therefore not a lack of material but a choice of values, made in the description itself.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:52

> And with them will be maidens of modest gaze and equal age

**Expanded Commentary**

**Companions, Not Attendants**

The verse adds a third element to the scene, and its grammar places the companions alongside the inhabitants rather than below them. The phrase is *and with them*, which is the language of company rather than of service. Nobody in the scene is described as attending on anybody else, and the absence of service is consistent with the previous verse, in which the fruit and drink arrive without anyone fetching them.

The Qur'an's descriptions of the Garden elsewhere use the same construction. The inhabitants recline with their spouses in shade (36:56), and they are paired with companions of a particular description (52:20; 44:54). The pairing is presented as part of the reward rather than as an addition to it, and the verse here follows that pattern exactly.

The construction also answers a question the description might otherwise raise. If everything arrives at a call, is anyone needed? The verse answers that the provision is not the point of the scene, and that a place in which nothing is lacking would still be incomplete without company.

**"Of Modest Gaze"**

The first quality named is a restraint of the eyes, and the commentators explain it as looking at no one but one's own companion. The gaze is limited by choice rather than by prohibition, and the limitation is what makes the company exclusive.

The same description appears in al-Raḥmān with an addition that sharpens it: *“In both ˹Gardens˺ will be maidens of modest gaze, who no human or jinn has ever touched before.”* (55:56). The addition identifies the restraint as fidelity rather than shyness, and it makes the companionship described here a relation of mutual exclusivity. The verse's four words carry that content, and the Qur'an's other uses of the phrase confirm it.

The emphasis on the eyes is also an emphasis on attention, which is the subject the chapter has been circling since the horse review. Solomon was undone for a moment by what he looked at; the companions of the Garden are described by what they look at and by the fact that they look nowhere else. The chapter treats the direction of the gaze as a moral fact, and it treats it the same way in both places.

**"Equal Age"**

The second quality is a matching in years, and the word denotes those of one age or of equal maturity. The detail has a specific function: it removes from the scene any relation of dependency or of unequal standing. Companions of equal age are peers, and the description is a description of peers.

Al-Nabaʾ states the same quality in similar terms, describing companions of equal age (78:33), and al-Wāqiʿah describes them with a different attribute altogether (56:22). The variation across sūrahs suggests that the Qur'an is not building a single fixed portrait but naming qualities that matter: fidelity, compatibility and equality. This verse names two of the three.

The pairing of the two qualities is worth noting as well. Fidelity without equality would describe a servant; equality without fidelity would describe a stranger. The verse gives both, and the combination describes a companion in the full sense — someone who stays and someone who stands beside.

**Why the Detail Is Given**

A description of the afterlife that included no human company would be incomplete, and the reason is not difficult to find. Loneliness is a form of suffering, and a reward that removed every hardship but left a person alone would not be a complete reward. The verse supplies the missing element in the shortest possible form.

The detail also answers an expectation in the audience. The men addressed by this sūrah understood honour in terms of household and company, and the verse describes the household of the Garden in terms they recognised, while removing from it the features that made households in this world sites of conflict. No rivalry, no inequality, no ageing and no loss.

**The Verse in Its Place in the Sequence**

Three verses describe the Garden, and each adds one element. The first gives the setting and the entry; the second gives the food and drink; the third gives the company. The sequence moves from place to provision to persons, and the last of the three is the shortest.

The order is instructive. A description that began with the companions would read as an advertisement; a description that ends with them reads as a completion. The verse is placed last because company is what makes the rest of the description intelligible as a life rather than as a supply.

**What the Description Does Not Do**

The verse does not describe beauty, does not quantify, and does not elaborate. Two qualities are named and the description stops. The restraint is characteristic of this chapter's treatment of the Garden, which is consistently plainer than the Qur'an's treatment of it elsewhere.

The plainness has an effect on the reader. A description that dwells on physical detail invites the imagination to linger on the detail; a description that names two qualities and stops directs the imagination toward the relation. The verse chooses the second course, and the choice is consistent with everything the chapter has said about what the destination consists in.

The restraint also protects the verse from being used as an inducement. A reader who is offered company as a prize will weigh the prize; a reader who is told that the inhabitants of the Garden are accompanied by faithful equals is being given a description of a state rather than a price. The chapter has been arguing from its second verse that the objectors mistook what God gives for something that can be competed over, and this verse declines to feed that mistake.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:53

> This is what you are promised for the Day of Reckoning

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Promise Already Given, and the Tie to the Argument**
The verse does not introduce the description that has just been given; it identifies it. The gardens, the open gates, the reclining, the fruit, the drink and the companions are named as the content of a promise, and the promise is treated as something the audience already possesses. Nothing here is being offered for the first time.

The identification matters because of who is being addressed. The men of Makkah had heard this promise before, in other sūrahs and from the same mouth, and the verse tells them that what they have just heard is not new material but the same material restated. The chapter's whole method has been reminder rather than disclosure, and the verse says so about its own description of the Garden.

Structurally this verse performs a function that is easy to miss. The description of the Garden could have stood alone as an appendix to the chapter's argument; the verse attaches it to the argument instead. What is described is what was promised, and the promise was made in the context of the dispute the chapter has been conducting since its second verse.

The attachment answers a specific objection. The notables of Makkah treated the Prophet's message as an invention, and an invention would be a new claim. The verse describes the Garden as the fulfilment of an existing promise, which places the whole of the message inside a continuity the objectors could not deny. The Garden is not a novelty; it is the delivery of something already undertaken.

**"For the Day of Reckoning"**
The phrase attaches the promise to an event, and the attachment is the verse's argument. The Garden is not a reward distributed at intervals during life; it is what is promised for the Day of Reckoning, and the Day has been named twice already in this chapter — once in the mockery of the pagans (38:16) and once in the charge to David (38:26).

The three occurrences form a sequence. The pagans asked to have their portion before the Day; David was warned that those who neglect the Day will be punished severely; and here the Day is named as the occasion on which the promise is delivered. The same event is the object of mockery, the ground of warning and the date of fulfilment, and the verse supplies the third of those functions.

**Addressed to "You"**
The promise is addressed in the second person, and the shift is significant. The previous verses described the righteous in the third person — the righteous will have, they will recline, with them will be. This verse turns and speaks to the audience: this is what *you* are promised.

The turn opens the promise to the hearer without lowering its terms. The description has been given objectively, and the address now invites the reader to place himself inside it. Whether he belongs there is not stated, and the omission is deliberate: the verse makes the promise available and leaves the qualification to the conduct it has already described.

The second person also does something to the reader's position. A third-person description can be observed; a second-person promise must be accepted or refused. The verse removes the possibility of reading the account as information about other people, and it does so at the exact point where the description has become most attractive.

**A Promise and Its Date**
Every promise has a date, and the verse supplies the date. The effect is to convert a description into an obligation. What has been described is not a possibility or a hope but a commitment with an appointed occasion attached to it, and the occasion is the one the chapter has already said cannot be postponed.

The pairing of promise and date also answers the mockery of verse 16. The pagans asked to have their portion before the Day of Reckoning, and the verse replies by placing both portions — the Garden and, in the verses that follow, the Fire — on the same date. Neither is available early, and neither can be deferred.

The date also does something to the reader's relationship to the description. A reward that could arrive at any moment would be watched for; a reward attached to a fixed occasion allows the intervening time to be lived. The chapter has already told its audience that the interval is the only part of the story anyone can act on, and this verse fixes the far end of the interval.

There is a further implication in naming the Day of Reckoning as the occasion. A reckoning is an accounting, and an accounting requires records. The verse therefore ties the promise to the same process by which the transgressors will be judged, which means the two outcomes described in this chapter are produced by one procedure rather than by two separate decisions.

**The Shortest Verse Carrying the Most Weight**
Six words in translation, and they do the work of a conclusion. The verse could have been expanded into a restatement of the terms of the promise; instead it simply names the promise and its date and moves on.

The economy is the chapter's habit at its turning points. The verdict on the three patriarchs was given in a single sentence (38:47), the verdict on the three lesser-known prophets in another (38:48), and the identification of the Garden as a promise in this one. The chapter reserves its elaboration for its arguments and states its conclusions flatly.

The flatness has a purpose beyond style. A promise stated at length can be negotiated; a promise stated in one line can only be received. The verse gives the reader no room to bargain over the terms, and the next verse confirms that the supply behind the promise is inexhaustible.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:54

> This is indeed Our provision that will never end

**Expanded Commentary**

**Provision, Not Reward**
The verse names what has been described, and the name it chooses is unexpected. Not a reward, not a recompense, not a wage — a provision. The word denotes what is supplied for the sustenance of a living being, the regular allotment on which a life depends, and its use here places the Garden in the category of food rather than of payment.

The choice is theologically deliberate. A reward is proportionate to a deed, and a proportionate payment can be calculated and therefore limited. A provision is supplied according to need, and the inhabitants of the Garden have needs that do not cease. The verse is saying that what has been described is not a settlement of accounts but a continuing supply, and the continuing character is stated in the second half of the verse.

The word also links the Garden to the ordinary life of the reader. Provision is what a person receives every day and what he cannot do without, and describing the Garden in those terms makes it intelligible as a life rather than as a prize. The inhabitants do not collect a payment and leave; they are fed, and the feeding goes on.

**"That Will Never End"**
The claim is stated flatly and without any qualification whatsoever. The provision does not diminish, does not run out and is not withdrawn. Al-Naḥl makes the same contrast with the provision of this world in a single sentence: *“Whatever you have will end, but whatever Allah has is everlasting. And We will certainly reward the steadfast according to the best of their deeds.”* (16:96).

The contrast is the whole point of the verse. Everything the chapter has described so far — a throne, a kingdom, a herd of horses, a family, a spring of cool water — was temporary, and the temporariness was not incidental to those accounts but central to them. The verse now describes a provision defined by the absence of that feature, and the definition answers every loss narrated earlier in the sūrah.

Raʿd makes the same point in the language of the Garden itself: eternal is its fruit as well as its shade, and that is the outcome for the righteous while the outcome for the disbelievers is the Fire (13:35). The eternity is predicated of the fruit and the shade as well as of the place, which is precisely what this verse claims — the supply and not merely the location is without end.

**The Word "Indeed"**
The verse carries an emphasis particle, and the emphasis is directed at a specific doubt. A reader who has heard promises before knows that promises can fail, that supplies run out and that the giver can change his mind. The particle asserts that none of those possibilities applies here.

The emphasis is also a response to the chapter's audience. The men of Makkah had been told about the Garden for years and had treated the telling as invention. The verse does not argue with them; it emphasises the claim, which is a different rhetorical act. An argument invites examination; an emphasis invites belief, and the verse chooses the second.

The choice is consistent with the chapter's method at this point. Its argumentative work was completed in the first sixteen verses, and everything since has been presentation. A reader who was not convinced by the argument will not be convinced by the description either, and the verse knows it: it does not attempt further persuasion, it confirms what has been promised and leaves the reader with the confirmation.

**Why Provision Is the Right Word, and the Contrast It Opens**
The word connects this verse to the previous one through a shared logic. A promise is a commitment to give; a provision is the giving. The two verses together describe the Garden as both committed and supplied, and the combination removes the gap between undertaking and delivery that makes human promises unreliable.

The connection also answers a question about the Day of Reckoning. If the Garden is delivered on a date, is it delivered once and then finished? The verse says no: the provision continues. The date is when the supply begins, not when it is exhausted, and the distinction is what separates the Garden from every reward given in this world.

The verse ends the description of one destination, and the verse that follows begins the description of the other. The two are matched in structure and opposite in content, and the matching is deliberate: the chapter is not presenting two unrelated pictures but two outcomes of the same process.

The provision that never ends stands beside the punishment that does not cease, and both are described in the same register of plain statement. The chapter refuses to soften either, and the refusal is what gives its earlier portraits their seriousness. A reader who has been shown both accounts in the same tone cannot dismiss one of them as rhetoric.

**A Verse That Closes the Description of the Garden**
Six verses have described the honourable destination, and this is the last of them. The description ends not with an image but with a claim about duration, and the ending is characteristic of the chapter: the particulars are given, and then the principle is stated.

The principle is that what God gives does not run out. Everything in the chapter's portraits was a loan with a term — a kingdom that could be stolen, a family that could be lost, a body that could be diseased. The verse places beside all of them a provision with no term, and the placement is the chapter's answer to the fear of loss that runs through every one of its stories.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:55

> That is that. And the transgressors will certainly have the worst destination

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn to the Other Destination, and Its Closing Formula**
The verse opens with a phrase that closes an account, and the closing is emphatic. What has been described is settled; no further description is needed and no revision is possible. The formula is used in Arabic to end a matter that has been fully stated, and its use here signals that the description of the Garden is complete.
