The same formula appeared at the opening of verse 57 in the parallel account of the Fire, and the two occurrences frame the description of the punishment in the same way. The chapter uses the formula twice, and both uses mark a transition between what has been established and what is about to be stated.

The second half of the verse turns without any connective of contrast. There is no *but* and no *however*; the sentence simply moves from the destination of the righteous to the destination of the transgressors, and the abruptness of the move is the point.

The two destinations are not alternatives presented for choice at this point in the chapter. The choice was made in the conduct the chapter has described across its whole length, and the verse reports the two outcomes as settled facts. The reader is not being offered the second destination; he is being told that it exists and who it is for.

**"The Transgressors"**
The word used denotes those who exceed a limit, and the exceeding is the defining feature. It is not a term for a particular sin but for a manner of acting — going past what has been set. The Qur'an uses it of those who disobey God and oppose His messengers, and the chapter has been describing exactly such people since its second verse.

The term also connects this verse to the earlier descriptions of the destroyed peoples. The parties of Noah, ʿĀd, Pharaoh, Thamūd, Lot's people and the residents of the Forest were all described with a single verb — they denied (38:14) — and the denial was an exceeding of a limit set by a messenger. The verse is naming the same people under a different heading, and the heading is what determines the destination.

The word also has a specific application to the audience of this sūrah. The men who demanded that their punishment be hastened (38:16) were exceeding a limit in the most literal sense: they were asking for the boundary between warning and seizure to be removed. The verse names the category that such a request belongs to, and it names the destination attached to it.

**"The Worst Destination"**
The phrase is the exact opposite of the one used three times for the righteous, and the opposition is word for word. An honourable destination for the righteous; the worst destination for the transgressors. The matching is not stylistic but argumentative: the chapter is asserting that the two outcomes are not merely different but opposed, and that no third outcome exists.

The word *worst* is a superlative, and it forecloses a comparison. The reader is not told that the destination is bad or severe; he is told that nothing worse exists. The claim is the counterpart of the previous verse's claim that the provision never ends, and the two superlatives frame the chapter's ending.

**Symmetry Between the Two Accounts**
The description of the righteous occupied six verses; the description of the transgressors will occupy the same number. The symmetry is exact, and it is not a coincidence of length. The chapter is asserting that the two accounts are of equal weight, and it demonstrates the assertion structurally.

The symmetry also answers an objection that arises whenever the Qur'an describes punishment. A reader might suppose that the description of the Garden is the substance and the description of the Fire an appendix. The chapter gives them equal space, and the equal space is a claim that both are equally real and equally relevant to the reader.

The symmetry extends below the level of verse counts. The Garden was entered through open gates; the Fire is entered by crowds being thrown in. The Garden contained provision without end; the Fire contains drink that does not relieve. The Garden ended with a greeting of peace; the Fire contains an exchange of curses. Each element of the first account has its counterpart in the second, and the counterparts are matched in order.

**Why the Turn Comes Here**
The placement of the turn is deliberate. The chapter has moved from argument to narrative to description, and the description of the Garden is its most attractive material. To place the other destination immediately after it is to refuse the reader the comfort of ending on the pleasant part.

The same method governs the whole sūrah. Every portrait of a servant is followed by the trial that tested him; every account of a gift is followed by the loss that revealed its terms. The chapter does not allow its reader to rest on any single description, and the turn in this verse is the clearest instance of the habit.

There is also a logical reason for the placement, and it belongs to the argument made in verse 28. The chapter asked whether God would treat those who believe and do good like those who spread corruption, and the question was rhetorical because the answer is obvious. The two descriptions that follow are the answer given in full, and the second of them is required for the first to mean anything. Without the worst destination there is no distinction, and without the distinction the whole of the chapter's moral argument collapses.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:56

> Hell, where they will burn. What an evil place to rest

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Place Named and a Verb Attached, a Rest That Is Not Rest**
The verse names the destination and describes what happens there in a single verb. The inhabitants burn, and the burning is given as a continuous state rather than an event. Nothing is said about how long it lasts, what precedes it or what follows it; the verse gives the place and the condition and stops.

The economy is striking after the six verses devoted to the Garden. The description of the honourable destination was built from particulars — gates, posture, fruit, drink, companions — and the description of the evil destination begins with two facts. The asymmetry in detail is not a lack of material; it is a statement about what the second place contains, which is less rather than more. A place with fewer particulars has fewer ways to be endured, and the chapter lets the shortness of the description carry that meaning without saying it.

The deepest claim in the verse is that the transgressors have been given the opposite of what they sought. The men described in the first verses of this sūrah wanted to be left alone with their gods, their standing and their way of life. What the verse describes is a place of permanent settlement in which nothing can be left behind.

The Qur'an's descriptions of the Fire elsewhere make the same point in different terms: no relief, no coolness, no death and no life. The verse here compresses all of it into the image of a bed that does not allow rest, and the compression is more effective than any list.

**Burning as the Basic Description**
The verb denotes entering a fire and being subjected to it, and the Qur'an uses it consistently for the punishment of the Fire. What matters here is that the description is physical and unqualified. No purpose is attached to the burning, no correction is promised and no limit is stated.

The absence of a purpose is worth noting, because punishment in the Qur'an is elsewhere described in terms that include warning and consequence. Here the description is reduced to the fact of the burning, and the reduction matches the reduction of the people described. The transgressors of the previous verse exceeded a limit; the verse describes what exceeding leads to, and it does so without commentary.

The contrast also answers the question the reader is likely to bring: is the punishment temporary? The verse does not answer directly, and the answer is given by the next verse instead, which describes the other torments of the same kind.

**"What an Evil Place to Rest"**
The closing exclamation uses a word that means a bed, a cradle or a place laid down for repose, and the choice is the sharpest element of the verse. A bed is where a person settles, and the verse says that this is the bed of the transgressors.

The word carries an irony that the chapter sets up deliberately. The righteous were described as reclining (38:51), and reclining is the posture of rest; the transgressors are given a bed, and the bed is a place of burning. The two descriptions use the language of repose, and only one of them describes repose. The verse's exclamation is the reader's cue to notice the inversion.

The inversion is also permanent. A bed implies staying, and the chapter has already described the Fire as a place to settle (38:56). What is given to the transgressors is not a punishment with a term but a dwelling with the shape of comfort and the substance of fire.

**The Superlative Applied Again**
The previous verse said that the transgressors would have the worst destination, and this verse supplies the destination and then exclaims over it. The exclamation is a second assertion of the same judgement, made in different words, and the doubling is emphatic.

The doubling also has a rhetorical function. A superlative stated once can be taken as a description; stated twice, in a statement and then in an exclamation, it becomes a verdict. The chapter has now given its verdict on both destinations, and it has given each in the same form — a promise and a warning, each followed by its own emphasis.

The exclamation also places the reader inside the judgement. A statement reports; an exclamation evaluates, and it invites the hearer to evaluate with it. The chapter has spent its first half refusing to argue with its objectors on their terms, and here it asks the reader to say something about the outcome — not to be persuaded by an argument but to recognise a fact about it.

**Why the Verse Is So Short**
The Garden received six verses and the Fire receives this one plus the four that follow, but the balance is misleading. The verses describing the Fire are shorter, and the shortest of them is this one. The chapter does not elaborate on the punishment, and the refusal to elaborate is itself a statement.

A description that dwelt on suffering would risk becoming spectacle. This verse names the place, states the condition and exclaims over the result, and then moves on to what the inhabitants are given to drink. The chapter treats the punishment as a fact to be reported rather than as a scene to be imagined.

There is a further reason for the brevity, and it belongs to the chapter's audience. The men of Makkah had asked to have their punishment hastened (38:16), which means they did not believe it existed. A reader in that position is not moved by graphic description; he is moved by certainty. The verse supplies certainty in the shortest form available, and it leaves the particulars to the verses that follow.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:57

> Let them then taste this: boiling water and ˹oozing˺ pus

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Let Them Taste": An Imperative of Retribution**

The verse opens with a command, and the command is addressed to no one in particular. Let them taste is the form Arabic uses for a retributive imperative — an order that expresses the justice of what is happening rather than directing anyone to perform it. The same formula appears in the Qur'an's descriptions of punishment elsewhere, and it always carries the sense of *this is what they earned*.

The verb of tasting is important. The punishment is not merely inflicted; it is experienced. A person can be punished without fully registering it, and the verse forecloses that possibility by using a verb of sensation. What is described is given to the senses, and the senses are the means by which it is known.

The formula also carries a note of contempt that the chapter has earned the right to use. These are the people who asked for their portion to be hastened (38:16), and the verse grants the request in the only terms in which it can now be granted. What they demanded is given to them, and the giving is called tasting.

**Boiling Water**

The first of the two drinks is described with a word denoting liquid at its maximum heat, and the Qur'an uses it repeatedly for the drink of the Fire (6:70; 37:67; 44:46; 56:42; 56:54; 88:5). Al-Ḥajj describes the application in a way that leaves nothing to the imagination: boiling water poured over the heads of those for whom garments of fire have been cut (22:19).

The choice of water is significant, because water is the universal object of thirst. The punishment is not an exotic substance but the ordinary remedy for thirst taken to the opposite extreme. A person who drinks it does not quench anything, and the drinking is repeated. The verse is describing a punishment shaped like a relief.

The shape is what makes it severe. A punishment that bore no resemblance to a good would be met by resistance; a punishment that arrives in the form of the thing wanted is met by hope, and the hope is disappointed each time. The Qur'an's account of the Fire is full of such deceptions, and this verse supplies the clearest of them.

**Ghassāq: The Cold That Cannot Be Borne**

The second drink is named with a word that occurs only twice in the Qur'an, here and in al-Nabaʾ, where it is paired with the same boiling water: *“There they will not taste any coolness or drink, except boiling water and ˹oozing˺ pus”* (78:24–25). The commentators explain it as a fluid so cold that it cannot be endured, and also as the discharge of the inhabitants of the Fire.

Both explanations are supported by the word's usage, and the pairing in al-Nabaʾ suggests that the cold reading is intended: the verse there says they will taste no coolness, and then names ghassāq as what they will taste instead. On that reading the second drink is the opposite of the first, and the opposition is the point of the pairing.

The rarity of the word is itself informative. A term used twice in the whole Book describes something with no counterpart in ordinary experience, and the verse does not explain it further. The reader is given a name and a temperature and left with both.

**Two Extremes, One Place**

The pairing of boiling water and freezing fluid in a single verse is a deliberate construction, and its purpose is to describe a place of extremes. There is no middle temperature in the Fire, no point of comfort between the two, and no escape from one into the other.

The construction answers a natural expectation. A person imagining punishment tends to imagine a single kind of suffering that could in principle be endured by becoming accustomed to it. The verse removes that possibility by making the two sufferings opposite: whatever a person becomes able to bear, the other is waiting. Al-Nabaʾ states the exclusion explicitly — no coolness and no drink — and this verse supplies the two substitutes.

**What the Pairing Demonstrates**

The two drinks also demonstrate something about the nature of the punishment that goes beyond physical description. Both are presented as provisions — things given to drink — and both fail to provide. The form of a gift is preserved while the content of the gift is removed, and the preservation is what makes the description so severe.

The same structure appears in the previous account of the Garden, inverted. There the provision is real and inexhaustible (38:54); here the provision is named and useless. The chapter has built its two descriptions as mirror images, and the mirroring is what allows it to be so brief.

**The Verse in the Sequence**

This verse follows the naming of the destination and precedes the statement that other punishments of the same kind await. Its position in the middle of a short sequence gives it the function of an example: the reader is shown one pair of punishments in detail and then told that the pattern continues.

The method is the same one the chapter used in its earlier lists. The destroyed peoples were named in a roll-call and the reason for their destruction was stated once; the punishments are described in one instance and their variety is asserted in the next verse. The chapter gives a sample and a generalisation, and leaves the multiplication to the reader.

The sample is also chosen for its correspondence to the other destination. The righteous were given fruit and drink that satisfy and never run out; the transgressors are given drink that resembles satisfaction and never delivers. The two accounts are built from the same materials, and the difference between them is the difference between a provision and a deception.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:58

> and other torments of the same sort

**Expanded Commentary**

**A List Left Open**
The verse does three words of work and does them deliberately. After naming two specific punishments, the Qur'an adds a category rather than a further item, and the category is open-ended. The reader is told that more exists and is not told what it is.

The openness has a purpose that a longer list would defeat. An enumerated catalogue can be surveyed, measured and eventually discounted; a category with unspecified members cannot. The verse asks the reader to do the multiplying, and a multiplication performed by the imagination always exceeds one performed by a text.

The method is also honest. A text that invented specific torments would be claiming knowledge it does not have; a text that says *others of the same kind* claims only what has been revealed. The verse keeps the description inside the limits of the revelation, and the limit is what gives the remaining silence its weight.

**"Of the Same Sort"**
The phrase that qualifies the addition is the most important element of the verse. The other torments are not merely additional; they are of the same kind, matched in pairs or in types. The word used denotes pairs and counterparts, and the commentators read it as indicating that the punishments come in matched sets.

The reading is confirmed by the verse before it. Boiling water and freezing fluid are a pair, and the pairing is what makes them unbearable — whichever a person becomes able to endure, its opposite remains. The verse is therefore saying that the pattern of the two drinks is repeated: each punishment has its counterpart, and the counterparts are what prevent adaptation.

The pairing also mirrors the structure of the world the inhabitants came from. Every choice they made had two sides, and they were told which side they were on. The verse describes a place in which the two sides arrive together, always, and neither can be taken without the other.

**Why the Pattern Matters More Than the Items**
A description that listed punishments would describe a set of events. A description that names a pattern describes a condition, and a condition has no end in the way an event does. The verse's insistence on likeness is an insistence on continuation.

The Qur'an makes the same point elsewhere in explicit terms. Al-Nabaʾ excludes relief by name: *“There they will not taste any coolness or drink, except boiling water and ˹oozing˺ pus”* (78:24–25). The exclusion of coolness is the exclusion of the counterpart that would help, and this verse generalises the exclusion: whatever is given, its counterpart is given with it.

The pattern also explains why the punishment is described as a destination rather than as a sentence. A sentence has a term; a condition does not. The chapter named the Fire as a place to settle (38:56), and a settled condition of matched opposites has no internal mechanism for ending.

**Brevity as Severity, and the False Comfort It Prevents**
There is a comfort available in any catalogue of suffering, and the verse removes it. A reader who is told that the punishment consists of two specific things can imagine enduring them, or can imagine that they will exhaust themselves. A reader told that other torments of the same sort exist cannot make either calculation.

The removal of that comfort is not cruelty but accuracy, on the chapter's own terms. The whole sūrah has argued that the transgressors' confidence rests on an estimate of their situation that is wrong, and the estimate is wrong because it is based on what they can currently see. The verse extends the same correction to the reader: what is described is a sample, not a total.

The verse is the shortest in the chapter's account of the Fire, and its brevity is not a sign that the material has run out. It is a refusal to elaborate, and the refusal is more severe than elaboration would be. A description that lingered would give the reader something definite to hold; a description that gestures and stops gives him nothing.

The chapter's treatment of the Fire throughout follows this pattern. The destination is named in one clause, the burning in one verb, the drinks in one line, and the remainder in three words. The economy is consistent, and it produces an effect that detailed description could not: the sense of a reality too large to be inventoried.

The economy also reflects the chapter's confidence. A writer who doubts his subject elaborates; one who does not states and moves on.

**What the Verse Contributes to the Chapter's Argument**
The chapter's argument from verse 27 onward has been that creation is not purposeless and that the righteous and the wicked cannot receive the same end. The descriptions of the two destinations are that argument given in concrete form, and this verse completes the second half of it.

The completion matters. If the punishment were described as two drinks and nothing more, a reader could conclude that the difference between the two destinations is one of degree. The verse removes that conclusion by making the punishment open-ended, and by making its open-endedness the same kind of fact as the inexhaustible provision of the Garden. Both are without limit, and the limits of neither are set by the people who occupy them.

That symmetry is the chapter's final word on the objection that opened it. The notables of Makkah asked whether they held the treasuries of mercy and the kingdom of the heavens and the earth. The answer is that neither destination is theirs to distribute, neither is measurable in their terms, and neither has an end they could calculate or shorten.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:59

> ˹The misleaders will say to one another,˺ “Here is a crowd ˹of followers˺ being thrown in with us. They are not welcome, ˹for˺ they ˹too˺ will burn in the Fire.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Crowd Rushing In**

The verse shifts from description to dialogue, and the first thing it reports is movement. A crowd is rushing in, and the word used conveys a pressing, hurried entry — the same verb the Qur'an uses elsewhere for a group entering a place in a mass. The arrival is not orderly and it is not chosen.

The contrast with the other destination is exact. The righteous arrive in successive groups at gates already open, and their keepers greet them with peace (39:73). This crowd is thrown in, and the people who are already there are the ones who notice it. The difference between arriving and being thrown is the difference between the two accounts, and the verse states it in a single word.

The crowd is also completely anonymous. No names, no tribes, no histories — a mass arriving and a mass objecting. The chapter stripped the individuals of identity in the same way it stripped the litigants before David, and the stripping serves the same purpose: a scene with no names can be recognised by anyone.

**"No Welcome": The Greeting Inverted**

The words spoken over the arriving crowd are a formula of non-welcome, and the formula is a deliberate inversion. In the usage of the Arabic the phrase is used to repel a visitor, and its ordinary sense is *you have no place here*. Spoken to people who are already being thrown in, it is both futile and revealing.

The futility is the point. The speakers cannot prevent the arrival, cannot alter their own situation and cannot help the newcomers. What they can do is refuse a greeting, and the refusal is the only act left to them. The Qur'an's descriptions of the Fire consistently show its inhabitants performing verbal acts in place of real ones, and this exchange is the first of them.

The inversion also inverts a duty. Hospitality was the highest social obligation in the society this sūrah was revealed to, and a refusal of welcome was among the gravest insults available. The verse places that refusal at the door of the Fire, spoken by the people who most needed the courtesy themselves.

**The Leaders Speaking First**

The speakers are identified as those who led, and the identification shapes everything that follows. These are the men who set the course for others — the chiefs, the notables, the ones whose opinion the crowd followed in the world. Their first recorded act in the Fire is to complain about the arrival of the people who followed them.

The complaint contains no repentance and no concern. The leaders do not express sorrow at having misled anyone; they express displeasure at the company. Al-Aʿrāf records the same scene with the same ordering: whenever a group enters the Fire it curses the group that came before it (7:38). The verse is describing a habit, not an incident.

The habit connects directly to the opening of the sūrah. The chiefs of Quraysh went out telling people to hold fast to their gods (38:6), and the verse shows the same men standing in the same relation with the same followers, saying something quite different. Leadership survives into the Fire; responsibility for it does not.

**What the Sentence Actually Says**

The content of the complaint is worth reading precisely. *They are not welcome, for they too will burn in the Fire.* The reason given for the unwelcome is not that the newcomers did anything to the speakers, but that the newcomers will suffer the same fate. The ground of the complaint is shared punishment.

The reasoning exposes something about the speakers. In the world they measured themselves against others and found themselves superior; in the Fire they measure themselves against others and find themselves equal. The equality is what they cannot bear, and the verse records the inability in their own words.

**The Contrast With the Greeting of the Garden**

The chapter has already described the greeting given to the righteous, and the two greetings are matched in structure and opposite in content. There: peace be upon you, you have done well, enter and stay (39:73). Here: no welcome, they will burn.

The matching is not accidental. The Qur'an repeatedly sets the two receptions against one another, and the setting is a claim about what a destination consists in. A place is known by how its inhabitants receive a newcomer, and the two receptions described in this chapter tell the reader everything about the two places.

**Why the Dialogue Begins Here**

The chapter's account of the Fire has so far been description: a place, a condition, two drinks, a category of others. The dialogue begins at the point where the description has exhausted the physical facts, and its function is to supply what description cannot — the social condition of the place.

That condition is the chapter's real subject in these verses. The transgressors were described at the start of the sūrah as arrogant and oppositional, and the arrogance and opposition are shown here in their final form: men who cannot welcome anyone, including the people they led.

The dialogue is also the chapter's answer to the slogan the chiefs carried through Makkah. *Carry on, and stand firm in devotion to your gods* (38:6) was an appeal to solidarity, and the verse shows what solidarity becomes when the gods have nothing to give. The group is intact, the loyalty is gone, and what remains is a crowd of people refusing each other.

The chiefs promised that holding together would protect them. The verse records the holding together and the absence of protection in the same scene.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:60

> The followers will respond, “No! You are not welcome! You brought this upon us. What an evil place for settlement!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer Comes Back**

The verse records the reply, and the reply is symmetrical with the statement it answers. The leaders said *no welcome*; the followers say *no, you are not welcome*. The same formula is returned, and the returning of it is the verse's structural point — an exchange in which nobody gives anything and everybody refuses.

The symmetry is not a stylistic device but a description. In the world these two groups had a relation of authority and obedience; in the Fire the relation has collapsed into a mutual refusal. The verse shows the collapse happening in a single exchange, and it shows that neither party has learned anything from it.

The refusal is also equal, and the equality is new. In the world the followers could not answer their chiefs; here the answer comes immediately and in the same words. The verse records the moment at which the hierarchy that organised the whole opposition to the Prophet ceases to exist, and it records it without comment.

**"You Brought This Upon Us"**

The substance of the reply is an accusation, and the accusation is the reason the exchange exists. The followers do not merely return the insult; they identify a cause. You brought this upon us — you prepared this place for us, or you sent it ahead of us, and we are here because of what you did.

The verb carries the sense of presenting or advancing something, and the image is of a gift delivered in advance. The leaders furnished the Fire for their followers by the direction they gave them in the world, and the followers name the furnishing at the moment of arrival. The accusation is accurate, and its accuracy changes nothing.

It changes nothing because the accusation is true and irrelevant at once. True, because the leaders did furnish it; irrelevant, because the followers walked. The verse lets both halves stand without resolving them, and the unresolved tension is the shape of the whole exchange.

**An Accusation That Has Already Been Answered**

The Qur'an elsewhere records the same accusation and the answer given to it. Al-Aʿrāf reports the followers saying that it was the leaders who misled them, and asks for a doubled punishment for them; the reply is that the punishment has already been doubled for all of them, though they do not know it (7:38). The doubling applies to both groups, and the reason is that each bears its own burden as well as a share of the other's.

Al-ʿAnkabūt states the principle behind the reply: those who mislead will carry their own burdens and the burdens of others along with them (29:13), and the carrying does not reduce the burden of those who were misled. The verse here records the accusation without recording the answer, and the omission leaves the reader with the futility of it.

**What the Exchange Reveals**

The most striking feature of the dialogue is that it produces nothing. No one is comforted, no responsibility is transferred, no arrangement is made. Two groups arrive at the same place, exchange two refusals and a cause, and remain where they were.

That unproductiveness is the chapter's point about the Fire. The transgressors were described at the start of the sūrah as being in schism — a state of splitting off and standing against. The verse shows the state in its final form: opposition that has no object and achieves nothing, conducted between people who need each other and cannot use each other.

The unproductiveness also answers the question the chapter raised in verse 2. Arrogance and opposition were named there as the reason the truth had been refused, and the verse here shows the two faults in their completed state. Nothing has been corrected by suffering, because the faults were never about information. What was wrong was the disposition, and the disposition has travelled intact.

**"What an Evil Place for Settlement"**

The closing exclamation repeats the formula used of the place in verse 56, and the repetition is spoken this time by an inhabitant rather than by the text. The judgement the chapter passed on the Fire is confirmed from inside it, by a person who has arrived.

The confirmation is rhetorically effective because of who gives it. A warning issued from outside can be doubted; the same judgement uttered by someone who is inside cannot. The verse places the chapter's verdict in the mouth of a follower in the Fire, and the placement closes the account of the destination.

**The End of the Argument Between the Two Groups**

The exchange is the last word the Qur'an gives these two groups in this chapter. They do not reconcile, they do not separate, and they do not fall silent in resignation. They finish an argument that has no possible conclusion and remain in the place the argument was about.

The chapter then turns away from them entirely, and the turn is the same one it made after the mockery of verse 16. A dispute that cannot be resolved is not continued; it is left, and the sūrah moves to the address that follows, in which the Prophet is told to say who he is and what he has been sent with.

The turn is also a judgement on the reader's attention. Six verses of the chapter have been given to the two destinations, and the last of them ends in a quarrel with no content. The reader who has followed the chapter is being shown what remains of the argument he was invited to join, and the showing is brief because there is nothing further to say.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:61

> Adding, “Our Lord! Whoever brought this upon us, double their punishment in the Fire.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Request Addressed Upward**

The verse records a second speech from the people of the Fire, and this one is not addressed sideways but upward. The previous verse was a quarrel between two groups at the same level; this one is a petition to God, and its form is that of a supplication — *Our Lord*, followed by a request. The structure of the sentence is exactly that of a duʿāʾ.

The form matters because it exposes the state of the speakers. They have retained the language of prayer while losing everything that made prayer effective. They address God by the name that acknowledges His lordship over them, and they use that acknowledgement to ask for something. The verse is showing a religious vocabulary surviving the loss of the relationship it belongs to.

What they ask for is also characteristic. They do not ask for relief, for release or for a second chance. They ask for someone else to be punished more. The petition is the last available form of the same disposition the chapter identified in its second verse, and it is offered in the one idiom that once had access to mercy.

**"Whoever Brought This Upon Us"**

The object of the request is described with a phrase that names a cause rather than a person. Whoever brought this upon us — whoever prepared it, whoever sent it ahead, whoever furnished the path that ended here. The speakers do not know who it is, and the vagueness is not accidental.

The vagueness is a confession. To ask that a person be punished is to admit that there is a person responsible, and to describe that person without naming him is to admit that the speakers do not know where their own situation originated. They can see the effect clearly and the cause not at all, which is precisely the condition they were in during their life.

The phrase also carries the accusation the followers made in the previous verse. The followers said *you brought this upon us*; the speakers here say *whoever brought this upon us*. The same charge is being made, but its target has become indefinite, and the indefiniteness is what remains of a blame that no longer has anywhere definite to go.

**The Doubling Asked For**

The request is specific: double their punishment. Not punish them, and not punish them as we are punished, but twice as much. The speakers are not asking for justice in any general sense; they are asking for an inequality in their own favour.

The Qur'an records the same request elsewhere with the same wording. Al-Aʿrāf reports the followers saying that their leaders misled them and asking for the torment to be multiplied, and the answer given there is that it has already been multiplied for all of them, though they do not know it (7:38). The answer refuses the inequality while granting the substance, and it does so by telling the petitioners that they do not know what is actually happening.

The doubling the Qur'an describes is a doubling of a different kind. It is not one group receiving twice what another receives; it is each group receiving two punishments, one for its own straying and one for the straying it caused in others. Al-Aḥzāb records the same plea and the same logic (33:67–68). The request for a doubled punishment is granted, and granted to everyone, which is not what the petitioners wanted.

**Burdens Carried and Burdens Shared**

The principle behind the answer is stated plainly in al-ʿAnkabūt: those who sought to mislead others will carry their own burdens, and other burdens along with their own (29:13). The carrying is additive, and the addition does not transfer anything.

That last point is the verse's sharpest implication. A reader might suppose that if the leaders carry extra burdens, the followers carry fewer. The Qur'an says the opposite explicitly, and the commentators note that the punishment of those who were led astray is not lessened by the doubling given to those who led them (7:38–39). The account is not a zero-sum distribution.

The arrangement removes the comfort the petition is reaching for. If suffering could be moved from one person to another, the Fire would contain a politics, and a politics would contain a hope. The verse closes that possibility by making the account purely additive: everyone carries everything they earned, and nobody's carrying reduces anybody else's.

**What the Request Reveals**

The petition is a diagnostic. A person who has understood what has happened to him asks something different from this. The speakers have been inside the Fire long enough to know where they are and what it is, and their conclusion is that the problem is located in someone else.

The chapter has been making that point from its opening verses. Arrogance and schism were named there as the reason the message was refused, and the verse here shows those two faults in their final exercise: an arrogant demand that another be punished more, made from inside a schism that has no outside. The faults have not been corrected by the punishment, because the punishment was never directed at information.

There is also a note of futility in the verse that the chapter leaves unstated. Al-Aʿrāf records the leaders answering their followers with *you were no better than us* (7:39), which is the same refusal to accept a distinction. Both groups want someone else to be worse off, and neither can produce one, and the verse records the request without recording its outcome.

**The Verse in the Chapter's Sequence**

This verse closes the exchange that began with the arrival of the crowd. Three verses of dialogue have been given to the people of the Fire, and they end in a petition that goes nowhere, which is the shape the chapter has given to every argument it has reported from that side.

The close is also the pivot. The next verse begins a new exchange, and after that the chapter leaves the Fire and turns to the declaration the Prophet is told to make. The sūrah has finished with the disputants, and the turn away from them is the chapter's final comment on what dispute achieves.

The placement also answers the mockery of verse 16. The pagans asked to have their portion hastened, and the chapter has now shown the portion being received by people who are still asking for someone else's to be larger. The haste they demanded has been granted in the only form in which it could be granted, and the form is this verse.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:62

> The tyrants will ask ˹one another˺, “But why do we not see those we considered to be lowly

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Begins With an Absence**

The verse opens a new exchange, and the exchange begins not with an accusation but with a puzzle. The speakers have noticed that someone is missing, and the noticing is the first sign in the chapter that the people of the Fire are looking at anything other than themselves.

The absence is the whole content of the question. Nothing else is said about the missing people in this verse; they are identified only by what the speakers once thought of them. The structure of the sentence puts the judgement in the world first and the absence second, and the ordering is what makes the question significant.

The question is also the first genuine question in the chapter's account of the Fire. The previous verses contained refusals, accusations and petitions, none of which sought information. This one does. It asks *why*, and the asking is the beginning of something that the next verse shows never completing.

**"Those We Had Counted Among the Evil"**

The description of the missing people is the verse's centre, and it is a description of a judgement rather than of a fact. We had counted them among the evil — we placed them in that category, we assigned them to it, we treated the assignment as settled.

The verb carries the sense of taking someone as or counting someone among, and it implies a deliberate act of classification. The speakers did not merely dislike these people; they categorised them, and the categorisation was part of how they organised their own position. A group that defines itself by who it excludes needs the excluded, and the chapter has already shown the mechanism in Makkah.

The exclusion was also public. The notables of Quraysh drove the poor believers away from their assemblies and asked whether God had favoured those particular people out of all of them (38:6; cf. 6:53). The verse records the same reasoning from the other end of time, and the reasoning has not changed while its object has disappeared.

**The Judgement That Was Wrong**

What makes the question a question is that the classification has been reversed by the outcome. The people counted among the evil are not in the Fire, and their absence is the evidence that the counting was mistaken.

The verse does not state the reversal in those terms, and the restraint is deliberate. The speakers are not saying *we were wrong*; they are saying *we do not see them*. A confession would be a change of mind, and the verse is careful to show that no change of mind has occurred. What has occurred is an observation that does not fit a belief, and the belief has been left in place.

That detail is what makes the verse severe. A person who has been shown that his judgement was wrong and who responds by asking where the people are, rather than by admitting the error, has not been reached by the evidence. The chapter has described exactly this condition from its first verses and is now showing it after the Day of Reckoning.

**Who the Missing People Are**

The chapter does not name them, and the omission is meaningful. The missing are the poor believers of Makkah — Bilāl, ʿAmmār, Ṣuhayb, Khabbāb and others like them — the people the notables refused to sit with and asked the Prophet to drive away. Their names are absent from this verse as they were absent from the assemblies.

The absence operates in two directions at once. In the world the believers were absent from the gatherings of the notables by exclusion; in the Fire they are absent by a completely different cause, and the people who arranged the first absence cannot see the second. The verse lets the reader supply the names, and the supplying is part of the verse's work.

The chapter has already told the reader what happened to these people. They are among the reverent for whom the gates were opened and the provision does not run out (38:49–54), and they are among those the notables called evil. The verse therefore requires no explanation; it requires the reader to remember the description of the Garden.

**The Same Question in Al-Aʿrāf**

The Qur'an records the same astonishment elsewhere, and there it is turned into a question addressed to the astonished. Al-Aʿrāf reports the people of the Fire being asked whether these are the ones they swore God would never show mercy to (7:49), and the asking is itself the reversal, delivered by someone else.

The two treatments differ in a way worth noting. In this verse the question is asked by the people of the Fire and remains unanswered; in al-Aʿrāf it is asked of them and answered by the sight of the believers entering the Garden. The chapter here withholds the answer, and the withholding keeps the speakers inside their own incomprehension.

Both accounts agree on the substance. The people who made the classification are the ones who discover its falsity, and they discover it by looking for the people they classified and not finding them. The discovery is visual, which is the same mode the chapter used for the Fire throughout: what happens there is seen before it is understood.

**Why the Question Is Asked at All**

The verse raises a question about its own question. If the speakers are in the Fire, and the Fire is populated, and the people they are looking for are absent, the conclusion available to them is obvious. Asking why indicates that the conclusion has not been drawn.

The commentators note this explicitly: that the denizens continue to ask such questions implies that, despite having received the punishment, they still do not fully understand. The punishment has been administered and the understanding has not followed, and the verse is describing the gap between the two.

The gap is the chapter's subject in these verses, and it answers the objection the sūrah began with. The notables claimed to be the ones who knew; the verse shows them in a state of not knowing, after the event, about the people they claimed to have assessed correctly. The knowledge they asserted in the world is not present in the place where knowledge would be of some use.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:63

> Were we wrong in mocking them ˹in the world˺? Or do our eyes ˹just˺ fail to see them ˹in the Fire˺?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Explanations Offered, Neither of Them the Right One**

The verse supplies two candidate answers to the question the previous verse raised, and both of them are wrong in the same way. Either the mockery was mistaken, or the eyes are failing. The first would mean the judgement was incorrect; the second would mean the evidence is defective.

Neither possibility contains the actual answer, which is that the people are in a different place. The speakers have the observation and the two explanations, and the correct explanation has not occurred to them. The verse is precise about this: it lists what they can think of, and the correct thought is not on the list.

That precision is what gives the verse its weight. A reader might expect a person in the Fire to have become clearer about things. The verse shows the opposite — a mind that has kept its old categories, is now applying them to new evidence, and is generating explanations that preserve the categories rather than accounting for the evidence.

**"Did We Take Them in Mockery?"**

The first explanation revisits the behaviour itself. We took them in mockery — we laughed at them, we treated them as objects of derision. The question asks whether that treatment was misplaced.

The wording is important because it stops short of admitting anything. To ask whether one was wrong to mock is not to say that one mocked wrongly; it is to raise the possibility while keeping the act intact. The speakers are examining a practice, not repenting of it, and the examination is conducted in the language of a calculation that may have gone wrong.

The chapter has a particular interest in this word. Mockery and derision were the response the notables gave to the message from the beginning, and the Qur'an says elsewhere that those who mocked the believers will be mocked in return (83:29–34). The verse here shows the mockers questioning the practice for the first time, and the questioning arises from an absence rather than from a recognition.

**"Or Do Our Eyes Fail to See Them?"**

The second explanation shifts from the act to the instrument. Perhaps they are here and we cannot see them. The possibility preserves the original judgement entirely: the people are still where they were classified, and only the perception is defective.

The second explanation is the more revealing of the two. A mind that cannot abandon a classification will locate the failure in the senses before locating it in the belief. The verse records that preference, and the preference is the same one the chapter described in its account of the litigants before David: a person who has decided what is true will examine his evidence rather than his conclusion.

The phrase also carries a note of hope. If the eyes are failing, the people may still be in the Fire, and if they are still in the Fire the original assessment stands. The explanation is offered because it is the one that would allow the speakers to keep what they already believe, and the verse lets them say it.

**An Alternative Reading**

The first sentence of the verse can be read differently. Instead of a question — *did we take them in mockery?* — it can be read as a statement: *we took them in mockery*. The commentators record both readings, and the second removes the uncertainty from the first half of the verse.

On the statement reading, the speakers are not asking whether the mockery was wrong; they are stating that they mocked, and then asking where the people are. The admission becomes flat and unexamined, and the question that follows becomes more striking, because a person who admits mocking and then wonders at the absence has admitted nothing.

Both readings are preserved in the tradition, and the difference between them is small in wording and large in effect. The question reading shows hesitation; the statement reading shows none. The chapter's account of the Fire elsewhere supports the second, since nothing in it indicates a change of mind.

**Mockery as the Chapter's Organising Theme**

The word chosen here connects this verse to the opening of the sūrah more directly than anything else in the passage. The notables went out saying that Muhammad was a liar and an impostor, and they said it in the register of contempt rather than of argument. The verse names that register and locates it in the past tense.

The past tense does the work. We took them in mockery describes something completed, and the speakers are now examining a completed act from inside its consequence. The chapter has been describing exactly this structure since its first verses: a judgement made in one condition and evaluated in another, with no opportunity to revise it in between.

The verse also completes the account of the poor believers that the chapter began in verse 6. The notables asked whether God had favoured those particular people; the verse shows them asking where those people are. Both questions have the same answer, and in both cases the answer is given by a fact rather than by an argument.

**Understanding That Never Arrives**

The commentators note that the continuation of these questions shows the speakers still do not fully understand. The observation to draw from that is not that the Fire prevents understanding but that the disposition prevents it. Nothing in the account suggests an obstacle; everything in it suggests an unwillingness.

The verse is the chapter's clearest statement of that point, because it presents the two explanations the speakers can produce and shows that both are designed to preserve what they already believe. The failure is not intellectual. It is the same arrogance named in verse 2, operating in a place where it no longer has anything to protect.

The chapter then closes the account with a single sentence confirming that the dispute is true. The closing is deliberate: the speakers are not answered, corrected or instructed. They are reported, and the reporting is left to stand on its own.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:64

> This dispute between the residents of the Fire will certainly come to pass

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sentence That Confirms What Was Just Reported**

The verse steps outside the dialogue and comments on it. Three verses of speech between the people of the Fire have been reported, and this verse states that the speech will happen. The statement is an assurance, and the assurance is the chapter's signature on the account.

The form of the sentence is worth reading closely. It does not say that the dispute is true in the sense of accurate; it says that it is true in the sense of certain, of realised, of something that will occur. The Arabic construction combines a noun of truth with a confirming particle, and the combination produces an emphatic assertion about occurrence rather than about content.

The effect is to place the reader's relationship to the dialogue on a different footing. The preceding verses could be read as illustration; this verse removes that reading. What was reported is not a device but an event, and the verse says so in the shortest form available to it.

**What They Dispute About**

The commentators describe the dispute as being about what led the people of the Fire to their end, and also as a dispute with God. The two objects of the dispute are not separate; the argument about causes and the argument with God are the same argument conducted in two directions.

The chapter has already shown the argument about causes in the exchange between the leaders and the followers. You brought this upon us is a claim about a cause, and the reply is a refusal to accept it. Neither party can produce a cause that accounts for the whole, and the argument stops there.

The argument with God is recorded elsewhere in the Qur'an and is characterised by the same feature: it does not conclude. Its denizens are described as calling out, asking to be returned, and being told that they were reminded and refused (23:99–108). The verse here generalises all of that into a single noun and confirms it.

**Disputing With One Another**

The first object of the dispute is the other inhabitants. The chapter has shown this in detail: the leaders refusing welcome to the followers, the followers accusing the leaders, and both groups asking for someone else to be punished more. The disputing is continuous and productive of nothing.

The Qur'an describes the same condition in other terms elsewhere. Al-Shuʿarāʾ records the inhabitants saying that they were in manifest error when they equated others with the Lord of the worlds, and describes them being thrown into the Fire along with those they followed (26:96–99). The mutual recrimination is a fixed feature of the description.

The feature matters because it inverts what the people of the Fire expected. They spent their lives in a competition for standing, and the verse describes a place where the competition continues with the prizes removed. The dispute is the last form the competition takes, and it is the form in which it can never be won.

**Speaking Across the Barrier**

The commentators note a distinction that the verse's wording preserves. No dispute passes between Hell and the Garden, though speech does. The distinction is between argument and request, and the two are treated differently throughout the Qur'an's accounts.

The speaking is recorded in several places. Its inhabitants call out to the people of the Garden asking for water or for some of what God has provided (7:50), and the hypocrites ask the believers to wait so that they may take some of their light (57:13). Both are requests rather than arguments, and both are refused.

The refusal in al-Aʿrāf is stated as a prohibition: God has forbidden both to the disbelievers (7:50). The blessings of the Garden cannot be transferred, and the reason given is a prohibition rather than a shortage. The chapter has already described the provision of the Garden as inexhaustible (38:54), so the refusal is not about quantity.

**Why the Distinction Matters**

The absence of dispute with the people of the Garden tells the reader something about the state of both groups. A dispute requires a shared framework, and the two destinations no longer share one. The people of the Fire can argue with each other because they still agree about what is at stake; they cannot argue with the people of the Garden because the people of the Garden are no longer in the contest.

That is the chapter's point about the whole exchange. The argument the notables opened in Makkah was an argument about standing, and the verse describes its end: one side still arguing, the other side no longer participating. The contest did not conclude with a winner; it concluded with one party leaving the field.

The verse also explains why the chapter turns away at this point. A dispute with no counterpart cannot be continued, and the sūrah's next words are addressed to the Prophet rather than to the disputants. The turn is not a change of subject but the natural end of this one.

**A Truth Stated Twice**

The verse's structure repeats its own assertion. The truth and the confirming particle both do the work of establishing certainty, and the doubling is the same device the chapter used when it called the destination of the transgressors the worst and then exclaimed over it (38:56).

The doubling has a specific function here. The chapter has been reporting speech from the people of the Fire, and reported speech can be taken as rhetorical. The verse removes the possibility by asserting the occurrence twice over, and the assertion closes the entire account of the two destinations that began at verse 49.

The closure is also a transition. Sixteen verses have described the righteous and the transgressors and their two ends, and the verse ends the description with a statement of fact rather than with an image. The chapter then addresses the Prophet directly, and the direct address is the chapter's way of returning the reader to the only place where anything can still be done.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:65

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “I am only a warner. And there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Allah—the One, the Supreme

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Address Returns to the Prophet**

The chapter has spent sixteen verses describing the two destinations and three verses reporting the speech of the people of the Fire. This verse turns back to the Prophet and gives him something to say, and the turn is the chapter's pivot from description to declaration.

The word *say* is a command in the imperative, and the Qur'an uses it at moments where what follows is to be stated in the first person by the Messenger. The command has a specific effect: it makes the declaration an utterance rather than a proposition. What follows is not presented as a claim about God that the reader evaluates; it is presented as words a man is told to speak.

The placement of the command after the account of the Fire is deliberate. The chapter has shown what dispute produces, and it now supplies the alternative. The alternative is not a better argument. It is a declaration with a defined speaker and a defined content, and the two are given together.

**"I Am Only a Warner"**

The first half of the declaration limits the speaker's own role, and the limitation is stated with a restricting particle. Only — nothing more than a warner. The verse is telling the Prophet to say what he is not as well as what he is.

The same formulation appears repeatedly in the Qur'an, and the repetition indicates a persistent objection. The Prophet is described as nothing but a warner in Hūd (11:12), al-Raʿd (13:7), al-Naml (27:92), Sabaʾ (34:46), Fāṭir (35:23) and al-Nāziʿāt (79:45). Each occurrence answers a demand that he produce something other than a warning.

The commentators explain the limitation precisely: his function is to deliver the message, and he does not have knowledge of the unseen except what God gives him. The two halves of the verse are connected by that explanation. The warner does not claim knowledge, and the verses that follow will say so explicitly.

**What a Warner Does and Does Not Do**

A warner delivers information about a danger and leaves the response to the person warned. He does not compel, does not guarantee and does not bear responsibility for the outcome. The verse is defining the role in terms of what it excludes, and the exclusions are the ones the notables of Makkah kept demanding that he violate.

They asked him to produce the punishment he warned about, to bring angels as witnesses, to move mountains and to split the earth (38:16; cf. 17:90–93). Every one of those demands requires the warner to be something other than a warner. The declaration answers all of them at once by refusing the role they were asking for.

The refusal is also a protection. A warner who could compel belief would not be delivering a message but imposing one, and the Qur'an states repeatedly that compulsion is not available to the Messenger (10:99; 88:21–22). The verse's limitation is therefore not a diminishment of the Prophet but a statement of the kind of thing revelation is.

**"There Is No God but God"**

The second half of the declaration is the tawḥīd, and its placement inside the warner's speech is the verse's structural claim. The warning and the declaration of oneness are given as one utterance, and the connection between them is that the warning is about what follows from rejecting the declaration.

The formulation is the negative followed by the affirmation: no god, then God. The order matters, because the negation clears the ground before the affirmation occupies it. A declaration that began with *God is one* would leave the other gods standing alongside; the verse removes them first.

The chapter has been working toward this sentence since its opening. The notables went out saying *carry on, and stand firm in devotion to your gods* (38:6), and their objection was that Muhammad had made the gods into one god. The declaration answers the objection in the exact terms in which it was raised.

**"The One"**

The first of the two names attached to God in this verse is the name of unity, and its function here is to answer the objection directly. The notables found the reduction to one god astonishing; the verse makes the oneness the first attribute named.

The Qur'an pairs this name with the one that follows in five other places, and the pairing is consistent: al-Wāḥid al-Qahhār, the One, the Paramount (12:39; 13:16; 14:48; 39:4; 40:16). In every instance the pairing occurs in a context where a claim to power or lordship is being displaced. Yūsuf uses it to ask whether many lords or the One, the Paramount is better; al-Raʿd uses it to conclude that God is the Creator of all things.

The consistency of the pairing indicates that the two names are not chosen independently. Oneness describes a fact about God; the name that follows describes what that fact means for anyone who contests it. The verse is moving from a statement to a consequence.

**"The Paramount"**

The second name is al-Qahhār, from the same root as al-Qāhir, and it denotes one who overcomes, subdues and prevails over everything. The intensity of the form indicates that the prevailing is complete and unresisted.

The name answers the situation of the sūrah's audience. The notables of Makkah were powerful men, and their objection to the message was partly an objection to being told what to do by someone with no power. The name states that the one being declared has power over them, and the stating is not a threat but a description.

The verse's two names together produce the chapter's central claim in compressed form. There is one God, and that one God prevails. The first half answers the theological objection raised in verse 5; the second half answers the practical objection raised in verse 2. The declaration is aimed at both, and it is placed in the mouth of a man who has been told to say that he is only a warner.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:66

> ˹He is the˺ Lord of the heavens and the earth and everything in between—the Almighty, Most Forgiving.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Declaration Extended**
The previous verse declared God's oneness and supremacy; this verse extends the declaration into lordship and adds two further names. The extension is not an addition of new material but a specification of what the oneness covers, and the specification is given in spatial terms.

The structure of the two verses together is worth noting. The first names God as One and Paramount; the second names Him as Lord of a defined domain and then as Mighty and Forgiving. Four names across two verses, each pair answering a different objection, and the whole set placed inside a single declaration the Prophet is told to make.

The closing quotation mark at the end of this verse closes what the previous verse opened. The declaration is a complete unit, and the unit is what the chapter offers in place of the dispute it has just described.

**"Lord of the Heavens and the Earth"**
The title of lordship is given with its domain specified, and the specification is the largest one available in the Qur'an's vocabulary. Not a tribe, not a city, not a region — the heavens and the earth.

The Qur'an uses this formulation as an argument elsewhere, and the argument has a fixed shape. Al-Raʿd asks who the Lord of the heavens and the earth is, answers *God*, and then asks why lords have been taken besides Him who cannot benefit or protect themselves (13:16). The question is rhetorical because the answer was already accepted by the people being asked.

That is the situation in Makkah as well. The notables did not deny that God created the heavens and the earth; the Qur'an says they would affirm it if asked (29:61; 31:25). Their error was not in the premise but in the inference, and the verse is supplying the inference they refused to draw.

**Whatsoever Is Between Them, and the Name of Forgiving**
The addition of what lies between the two is not padding. It closes a gap that the two terms alone would leave, and the gap is where the idols were thought to operate.

The deities of the Arabian pantheon were not creators. They were intermediaries, and the Qur'an records the claim explicitly: the idolaters said they worshipped them only so that they might bring them nearer to God (39:3). An intermediary requires a domain, and the domain claimed for these beings was the space between heaven and earth.

The phrase removes that space. Whatever is between the heavens and the earth is included in the lordship, which means there is no region in which an intermediary could act independently. The verse is answering the actual theology of its audience rather than an abstract one, and the answer is geographical.

The fourth name is the one that changes the character of the declaration. The first three names establish power; this one establishes that the power is not exercised without limit against those who have earned it.

The commentators note that the combination of the Mighty and the Forgiving refers to God's threat against the disbelievers and His promise to the believers. The two names are not a softening of the first three but a specification of how they apply: might toward those who persist, forgiveness toward those who turn.

The placement of forgiveness last is significant given what the chapter has just described. Sixteen verses have been devoted to the two destinations, and the Fire has been described without softening. The declaration ends on the name of forgiveness, and the ending is the chapter's offer to the men it has been describing.

**"The Mighty"**
The third name is al-ʿAzīz, and its sense is not merely strength but a strength that cannot be overcome. The word denotes one who is powerful and unconquerable, and the Qur'an uses it as a closing name more than any other except the Merciful.

In this declaration the name answers the same objection the previous verse's al-Qahhār answered, and the repetition from a different angle is characteristic of the Qur'an's method. Supremacy describes the fact of prevailing; might describes the quality that makes prevailing unavoidable. The two names together leave no room for the possibility that God could be resisted successfully.

The name also answers the specific circumstance of the sūrah's revelation. The Prophet was a man with no army, no wealth and no tribal backing sufficient to protect him, and the notables were men who had all three. The declaration asks the reader to locate might somewhere other than in those three, and it does so by attaching the name to the Lord of the heavens and the earth.

**Two Names in Combination**
The pairing of the Mighty with the Forgiving occurs in three other places in the Qur'an (39:5; 40:42; 67:2), and in each case the combination carries the same double reference. Al-Zumar states that God created the heavens and the earth with truth and then names Himself the Mighty, the Forgiving — creation and forgiveness in the same breath.

The more frequent pairing in the Qur'an is the Mighty with the Merciful, which occurs dozens of times, most densely in al-Shuʿarāʾ. The choice of the Forgiving here rather than the Merciful is a small variation with a specific effect: forgiveness is directed at an offence already committed, and the chapter has just described people who have committed one.

The four names of the declaration therefore form a sequence rather than a list. One, Paramount, Lord of everything, Mighty, Forgiving. Each term follows from the one before, and the final term is the one addressed to the reader's own situation. The declaration closes with the possibility that the preceding verses have made urgent.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:67

> Say, “This ˹Quran˺ is momentous news

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Second Command to Speak**
The chapter gives the Prophet a second declaration to make, and the command is the same word as before. The first declaration concerned who God is; this one concerns what the message is. The two commands are separated by nothing, and the proximity indicates that the second follows from the first.

The structure is worth observing. Verse 65 opens with *say* and closes with the names of God; verse 67 opens with *say* and describes the message. The chapter is giving the Prophet a complete statement of his position — the content of what he proclaims and the character of the thing he brings — and it gives both in the imperative.

The imperative form also answers the accusation the notables made. They said the Qur'an was nothing but an invention, or poetry, or magic, or the speech of a man being taught (38:4–7; cf. 16:101; 25:4–5). The verse responds by having the Prophet describe the thing himself, in terms that place it above all of those categories.

**A Great Tiding, and What It Refers To**
The word used is *nabaʾ*, which denotes news of consequence — information about something that matters, as distinct from ordinary report. The Qur'an distinguishes it from mere talk by using it for events of weight, and the adjective attached here makes the weight explicit.

Great is the same adjective the Qur'an uses elsewhere for matters of ultimate significance, and its application to the message is the verse's central claim. The notables treated the revelation as a subject for argument and mockery; the verse describes it as news, which is a different category entirely. News is not argued with; it is received or ignored.

The description also has a temporal implication. A tiding is about something, and something has happened or will happen. The verse is saying that the Qur'an is not a set of opinions but a report about a reality, and the reality is described in the verses that follow.

The commentators give three identifications, and the three are not competing but overlapping. The tiding is the Qur'an itself, or the Day of Resurrection, or the message of God's oneness issuing directly from Him.

The first reading takes the word referentially: this thing you are hearing is a great news. The second takes it prospectively: the news is about the Day, and the Qur'an is the medium by which the news arrives. The third takes it substantively: what is new and momentous is the declaration of oneness given in the previous two verses.

All three readings are supported by the immediate context. The chapter has just declared God's oneness, has described the Day of Reckoning and its two outcomes, and is describing the revelation itself. The verse sits at the intersection of the three, and the commentators' plurality indicates that the word is doing all three jobs at once.

**The Same Word in Al-Nabaʾ**
The Qur'an opens an entire sūrah with this word. Al-Nabaʾ begins by asking what they are questioning one another about, and answers: the great tiding, about which they differ (78:1–3). The identification there is the Day of Resurrection, and the difference is stated as the reason for the question.

The parallel is close enough to be significant. Both passages use the same phrase, both attach it to something the audience disputes, and both follow it with a description of the Day. In al-Nabaʾ the questioning is the subject of the opening; here the turning away is the subject of the next verse. The two treatments describe the same response from two angles.

The chapter here also echoes al-Nabaʾ in another respect. Al-Nabaʾ goes on to describe the Fire and the Garden in detail (78:21–36), and this chapter has just done the same. The sequence of great tiding followed by description of the two destinations is shared by both sūrahs.

**Greatness as Weight**
The adjective carries a sense that deserves attention. Great in Arabic usage denotes magnitude, and magnitude in this context is not size but consequence. A thing is great when what follows from it is great, and the chapter has spent sixteen verses describing what follows from this one.

The description therefore does the work of an argument without making one. The verse does not say the Qur'an is true, or proved, or undeniable; it says it is great, and the greatness has already been demonstrated by the account of the two destinations that precedes it. A reader who has followed the chapter knows what is at stake, and the verse names the magnitude of the stake.

The weight is also the answer to the mockery. Something treated as trivial cannot be trivial if the consequences of ignoring it are what the chapter has described. The verse's adjective is a claim about those consequences, and the chapter has already supplied the evidence for it.

**The Verse's Place in the Sequence**
This verse is the hinge between the chapter's eschatological section and its closing section. Everything before it has been description — the two destinations, the speech of their inhabitants. Everything after it is declaration — what the message is, what the Prophet knows and does not know, and the account of Adam that occupies the rest of the sūrah.

The hinge is a command to speak, and the command places the Prophet at the centre of the transition. The chapter has shown what the dispute produces and what the two destinations contain, and it now hands the reader the one thing that can still be acted on: the news itself, described in a single phrase.

The single phrase is also the chapter's shortest self-description. Elsewhere the Qur'an calls itself a reminder, a light, a guidance, a clear book. Here it is news, and news is the description that most directly implies a hearer who has not yet heard it.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:68

> from which you ˹pagans˺ are turning away.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Relative Clause That Becomes an Accusation**
The verse completes the sentence the previous verse began, and the completion is unexpected. The tiding was described as great; the clause that follows does not say why, or what it contains, or what will follow from it. It says that the audience is turning away from it.

The structure converts a description into an indictment. A statement of the form *this is a great news* is informative; the addition of *from which you turn away* makes the statement about the hearers rather than about the news. The verse is telling the audience something about themselves, and the telling is the point.

The change of address is also notable. The previous verse was a command to the Prophet; this one is spoken to the pagans directly, inside the quotation that the Prophet is told to deliver. The declaration is therefore made in their hearing, and the accusation is part of the declaration rather than a comment on it.

**The Direction of the Turning**
The verb denotes a turning aside, a diversion from something one has encountered. It is not the word for ignorance or for absence. A person who turns away has faced the thing first, and the verse's choice of verb establishes that the audience has been addressed.

That establishment matters, because it removes the defence of not having been told. The Qur'an says elsewhere that no people are destroyed until a messenger is sent to them (17:15), and the chapter has been describing a people who have received the message in detail. The turning is not a failure of reception but a response to reception.

The direction of the turning is also informative. One turns away from something one has judged not worth facing, and the verse's adjective answers that judgement. The news is great; the turning treats it as small. The contradiction between the two is the accusation, and the verse leaves it for the reader to see.

**A Habit Rather Than a Decision, and What Turning Does Not Change**
The grammatical form of the verb indicates a continuing action rather than a single event. You are turning away — the turning is ongoing, repeated, habitual. The verse is not describing a moment of refusal but a settled posture.

The distinction is important to the chapter's account of its audience. The notables did not hear the message once and reject it; they heard it repeatedly and rejected it repeatedly, and they organised their opposition around the repetition. The chapter describes them going out and urging one another to patience in devotion to their gods (38:6), which is an organised response to a continuing message.

The habitual form also explains why the turning is described without a reason. A single refusal might have a cause worth recording; a settled posture does not. The verse reports the posture and gives no cause, and the absence of a cause is the chapter's comment on it.

The verse's most severe implication is unstated. Turning away from a great news does not reduce the news. The greatness is a property of the thing, not of the response to it, and the verse's construction makes the two independent.

The chapter has already demonstrated this independence in its account of the destroyed peoples. ʿĀd, Thamūd, the people of Lot and Pharaoh all turned away, and the turning did not prevent what followed.
