The roll-call in verses 12–14 is a list of turnings and their outcomes, and the outcomes are unaffected by the turnings.

The verse therefore functions as a warning without saying so. It describes an action and leaves the reader to supply the consequence from the sixteen verses of the chapter that precede it. The supply is easy, because the chapter has just spent that space describing precisely what follows.

**Turning in the Chapter's Vocabulary**
The chapter has used this verb and its cognates before, and the repetition ties this verse to the earlier accounts. The notables are described as turning away in arrogance in verse 2, and the same turning is attributed to them in the account of the message's reception throughout.

The verb also appears in the chapter's account of the prophets. David was told not to follow desire lest it lead him astray from the path of God (38:26), and the warning concerns the same movement — a turning aside from something one knows. The chapter treats turning as the basic act of rejection, and it uses the same word for the individual and the collective.

The repetition produces a diagnosis rather than a description. By the time this verse arrives, the reader has seen turning in the notables' opposition, in their mockery of the believers, in their demands for signs, and in their treatment of the message. The verse names the pattern once more and leaves it at that.

**The Two Verses as One Statement**
Verses 67 and 68 form a single sentence, and the sentence is the chapter's summary of its own situation. A great news exists, and the audience is turning away from it. Nothing else is added, and the absence of addition is deliberate.

The brevity answers the length of what precedes it. Sixteen verses of description and three of reported speech have been given, and the chapter compresses its conclusion into two short lines. The compression is a claim about where the matter stands: the information has been delivered, the response has been observed, and there is nothing further to argue.

The sentence also sets up the verses that follow. The Prophet is next told to say that he has no knowledge of the highest assembly and that he is only a clear warner, which continues the same line — a man bringing news he did not originate, to people who are turning away from it. The two verses state the situation; the next two define the messenger's place in it.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:69

> ˹And say,˺ “I had no knowledge of the highest assembly ˹in heaven˺ when they differed ˹concerning Adam˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Confession of Ignorance**

The verse continues the speech the Prophet is commanded to deliver, and its content is a denial of knowledge. Not a claim, not an argument, not a proof — a statement that he did not know something, and could not have known it.

The confession is a rhetorical act of unusual directness. A claimant to revelation is expected to demonstrate access to hidden matters; this verse has the claimant deny access to a specific hidden matter in explicit terms. The denial is the ground on which the claim to revelation will be made in the next verse, and the two verses work as a pair.

The construction of the sentence is also significant. It is negative in form and refers to a specific occasion — when they differed. The verse is not saying that the Prophet knows nothing in general; it is saying that he had no knowledge of one particular event, which means any knowledge of it must have come from somewhere.

**The Highest Assembly**

The phrase denotes an assembly in the highest region, and the commentators identify it consistently as the assembly of angels. The term is used elsewhere in the Qur'an, and the identification is not disputed in the tradition.

The assembly is described as highest in a way that carries both spatial and rank meaning. It is the gathering of beings who are nearest to God, and the Qur'an describes the angels as ranged in ranks and as those who do not precede Him in speech (21:26–27; 37:165). The assembly is therefore a place of ordered deliberation rather than of debate in the ordinary sense.

The identification also matters for the argument the verse is making. The event described took place before human beings existed and outside the range of anything a human could witness. A man in seventh-century Arabia could not have attended it, heard of it from a witness, or inferred it. The verse selects an event whose inaccessibility is absolute.

**The Dispute They Disputed**

Most commentators identify the dispute as the one concerning the creation of Adam, and the Qur'an records that discussion in al-Baqarah. The angels ask whether God will place in the earth someone who will spread corruption and shed blood, while they glorify His praise and proclaim His holiness; God answers that He knows what they do not know (2:30).

The exchange is not a dispute in the sense of opposition. The angels are asking a question about a decision whose wisdom they cannot see, and the answer given to them is a statement of a knowledge they lack. The commentators use the word for difference and discussion, and the Qur'an's own account shows a question and a reply.

The identification matters because it connects this verse to the account that follows. Verses 71 to 85 will narrate the creation of Adam and Iblīs's refusal, and this verse establishes that the narrator had no independent access to the story. The narration is therefore evidence for the claim made in the next verse.

**The Report About the Expiations**

An alternative identification of the dispute is recorded in a ḥadīth narrated through several chains. The Prophet ﷺ reported that his Lord came to him in the night in the most beautiful form and asked him what the highest assembly was disputing about; he said he did not know, and the question was repeated. Then his Lord placed His palm between his shoulders and he felt its coolness between his breasts, and what is in the heavens and the earth was disclosed to him. When the question was put again he answered: about the expiations. Asked what the expiations are, he said: going on foot to the congregations, sitting in the mosques after the prayers, and performing ablution completely in difficult circumstances. Whoever does that lives well and dies well, and his offences are as on the day his mother bore him (Sunan al-Tirmidhī 3235).

The report is graded ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ by al-Tirmidhī and is transmitted in the Musnad of Aḥmad. Some versions add that the Prophet was then invited to ask for anything, and he asked for the performance of good deeds, the avoidance of bad deeds, love of the poor, forgiveness and mercy, and for the love of God and of those who love Him.

Both identifications can stand together. The dispute about the creation of Adam is the event the verse refers to historically; the report describes a different occasion on which the same phrase was used. The commentators record both, and the verse's wording is general enough to accommodate either.

**What the Confession Achieves**

The confession does three things at once. It establishes that the Prophet had no natural access to the story, it demonstrates that he is telling the truth about the limits of his knowledge, and it prepares the ground for the claim that follows.

The third function is the most important. A man who claims to know everything cannot be tested; a man who specifies what he does not know has given his audience something to check. The verse is inviting exactly that check — go and find out whether anyone in Makkah knew this story.

The answer was available to the audience. The notables had consulted the Jewish scholars of Madīnah for questions to test the Prophet with (cf. 18:9–26), which means they had access to people who possessed scriptural knowledge. The verse's claim is falsifiable by them, and the chapter places it in their mouths to deliver.

**The Argument From Ignorance**

The commentators note that the purpose of these verses is to instruct the Prophet to say that he could have had no knowledge of the discussion between God, the angels and Satan were it not for the revelation from God. The argument is a fortiori: if he did not know this, and he is now reporting it, the reporting has a source.

The form of the argument is characteristic of the Qur'an's treatment of the prophetic claim. Rather than asserting the source, it establishes the impossibility of the alternative. The Prophet was unlettered, had not travelled to the centres of learning, and had not studied scripture (29:48; 7:157–158). The verse adds a specific instance to that general claim.

The instance is well chosen because it concerns a matter that no human tradition preserved in the Qur'an's form. The accounts of Adam in the existing scriptures differ in detail and do not contain the exchange with Iblīs as this chapter gives it. The verse is pointing at a specificity that could not have been borrowed.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:70

> What is revealed to me is that I am only sent with a clear warning.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Clear Warner, and the Conclusion of the Declaration**
The verse completes the speech the Prophet was commanded to deliver, and the completion answers the confession that preceded it. He did not know the story of the highest assembly; what is revealed to him is that he is a clear warner. The two statements together form the whole of his claim.

The connection between them is a logical one. The ignorance establishes that the knowledge he does have is not his own, and this verse states what that knowledge consists of. It is not a body of information about hidden things; it is a commission, and the commission is to warn.

The closing quotation mark ends the speech begun at verse 67, and the whole passage — four verses of commanded speech — is the chapter's formal statement of the Prophet's position. Everything after it is narrative.

The adjective attached to the warner is *mubīn*, which denotes something that makes its meaning plain, that is evident in itself and makes other things evident. The Qur'an uses it of the Book, of the signs, and of the messengers.

Applied to a warner, the adjective has a specific force. A warning that is unclear places no obligation on the person warned; a warning that is clear transfers the responsibility entirely. The verse is describing the character of the warning in a way that has consequences for the hearer.

The chapter has been building toward this description. It has set out the two destinations in detail, described the speech of their inhabitants, declared the oneness of God, and named the message a great news. The clarity is demonstrated by the preceding material rather than asserted, and the verse names it once the demonstration is complete.

**"Nothing Is Revealed to Me Except"**
The construction is restrictive, and the restriction is the verse's most striking feature. The Prophet is commanded to say that the revelation he receives amounts to this one thing: that he is a warner. Not that he knows the unseen, not that he holds the treasuries, not that he can produce what his opponents demand.

The restriction answers the demands the chapter recorded earlier. The notables asked for the punishment to be hastened, for the heavens to be opened in pieces, for the angels to be brought before them (38:16; cf. 17:90–93). Each demand assumes a revelation containing powers and prerogatives. The verse says the revelation contains a commission.

The same restriction is stated elsewhere in the Qur'an with identical wording, and the repetition indicates that the demand was persistent. The Prophet is told to say that he does not possess the treasuries of God, does not know the unseen, and is nothing but a warner (6:50; 46:9; 79:45). The verse here is one instance of a standing answer.

**What Clarity Requires of the Hearer**
A clear warning has a defined effect on the person who receives it. Once the warning has been made plain, the hearer's subsequent state is his own, and the Qur'an states the principle repeatedly: whoever turns away bears the consequence of his turning (cf. 6:104; 10:108; 39:41).

The verse therefore completes the chapter's argument about responsibility. The notables claimed that the message was obscure, invented, or the product of a man being taught. The verse describes it as clear, and the description removes the basis of the claim. What remains is the choice, and the choice is theirs.

The connection to the previous verses is direct. The tiding is great and the audience is turning away from it; the warning is clear and the Prophet is only its deliverer. The two statements locate the entire matter in the response of the hearer, which is the only place the chapter has left for it.

**The Verse as a Summary of the Prophetic Claim**
Taken together with verse 65, this verse gives the chapter's complete account of what the Prophet is. He is a warner and nothing more; he proclaims that there is no god but God, the One, the Paramount; he does not know the hidden matters; he has been sent with a clear warning.

The account is remarkable for what it omits. There is no claim to power, to wealth, to miraculous ability on demand, or to knowledge of the future. The chapter has already noted that the notables found it strange that a man with no standing should bring such a message (38:4–8), and the declaration answers them by refusing every category they were measuring him against.

The refusal is also a test. A claimant who demanded belief on the basis of power would be believed by the powerful and rejected by the weak. A claimant who demands belief on the basis of a clear warning is judged by the clarity alone, and the clarity is available to everyone equally.

**The Chapter Turning to Narrative**
The verse closes the chapter's declarative section and opens its final narrative. The next verse begins the account of the creation of Adam, which runs to verse 85 and is the last such account in the textual order of the Qur'an.

The transition is prepared by the verse before it. The Prophet has said he had no knowledge of the highest assembly when they differed; the narrative that follows is the account of that difference. The reader is given the story immediately after being told that the storyteller could not have known it, and the placement is the argument.

The chapter's method throughout has been to place evidence next to claim rather than to argue from one to the other. The declaration of oneness followed the account of the two destinations; the confession of ignorance precedes the story that demonstrates it. The verse is the hinge, and the hinge is a single sentence about what the Messenger has actually been sent with.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:71

> ˹Remember, O Prophet˺ when your Lord said to the angels, “I am going to create a human being from clay

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Last Account of the Creation**
The chapter now begins its final narrative, and the narrative is the creation of Adam and the refusal of Iblīs. This is the last such account in the textual order of the Qur'an, and the commentators note that it is most likely the first in the order of revelation. The other accounts appear in al-Baqarah (2:30–39), al-Aʿrāf (7:11–25), al-Isrāʾ (17:61–65) and Ṭā-Hā (20:115–24), with a closely parallel version in al-Ḥijr (15:28–43).

The placement at the end of the sūrah is significant. The chapter has moved from the Qur'an to the pagans' objection, through the stories of David, Solomon, Job and the patriarchs, to the two destinations, and now to the origin of the enmity that the whole sūrah has been describing. The narrative is the root of the account, and the chapter saves it for last.

The timing is also relevant to the argument. A story about the first act of arrogance is placed immediately after an account of people whose arrogance has reached its consequence, and the placement makes the connection without stating it.

**"When Your Lord Said to the Angels"**
The narrative opens with a divine address to the angels, and the address is a declaration of intent rather than a request for consultation. God announces what He is going to do, and the announcement is made to beings who will be affected by it.

The form of the sentence uses the active participle of creation, which indicates something in progress or about to occur. The declaration precedes the act, and the precedence allows the angels to respond — which in al-Baqarah they do, asking whether God will place in the earth someone who will spread corruption and shed blood (2:30).

The address to the angels establishes them as witnesses. What follows is not a private act but a public one, performed before an assembly, and the assembly's presence is what makes Iblīs's refusal a witnessed event rather than a hidden one.

**"A Human Being"**
The word used is *bashar*, which denotes a human being in the physical sense — a being of skin and surface, one who appears. The Qur'an uses it consistently when the physical constitution of the human being is in view, and its use here prepares the argument Iblīs will make in verse 76.

The choice of word matters for what follows. Iblīs's objection will be about material, and the verse has already named the material in the same sentence that names the being. The declaration contains the ground of the objection, and the objection will be shown to rest on something God said openly.

The word also connects this account to the Qur'an's treatment of the messengers. The pagans objected that Muhammad was a man like themselves (38:4; cf. 17:94; 23:33), and the word *bashar* is the one used in those objections. The verse places the objection at the beginning of human history, spoken by a different objector about a different man.

**From Clay, in an Abbreviated Declaration**
The material is named without qualification in this verse, and the other accounts supply the detail. Al-Ḥijr describes it as sounding clay moulded from black mud (15:28), and al-Aʿrāf and al-Isrāʾ record Iblīs repeating the description dismissively (7:12; 17:61).

The naming is not incidental. God states the material Himself, before any objector can raise it, and the stating removes the possibility of concealment being part of the objection. Iblīs will not be exposing a hidden weakness; he will be repeating a fact that was announced.

The material also carries a meaning the commentators draw out. Clay is low, common and formed by another's hand. A being made from it has no claim to independence, and the verse's naming of the material is the first element of the chapter's answer to arrogance.

The commentators note that this verse is an abbreviated version of what God says in al-Ḥijr, where the description of the clay is fuller. The abbreviation is characteristic of the chapter's method: it gives the essential fact and leaves the detail to the parallel accounts.

The same abbreviation applies to the surrounding narrative. The exchange with the angels recorded in al-Baqarah is not given here; the account of Adam and Eve in the Garden, which follows the creation in al-Baqarah and al-Aʿrāf, is omitted entirely. The chapter takes only the refusal of Iblīs and its consequences.

The selection tells the reader what the chapter wants from the story. It is not an account of human origins; it is an account of the first refusal and of what a refusal costs. The omitted material is omitted because the sūrah's subject is elsewhere.

**Why This Story Comes Here**
The chapter has described the notables of Makkah going out and urging one another to hold fast to their gods (38:6). It has described their arrogance, their mockery of the believers and their demand for signs. It has described the two destinations and the speech of the people of the Fire.

The story of Iblīs is the account of the first instance of exactly that behaviour. A being is told something by God, judges himself superior to the object of the command, and refuses. The chapter places the archetype after the instances, and the placement allows the reader to recognise the pattern he has just been shown.

The recognition is the verse's purpose. The chapter does not say *the pagans are like Iblīs*. It tells the story, and the story does the work.

The placement also answers a question the chapter's opening raised. The notables found it strange that a man of their own city should bring a warning (38:4), and the strangeness they felt was about rank. The verse answers by going behind rank entirely, to the moment when rank was first contested and the contest was lost.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:72

> So when I have fashioned him and had a spirit of My Own ˹creation˺ breathed into him, fall down in prostration to him.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Acts of Creation**
The verse describes the making of the human being in two stages, and the two stages are what the rest of the narrative depends on. First the fashioning of the body from the clay named in the previous verse; then the breathing into it of a spirit. The human being is complete only when both have occurred.

The sequence is stated elsewhere in the Qur'an in the third person. Al-Sajdah describes God fashioning the human being and breathing into him of His spirit, and then giving hearing, sight and intellect (32:9). The two accounts agree on the order and on the fact that the fashioning alone does not complete the being.

Al-Rāzī draws the conclusion explicitly: the creation of the human being is completed only through the formation of the body and the breathing of the spirit together, which means human beings have both a body and a soul. The verse is therefore a statement about human constitution as well as a narrative step.

**"Of My Spirit"**
The phrase is the most discussed element of the verse, and the commentators approach it with a consistent concern: God's transcendence must be preserved, and nothing created can be a part of God. The explanations given all proceed from that concern.

Some gloss the spirit as God's power. Others say the spirit is not God's in the sense of being part of Him, but is attributed to Him as a way of honouring Adam. Others take it to mean the spirit that God possesses and no one else possesses. Al-Bayḍāwī makes little comment on the phrase at all.

The possessive construction is the crux. Attribution to God in the Qur'an is frequently honorific rather than constitutive — the house of God, the she-camel of God, the spirit of God. The commentators read the phrase here in the same way, and the reading preserves both the honour and the transcendence.

**Al-Rāzī on the Nature of the Soul**
Al-Rāzī takes the phrase further than most. He understands the soul as a noble, heavenly and holy substance, while maintaining firmly that it is created. The soul is subtle and luminous, and it arises as the bridge between the body and the spirit.

His position is a careful one. The soul is exalted enough to explain why the angels could be commanded to prostrate before the being who possesses it, and created enough to preserve the distinction between God and everything other than God. Both requirements are met, and the formulation became influential.

Some Sufi commentators hold the same view, that what was breathed into Adam is a created substance. Others understand the spirit within the human being as being of a supernal nature, and take that as the explanation of the command to prostrate.

**Rūzbihān al-Baqlī's Reading**
The Sufi commentator Rūzbihān al-Baqlī glosses the passage in a way that brings out its structure. He takes the clay to stand for incapacity and weakness, and the breathing of the spirit to mean being clothed in the lights of divine greatness and magnificence.

His gloss runs through the verse in order. God creates the human being from a material that signifies incapacity, and then perfects him, distinguishes him with the divine attributes, illuminates him with the light of the divine essence, and gives him life through the life and spirit that proceed from the disclosure of majesty and beauty. Only at that point does the command to prostrate arrive.

The reading is significant because it identifies the two poles the rest of the narrative will turn on. The human being is made of weakness and is honoured by an act of God. Iblīs's error will be to see only the first pole, and to treat what he sees as the whole of the case.

The gloss also preserves the order the verse insists on. The command to prostrate comes last, after the honour has been conferred. Iblīs answers as though the command had arrived before the honour, and the misreading of the sequence is the substance of his argument.

**The Command to Prostrate, and What the Verse Establishes**
The verse ends with a command addressed to the angels, and the command is unusual in its object. They are ordered to prostrate before a created being, and the prostration is directed at Adam rather than at God.

The commentators are careful about what the prostration means. It is an act of honour toward Adam and of obedience toward God, not an act of worship directed at the human being. The command comes from God, which makes the prostration an act of submission to the Commander however it is performed.

The Qur'an repeats the command in several places (2:34; 7:11; 15:29; 17:61; 18:50; 20:116), and the repetition indicates that the command is the pivot of the whole narrative. Everything that follows is a response to it, and the response divides the angels from Iblīs.

The verse establishes three facts that the following verses will use. The human being is made of clay. He is honoured by a spirit attributed to God. He is the object of a divine command to prostrate.

Iblīs will accept the first fact, dispute the second by ignoring it, and refuse the third. The verse has already given the reader everything needed to evaluate the refusal, and it gives it before the refusal occurs.

The order is deliberate. The chapter does not let the objection be raised first and then answer it; it states the grounds of the answer and then presents the objection. The reader who follows the verse knows what Iblīs is leaving out before Iblīs speaks.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:73

> So the angels prostrated all together—

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Immediate Obedience, All of Them Together**
The verse records the response to the command in a single clause, and the clause is notable for what it lacks. There is no hesitation, no question, no request for clarification and no delay. The command is given and the prostration follows.

The immediacy is the verse's subject. The angels are not described as considering the command, weighing it against their own standing, or asking why a being of clay should be honoured. They prostrate, and the verse moves on.

The brevity is also a contrast. The account of the angels' obedience takes one verse; the account of Iblīs's refusal and its consequences takes twelve. The disproportion is the chapter's way of saying where the interest lies, and where it does not.

The phrase emphasises unanimity, and the emphasis prepares the exception in the next verse. Every one of them prostrated, and the stating of the unanimity is what makes the single refusal visible.

The Qur'an uses the same emphasis elsewhere. Al-Baqarah says they all prostrated except Iblīs, who refused and acted arrogantly (2:34), and al-Aʿrāf and al-Isrāʾ repeat the formula (7:11; 17:61). The unanimity is stated in every account, and in every account it exists to be broken by one.

The word for together also carries a sense of simultaneity. They did not prostrate in sequence or by rank; they prostrated as one body. The unity of the act is part of what the refusal disrupts.

**What the Prostration Means**
The commentators are consistent that the prostration is an act of honour toward Adam and obedience toward God, not worship of the human being. The distinction matters, and the Qur'an's own treatment of it is clear: the command comes from God, and the obeying of the command is directed at Him.

The act also establishes a hierarchy that Iblīs will dispute. The angels, who are described elsewhere as not disobeying God in what He commands them and doing as they are commanded (66:6), perform an act of honour toward a being made of clay. Their standing is not diminished by it.

That point is central to the chapter's argument. The angels are higher than Adam in the order of creation, and their prostration does not lower them. The verse shows a being accepting a command that appears to reduce his standing, and the acceptance costs him nothing.

The costlessness is what Iblīs could not accept. He treated the command as a demotion, and the angels' obedience is the standing refutation: the same command, received by beings of higher rank, produced no loss. The verse therefore contains the answer to the argument that comes two verses later, and it contains it before the argument is made.

**The Verse's Function in the Narrative**
The verse exists to establish a baseline. Before the narrative can describe a refusal, it must describe the obedience that the refusal departs from. The unanimity is the norm, and the exception is measured against it.

The chapter uses the same method elsewhere. It describes the patriarchs as men of might and vision before describing anyone's failure (38:45), and it describes the righteous entering the Garden before describing the transgressors entering the Fire (38:49–58). The pattern is to establish what is right before showing what departs from it.

The method has a rhetorical effect. A reader who is shown the norm first evaluates the exception against it rather than on its own terms. Iblīs's refusal will be presented with reasons, and the verse ensures that the reasons are heard after the obedience has been established.

**The Dash That Ends the Verse**
The verse closes with a suspension rather than a full stop, and the suspension is grammatical as well as rhetorical. The sentence is completed by the next verse, which begins with the exception. The two verses form one statement split across a boundary.

The split has an effect on the reading. The unanimity is stated and left hanging, and the reader arrives at the exception with the unanimity still in mind. The construction refuses to let the two be separated, and the refusal is what the chapter wants.

The same construction appears in al-Aʿrāf and al-Isrāʾ, where the prostration and the exception are also joined in a single sentence. The Qur'an treats the unanimity and the exception as one fact rather than two, and this verse follows that pattern exactly.

**The Silence of the Angels**
Nothing is recorded as said by the angels in this account. Al-Baqarah records their question about the vicegerent (2:30), and the question is a genuine one; here they are silent, and the silence is obedience.

The silence is worth noting because the narrative is about speech. Iblīs will speak, and his speech will be an argument. The angels will not speak at all. The contrast between a reasoned refusal and a silent obedience is the chapter's sharpest statement about what reason does when it is set against a command.

The point connects to the chapter's opening. The notables of Makkah had reasons for their refusal, and the reasons were recorded at length. The angels had none, and the verse gives them none. The chapter is not impressed by the quantity of a person's reasons.

**A Verse With No Argument in It**
The verse contains no reasoning, no explanation and no justification, and the absence is the point. The angels were given no reason for the command, and they asked for none. The command was sufficient.

The chapter has been describing an audience that demanded reasons for everything and was satisfied by none. They asked for angels to be sent down, for the Book to be sent in pieces, for the punishment to be brought forward (38:16; cf. 17:90–93). Each demand was a request for a reason, and each request was followed by another.

The verse answers that pattern by showing beings who did not make it. The angels received a command whose purpose was not explained to them, concerning a being they had just questioned the wisdom of creating, and they obeyed. The obedience is the chapter's standard, and the standard is stated without commentary.

The absence of commentary is deliberate. A standard explained is a standard being defended, and the chapter is not defending it. It sets the obedience down beside the refusal that follows and lets the reader measure the distance, which is the same method it used with the two destinations.

**What the Verse Expects of the Reader**
The verse is short, and its shortness places a demand on the reader. Nothing is explained about the prostration, its meaning or its significance, and the reader must supply the understanding from the surrounding accounts and from the tradition.

The demand is deliberate. The chapter has already told the reader a great deal — about the two destinations, about the fate of the destroyed peoples, about the character of the message. The verse assumes that the reader has absorbed it, and it proceeds at the pace of someone who has.

The assumption is also a measure. A reader who has followed the chapter will find this verse sufficient; a reader who has not will find it puzzling. The chapter has been separating the two from its first verses, and this verse continues the separation without announcement.

The separation is not elitism but economy. A sūrah that re-explained its premises at every step would never reach the end of its argument, and this one has a long way to go. The verse trusts the reader with a single clause and moves on, and the trust is itself a statement about what the chapter thinks its audience has become.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:74

> but not Iblîs, who acted arrogantly, becoming unfaithful

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception, and What the Verse Does Not Explain**
The verse supplies the exception the previous verse prepared, and the exception is stated in the shortest possible form. Not Iblīs. Three words complete the deviation from the unanimity, and the naming is the first time the individual appears in the chapter.

The brevity is deliberate. The angels' obedience was described in a clause and their unanimity in a phrase; Iblīs's refusal is named and then explained. The explanation, not the refusal, is what the chapter is interested in, and it is given in the same verse.

The naming is also significant. The angels are described collectively throughout; this one is named. The individualisation marks him as a particular case rather than a category, and the particularity is what allows the chapter to examine his reasoning.

The verse does not explain why Iblīs refused. It states that he did, names the arrogance and records the resulting category, and stops. The explanation is left to the next verse, where Iblīs supplies it himself.

The leaving is significant. The chapter does not offer its own account of the refusal's origin; it lets the refuser give his own. The self-supplied explanation is the evidence, and the evidence is what the chapter wants on the record.

The same method governs the chapter's treatment of the notables. Their objections are quoted in their own words throughout the sūrah, and the chapter's comment is supplied by placement rather than by rebuttal. The verse here establishes the method at the point where it matters most.

**"He Acted Arrogantly"**
The verb is *istakbara*, from the root of greatness, and it denotes taking oneself to be great. The form indicates a deliberate act of self-elevation rather than an involuntary feeling. Iblīs did not feel superior; he made himself superior, in his own assessment.

The word is the same one the chapter used in its second verse, where the disbelievers are described as being in arrogance and schism. The repetition ties the two passages together: the fault of the sūrah's opponents is the fault of Iblīs, named with the same verb.

The connection is the chapter's central identification. It does not need to say that the pagans resemble Iblīs, because it has used the same word for both. The reader who remembers verse 2 has already made the connection when he reaches this verse.

**"And Was Among the Disbelievers"**
The second clause states the consequence of the arrogance, and the consequence is a change of category. He became one of the disbelievers — he joined a group he was not previously part of.

The wording is important. Iblīs was not a disbeliever before the command; the refusal made him one. The verse describes disbelief as an outcome of arrogance rather than as an independent state, and the ordering is the chapter's diagnosis of unbelief in general.

The phrase also has a polemical edge in its original context. The Arabs held that the jinn were beings of a different order, and some regarded them as powers to be appeased. The verse places their most prominent member in the same category as human disbelievers, and the placing removes any special standing.

The removal matters for the chapter's audience in a practical way. Some of the Quraysh sought refuge with jinn and treated them as powers with independent influence (72:6). The verse demotes the greatest of them to the rank of a disobedient creature, and the demotion removes the ground of that practice.

**Arrogance as the Root**
The verse identifies arrogance as the cause and disbelief as the result, and the identification is the narrative's thesis. Iblīs did not disbelieve because he lacked information. He had the command, the announcement and the assembly; nothing was hidden from him.

The chapter makes the same point about its own opponents. The notables were told what the message was, by whom it came and what followed from rejecting it. Their refusal was not a failure of knowledge, and the chapter never treats it as one. Verse 2 names arrogance and schism as the reason, and this verse names the same cause at the origin of the story.

The parallel is exact in structure. A command is given, a judgement about one's own standing is made, and the judgement produces the refusal. The chapter presents the archetype first in its opponents and then in its narrative, and the second presentation explains the first.

The order of the two presentations matters. A reader who met Iblīs first might treat him as a singular case; meeting the notables first, he recognises the pattern in people like himself before he meets its origin. The chapter arranges its material so that the recognition is unavoidable.

**What Iblīs Was Before**
The verse does not say what Iblīs was before the refusal, and the omission is informative. The Qur'an indicates elsewhere that he was among the jinn (18:50), and the commentators discuss at length how a jinn came to be in the company of the angels.

The discussion concerns his prior standing. He was, on the common account, one who had worshipped God for a long time and had attained a rank among the angels. The refusal therefore cost him something considerable, and the cost is what makes the arrogance remarkable.

The chapter does not narrate the prior standing, and the omission keeps the focus on the act. What Iblīs was is less relevant to the sūrah's purpose than what he did, and the verse gives only what is needed: the refusal, its cause and its consequence.

**The Verse as a Pattern**
The verse's structure — an exception, a cause, a consequence — is the pattern the chapter will apply to its own audience. The pagans are the exception to the message's acceptance, their arrogance is the cause, and their destination is the consequence.

The pattern is stated without commentary, and the absence of commentary is what makes it effective. The chapter does not draw the moral; it tells the story in a form that contains the moral, and leaves the reader to see it.

The form also answers the objection raised in verse 4. The notables found it strange that a warning should come from one of their own number. The verse shows that the first refusal came from one of the highest of created beings, and that his standing did not protect him.

**Arrogance Named Before the Reason Is Given**
The order of the two clauses deserves attention. The arrogance is named first, and the reason for the refusal comes afterwards in a separate verse. The chapter states the fault before it reports the argument, and the ordering determines how the argument will be heard.

A reader who meets the argument first may find it persuasive. A reader who has been told that arrogance is the cause will examine the argument for the arrogance, and the examination will find it. The verse arranges the reader's attention in advance.

The arrangement is the chapter's method throughout. It names the disposition of its opponents in verse 2, before reporting any of their objections, and every objection recorded afterwards is read against that naming. The verse here applies the same method to Iblīs.

The application is the chapter's most economical argument. It does not need to demonstrate that the notables' reasoning was arrogant; it shows the first arrogant reasoning in the order of creation and lets the resemblance do the work.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:75

> Allah asked, “O Iblîs! What prevented you from prostrating to what I created with My Own Hands? Did you ˹just˺ become proud? Or have you always been arrogant?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question Put to the Refuser**
The verse records God addressing Iblīs directly, and the address is a question. The question is not asked for information — nothing about the situation is hidden — and its function is to require an answer. Iblīs will be made to state his reasoning.

The requirement matters. A refusal that is not articulated can be dismissed as a lapse; a refusal that is articulated becomes a position, and a position can be examined. The verse forces the examination by asking for the reason, and the reason will be given in the next verse.

The same question appears in al-Aʿrāf in shorter form: what prevented you from prostrating when I commanded you? (7:12). The version here adds the reference to the two hands and the two further questions, and the additions are where the chapter's particular interest lies.

**"What I Created with My Two Hands"**
The phrase is the verse's most discussed element. God describes the creation of the human being as an act performed with His two hands, and the description is unique to this account.

The commentators read it symbolically, and their reasoning is explicit: creation in the Qur'an requires no instrument at all, since God's command when He wills a thing is simply to say *be*, and it is (36:82; cf. 2:117; 16:40). A description of hands must therefore signify something other than a manner of making.

Several explanations are given. Some take the phrase as a mark of the human being's greatness. Others read the two as pointing to paired divine qualities — majesty with beauty, or dominance with gentleness — and understand the human being as the place where such qualities are displayed together. Others again take the pair to cover the whole range of names that fall under dominance and under love.

The explanations differ in detail but agree on the function. The phrase singles out this act of creation from every other, and it does so in a way that no material description could.

**Why the Mention of the Hands Matters**
Whatever the phrase signifies, its function in the narrative is clear. God is distinguishing this act of creation from every other, and He is doing so in Iblīs's hearing before Iblīs has spoken.

The distinction answers the objection in advance. Iblīs's argument will rest on the material of the two beings — fire and clay. The verse has already introduced a factor that the material does not account for, and the factor is the manner and honour of the making.

The commentators note that the rhetorical question then carries a second meaning beneath the first: is Iblīs in truth a creature more exalted than this one? The question is not about the prevention but about the claim implied by it, and the claim is what the verse is exposing.

The exposure works because the question cannot be answered from the material alone. Worth in the Qur'an is never derived from substance; it is derived from what God confers. The verse introduces the conferring before the question is put, and the introduction is the answer.

**Did You Become Proud? The Question as an Offer**
The first of the two questions asks whether the arrogance is new. The verb form indicates a state entered into, and the question asks whether Iblīs has now taken on something he did not have.

The question is significant because it distinguishes two very different conditions. An arrogance newly adopted is a choice made at a moment; an arrogance long held is a settled character. The first might be corrected; the second is a nature.

The chapter is interested in the distinction because it applies to its own audience. Some of the notables had opposed the message from the beginning; others had been persuaded by their peers. The verse raises the question of when a person's arrogance began, and the raising is part of the diagnosis.

The commentators and the narrative structure both indicate that the questions are not merely interrogative. A being asked whether he has become proud is being given the opportunity to say so, and the saying would be an admission.

Al-Aʿrāf records what follows such an admission. There God says to Iblīs: it is not for you to be arrogant here, so go out, you are among the humbled (7:13). The expulsion follows the identification of the arrogance, and the identification is what the questions in this verse are seeking.

The chapter's version delays the expulsion by one verse, and the delay allows Iblīs to state his reasoning in full. The reasoning is what the chapter wants on the record, because it is the reasoning its own audience is using.

**"Or Have You Always Been Arrogant?"**
The second question uses a different construction, one that denotes a continuing state, and it asks whether Iblīs belongs to the category of the exalted. The phrase can be read as asking whether he has placed himself among the high, or whether he has always been of that rank.

The two questions together cover the whole field. Either the arrogance is new or it is old; either it was adopted at this moment or it has been the disposition all along. There is no third possibility, and Iblīs's answer will show which of the two he has chosen.

The alternative he chooses is neither, and that is the point. His answer in the next verse is a claim about material superiority, which is a third thing — an argument rather than a confession. The verse has offered him two ways of describing his state and he will supply a justification instead.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:76

> He replied, “I am better than he is: You created me from fire and him from clay.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Argument in the Qur'an's History**
The verse records the answer, and the answer is an argument. Iblīs does not confess, does not ask for clarification and does not submit. He reasons, and the reasoning is offered as a sufficient ground for disobeying a command.

The place of the verse in the Qur'an's narrative order gives it a particular weight. This is the first recorded act of disobedience, and the form it takes is a comparison. The whole of the opposition the chapter has been describing — the notables' claims about their own standing, their contempt for the poor believers, their demand for a messenger of higher rank — is contained in this sentence.

The form of the argument is also worth naming. It is an inference from origin to worth, and the inference is the error. Nothing in the command referred to origin; the command referred to obedience.

**"I Am Better Than He Is"**
The claim is stated absolutely and without qualification. Not better in some respect, not better for some purpose — better. The superlative is asserted as a fact about the two beings rather than as an opinion about them.

The assertion precedes the evidence, and the order is characteristic of the reasoning the chapter has been examining. A conclusion is stated, and a reason is produced for it. The reason does not generate the conclusion; it is selected to support it.

The commentators note that the same explicit argument appears in al-Aʿrāf, where Iblīs says *I am better than him* in identical terms (7:12). In al-Ḥijr the argument is implicit, expressed through a dismissal of Adam's material (15:33), and in al-Isrāʾ it takes the form of a question: shall I prostrate to one whom You created from clay? (17:61). All four versions contain the same inference.

The fourfold repetition is itself evidence about the argument's importance. A point the Qur'an makes once is a remark; a point it makes in four separate accounts of the same story is a diagnosis it wants fixed in the reader's mind. The verse is not merely reporting what Iblīs said; it is identifying the reasoning the chapter's own audience was using.

**"You Created Me from Fire"**
The first premise concerns Iblīs's own origin, and the premise is true. God did create him from fire, and the Qur'an states it elsewhere: the jinn was created from a smokeless flame (55:15). The verse is not disputing a fact.

The truth of the premise is what makes the argument dangerous. An argument built on a falsehood can be refuted by correcting the fact; an argument built on a true fact must be refuted by examining the inference. The chapter's method throughout has been the second, and this verse is its archetype.

The premise also reveals the standard being applied. Iblīs measures worth by material, and the material hierarchy he assumes places fire above clay. The assumption is never argued for in the verse; it is presented as self-evident, which is how the notables of Makkah presented their own assumptions about rank.

**And Him from Clay: What the Argument Leaves Out**
The second premise is also true, and God Himself stated it in the previous verse. Iblīs is repeating an announcement rather than disclosing anything, and the repetition is the whole of his contribution.

The point is significant for the chapter's account of its opponents. The notables said of the Prophet that he was a man like themselves, eating food and walking in the markets (25:7; 38:4). Every element of that statement was accurate. The error was not in the description but in what was inferred from it.

The verse therefore isolates the mechanism of the error. True premises, a hidden standard, and a conclusion that does not follow. The chapter will spend its remaining verses showing what follows from the conclusion instead.

The omission is the verse's sharpest feature. Iblīs says nothing about the spirit breathed into Adam, nothing about the command, and nothing about the honour God conferred by making the prostration obligatory.

The previous verse had supplied all three. God announced the material, described the fashioning and the breathing of the spirit, and issued the command to the assembly. Iblīs takes the first element and ignores the other two, and the ignoring is what the chapter identifies as arrogance.

The commentators draw the conclusion in the form of a question: is Iblīs truly a creation more exalted than this one? The question cannot be answered from the material alone, and the material is all the argument contains. The verse shows an inference being drawn from part of the available evidence.

**The Verse and the Chapter's Opponents**
The correspondence between this argument and the argument of the notables is close enough that the chapter does not need to state it. They asked whether God had favoured the poor believers out of all people (38:6; cf. 6:53), which is the same inference from standing to worth. They asked why the revelation did not come to a great man of the two cities (43:31), which is the same inference applied to the choice of a messenger.

The verse is placed at the end of the sūrah so that the reader arrives at it after seeing the argument in operation. The identification is made by placement rather than by statement, and the placement is the chapter's most economical device.

What the verse adds is the outcome. The argument is answered in the next verse not by rebuttal but by expulsion, and the expulsion is the chapter's comment on the value of the reasoning.

The comment is severe precisely because it is silent. An argument that is refuted has been taken seriously; an argument that is answered by removal has been judged not to require a reply. The chapter has been treating its opponents' claims the same way from the beginning, answering them with the stories of the prophets rather than with counter-claims.

**Why the Argument Persuades**
The verse deserves attention for the strength of its appeal. The inference from origin to worth is not obviously absurd; it is the inference most people make most of the time, and it governs the social world the sūrah's audience lived in.

That is what makes the verse dangerous rather than merely wrong. A reader who dismisses it as foolish has missed its force. The argument works because the standard it applies is the standard the world actually uses, and the world's use of it is what the chapter is contesting.

The contest is conducted by substitution rather than by refutation. The chapter does not argue that fire is not better than clay; it introduces a different measure entirely, one based on what God confers rather than on what a thing is made of. The verse states the worldly measure, and the surrounding narrative replaces it.

The replacement is what the reader is meant to carry out of the sūrah. The notables measured the Messenger by his standing in Makkah; the verse shows the first being to measure by origin, and shows where the measuring ended. The two measures are not compared, because only one of them survived contact with the command.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:77

> Allah commanded, “Then get out of Paradise, for you are truly cursed

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Answer That Is Not a Rebuttal**
The verse records God's response to the argument, and the response does not engage with the argument. There is no correction of the premises, no examination of the inference and no discussion of the standard applied. The response is an expulsion.

The form of the answer is the chapter's most striking rhetorical choice. A reader might expect the reasoning to be met with reasoning, and the expectation is disappointed deliberately. The argument is not shown to be wrong; it is shown to be irrelevant, and irrelevance is demonstrated by what follows from it.

The particle at the start of the command carries a sense of consequence. Get out, then — the expulsion follows from the claim. The claim established a self-assessment, and the expulsion is the assessment's result.

**"Get Out of It"**
The command is expulsion from a place, and the place is Paradise. The commentators note that the same command is given in al-Aʿrāf with an additional phrase: get out of it, disgraced and banished (7:18). Al-Ḥijr gives the command in identical words to this verse (15:34).

The expulsion has a specific significance given what Iblīs claimed. He asserted that he was better, and the response removes him from the place where the comparison was being made. The assembly before which he elevated himself is the assembly he is expelled from, and the expulsion is witnessed by the same beings.

The command also reverses the direction of the prostration. Iblīs was ordered to lower himself before Adam; he is now ordered to leave the place where Adam is. The refusal to descend is answered by a removal, and the removal is the same movement without the choice.

The loss of the choice is the punishment's core. What he refused to do voluntarily he now does necessarily, and the necessity is imposed by the one whose command he treated as optional. The verse states the reversal in four words and leaves the reader to supply its weight.

**"You Are Truly Cursed"**
The word used denotes one who is expelled, banished and cast away from good. The curse is not primarily a wish for harm but a statement of removal, and the statement is consistent with the command that precedes it.

The emphasis particle makes the statement definitive. Truly cursed — not conditionally, not provisionally, not subject to revision. The verse states a settled condition, and the next verse will give its duration.

The commentators connect the term to its use elsewhere in the Qur'an, where it describes those removed from God's mercy. The connection matters because it identifies what has been lost. Iblīs did not lose a position; he lost access, and the loss is what the following verses will describe him trying to negotiate.

The negotiation is the tell. A being who had lost nothing but rank would not ask for anything; a being who has lost access asks for time, and time is the only remaining route back to what was lost. The verse identifies the loss precisely, and the next verses confirm the identification by what is requested.

**The Verse's Place in the Parallel Accounts**
The commentators note that these verses are identical to 15:34–35, and the identity is exact in both wording and order. The two accounts give the expulsion and the curse in the same terms, which indicates that the chapter is following a fixed formulation.

The parallel accounts differ elsewhere. Al-Aʿrāf adds the phrase about disgrace and banishment (7:18), and al-Isrāʾ records a longer exchange in which Iblīs asks about the one honoured above him (17:62). This chapter's version is the shortest, and the shortness keeps the focus on the expulsion itself.

The consistency across accounts has a function of its own. A story told four times in the same words at its critical point is a story the Qur'an wants fixed in the memory, and the fixing is what allows the chapter to place it at the end without explanation.

**What the Expulsion Establishes**
The verse establishes that the argument had a consequence, and that the consequence was immediate. There is no interval between the claim and the expulsion, and no opportunity to revise the claim.

The immediacy answers the situation the chapter has been describing. The notables had been making their argument for years, and the Qur'an had been answering it with narrative and description. The verse shows what the argument produces when the answer is complete, and the showing is a warning.

The warning is not stated as one. The chapter does not say *this is what will happen to you*. It tells the story, and the story contains the sequence: a claim about one's own standing, and a removal that follows from it.

The withholding of an explicit warning is also a judgement on the audience. A reader who cannot see himself in the sequence is not going to be reached by a statement of it, and the chapter has already tried statement. What remains is recognition, and recognition cannot be commanded.

**The Refusal to Argue, and What Is Lost and Remains**
The most instructive feature of the verse is what God does not do. He does not dispute the comparison, and the not disputing is a statement about the comparison's worth.

The chapter has been making the same point throughout. The notables' claims about rank, wealth and standing were never answered on their own terms. The chapter answered them with the stories of the prophets, with the account of the two destinations, and with the declaration of God's oneness. The claims were bypassed rather than refuted.

The bypass is the chapter's method and its judgement. An argument built on a standard that God has not set does not require refutation; it requires the standard to be named, and the naming is what the declaration in verses 65–66 performed.

The verse takes away a place and confers a curse, and it leaves Iblīs's existence intact. He is not annihilated, and the next verses show him speaking and asking. The loss is of access, not of being.

The distinction is important to the chapter's account of the world its audience inhabits. A being who was destroyed at the moment of refusal would leave no temptation behind him; a being who is expelled and cursed but alive remains an actor, and the Qur'an describes him as an active one throughout.

The verse therefore sets up the problem the rest of the narrative addresses. Something harmful remains in the world after the judgement has been passed, and the next verses will describe both its scope and its limits.

**The Public Character of the Expulsion**
The expulsion happens before the assembly that witnessed the refusal, and the publicity is part of the punishment. Iblīs elevated himself before the angels, and he is removed before them. The audience of the claim becomes the audience of the consequence.

The Qur'an's accounts elsewhere give the same detail. Al-Aʿrāf adds that he is to go out disgraced and banished (7:18), and the two words describe a condition that is visible to others. The disgrace is not private, and the banishment is not quiet.

The publicity also serves the chapter's purpose. The reader is being shown a scene with witnesses, and the witnesses matter because the scene is meant to be recognised. A private fall would be a story about one being; a witnessed fall is a pattern available for inspection.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:78

> And surely upon you is My condemnation until the Day of Judgment.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Duration Attached to the Curse**
The verse extends the curse of the previous verse by giving it a term, and the term is the Day of Judgment. The condemnation is not open-ended in the sense of indefinite; it is bounded by an event, and the event is the one the chapter has already described.

The specification changes the character of the curse. An unspecified curse could be lifted at any time; a curse with a stated terminus cannot be lifted before it. The verse removes the possibility of an early release, and the removal is what drives Iblīs's request in the next verse.

The connection to the chapter's eschatology is direct. The Day of Reckoning was named earlier in the chapter as the appointed occasion for both destinations (38:53), and the verse attaches the curse to the same occasion. Everything in the chapter converges on that day.

**"My Condemation"**
The condemnation is attributed to God in the first person, and the attribution is emphatic. It is not a curse pronounced by the angels, not a natural consequence, not a condition that arose from the act alone. It is God's, and it rests upon him.

The preposition used indicates something lying upon a person, a weight that is carried. The condemnation is described as a burden borne rather than as a penalty applied from outside, and the description matches the Qur'an's treatment of burdens elsewhere (29:13; 35:18).

The first-person attribution also answers Iblīs's claim. He asserted a comparison between two created beings; the verse answers with a statement from the Creator about one of them. The comparison is not disputed — it is replaced by a relation of an entirely different kind.

**Why the Term Matters to the Narrative**
The stated terminus is what produces the next three verses. Iblīs hears that the condemnation lasts until the Day of Judgment, and he asks for respite until the day of resurrection. The request is a response to the term, and the term is therefore a narrative device as well as a statement.

The commentators note the distinction between the two days. The Day of Judgment is the day of universal resurrection and reckoning; Iblīs's request refers to the same event from the perspective of the dead being raised. The two expressions refer to one occasion, and the request is for the whole interval.

The request is also a recognition. By asking for respite until that day, Iblīs accepts the term. He does not dispute the condemnation or ask for its removal; he asks only for the interval, and the asking concedes everything the verse asserted.

That concession is the hinge on which the rest of the narrative turns. Everything Iblīs does afterwards — the oath, the strategy, the exception he makes for the sincere — happens inside an interval he obtained by asking. The verse establishes that the whole of his subsequent activity is permitted rather than seized.

**A Term That Cannot Be Shortened, in the Parallel Accounts**
Al-Ḥijr gives the same statement in identical words (15:35), and the commentators note the identity explicitly. Al-Aʿrāf's version places the condemnation within a longer exchange but retains the same substance (7:18).

The consistency indicates a fixed element of the narrative. Across four accounts the expulsion, the curse and its duration are given in the same terms, and the variation occurs in the surrounding material rather than in the sentence itself.

The fixed character of the sentence has a function in this chapter. The sūrah is the last to tell the story, and it can therefore rely on what the earlier accounts established. The verse states the term without elaboration because the elaboration has already been given.

The verse fixes the term and fixes it in a way that leaves no room for negotiation. The condemnation lasts until the Day of Judgment, and the fixing is what makes the following request necessary.

The necessity is instructive. Iblīs does not ask for the condemnation to be lifted or for its content to be changed. He asks only about time, and the asking shows that he has accepted everything the verse asserted. The concession is complete and is made without being stated.

The chapter makes the same point about its opponents in a different register. They are told repeatedly that the term is set and cannot be advanced or delayed (38:16; cf. 14:44; 16:61). The verse shows the first opponent discovering the same fact.

**What the Condemnation Does Not Remove**
The verse is notable for what it leaves intact. Iblīs is condemned and expelled, but he is not destroyed. The next verses will show him speaking, requesting and receiving an answer, which means the condemnation is compatible with continued existence and activity.

The distinction matters for the chapter's account of the world. The Qur'an describes Iblīs as active among human beings — making things seem fair, whispering, promising falsely (6:43; 16:63; 14:22; 4:120). The verse explains how that activity is possible: the condemnation has a term, and the term has not arrived.

The activity is also limited, and the limit is given in the verses that follow. Iblīs will be granted respite but not authority, and the distinction between the two is the chapter's account of how temptation works.

The distinction is what protects the reader from two errors at once. If the adversary had no respite, temptation would be impossible and the test would be meaningless; if he had authority, resistance would be futile and the test would be rigged. The verse holds the two apart, and the holding is the whole of its reassurance.

**The Condemnation and the Chapter's Opponents**
The verse's term connects Iblīs's fate to the fate the chapter has already described. The transgressors were promised the worst destination on the Day of Reckoning (38:53, 55), and the condemnation upon Iblīs runs to the same day.

The convergence is the chapter's way of joining the narrative to the eschatology. The story of Adam is not a separate account; it is the origin of the enmity that the two destinations resolve. The verse supplies the link by giving both the same terminus.

The link also answers the mockery of verse 16. The pagans asked to have their portion hastened before the Day of Reckoning, and the verse shows that the one who first made the claim they are making has been waiting for that day since the beginning.

**Why the Duration Is Stated Here**
The parallel accounts do not all give the duration in the same place, and this chapter states it as a separate verse. The separation gives the term a weight it would not carry as a clause attached to the command.

The weight matters because the term is what the rest of the narrative turns on. Verses 79 to 85 concern the interval — the request for it, the granting of it, the oath made within it, and the threat that closes it. The verse establishes the interval before any of that occurs.

The establishment also frames the reader's own position. He is inside the interval the verse describes, and the interval has an end. The framing is not stated as a warning, but it functions as one.

The framing also explains why the chapter can afford to be unhurried. It has spent sixteen verses on the two destinations and fifteen on the story of Adam, and the unhurried pace is available only to a reader who still has time. The verse supplies the fact that makes the pace meaningful rather than indulgent.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:79

> Satan appealed, “My Lord! Then delay my end until the Day of their resurrection.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Request From the Condemned**
The verse records Iblīs speaking again, and the speech is a supplication. The one who has just been expelled and cursed addresses God as *my Lord* and asks for something, and the asking is granted in the verses that follow.

The form of the address is worth noting. The lordship acknowledged is Iblīs's own relation to God, not a general truth. He has refused a command and been condemned for it, and his next act is to appeal to the same authority he refused. The verse is showing that the refusal was never about God's authority.

The commentators note that despite the banishment, Iblīs requests and is granted respite from God, so that he will neither die nor be punished until the day of resurrection. The granting is the verse's most consequential fact, and the next two verses will state it.

The fact is consequential for the reader rather than for the narrative. Everything the chapter has described about temptation presupposes an adversary who is still operating, and the verse supplies the reason he is. Without the grant there would be no test, and without a test the two destinations would have nothing to distribute.

**Delay My End, and What the Request Sets Up**
The word used denotes being given respite, a postponement of a term. Iblīs is asking for time, and the time he asks for is not indefinite — it is bounded by the day he has just heard named.

The request concedes the condemnation. A being who disputed the curse would ask for its removal; Iblīs asks only for the interval before it takes full effect. The concession is complete, and it is made in the act of asking.

The request also reveals what he intends to do with the time. The verses that follow supply the intention explicitly, and the intention is stated as an oath. The respite is asked for in order to be used, and the verse is the beginning of that use.

The verse is the hinge of the narrative's second half. Everything before it concerned the refusal and its immediate consequence; everything after it concerns the interval and what happens in it.

The chapter's interest is in the interval, because the interval is where its audience lives. The pagans of Makkah are inside the period Iblīs asked for, and their opposition to the message is part of what the interval contains. The verse explains the situation the whole sūrah has been describing.

The explanation is not an excuse. The Qur'an is careful throughout to state that Iblīs has no authority over God's servants (17:65), and the chapter will make the same point in the verses that follow. The request is granted, and the grant is limited.

**"Until the Day They Are Resurrected"**
The term requested is the day of universal resurrection, and the commentators connect it to the same event named in al-Aʿrāf (7:14) and al-Ḥijr (15:36–38). The three accounts give the request in nearly identical words, and al-Ḥijr's version of the answer is repeated verbatim from this chapter.

The choice of the resurrection as the terminus is significant. Iblīs does not ask for a fixed number of years or for an indefinite reprieve; he asks for the whole period that ends with the resurrection. The request covers the entire duration of human history.

The coverage is what makes the chapter's account of temptation possible. Human beings live inside the interval Iblīs requested, and the interval is bounded at both ends — by the expulsion at one end and by the resurrection at the other.

**The Verse as a Recognition of God's Power**
The request contains an implicit acknowledgement that is easy to miss. Iblīs asks God to delay his end, which means he accepts that God controls it. The being who claimed to be better than Adam accepts that his own existence is not in his hands.

The acknowledgement contradicts the argument of verse 76. A being whose worth derives from his material would not need to ask anyone for time. The verse shows the argument abandoned in practice while it is still being defended in principle, and the contradiction is the chapter's diagnosis of it.

The contradiction is also visible in the chapter's opponents. The notables claimed independence and standing, and the Qur'an answers by asking who provides for them and who controls life and death (10:31; 29:61–63). The answers they would give contradict the claims they make, and the verse is the archetype of the contradiction.

The archetype is useful because it is involuntary. Iblīs does not intend to concede anything; the concession happens in the grammar of his request. A reader who notices that will notice the same thing in his own speech about the matters he considers settled.

**The Appeal of a Condemned Being**
The verse's strangeness is its final feature. A being who has been expelled, cursed and given a fixed term of condemnation is shown appealing to the one who condemned him, and the appeal is heard.

The hearing is not a reversal. Nothing in the following verses restores Iblīs's position or lifts the condemnation. The respite is granted within the condemnation rather than instead of it, and the granting demonstrates a fact the chapter has been asserting throughout: God's control extends over the one who refuses Him.

That fact is the chapter's answer to its opponents. The notables supposed that their opposition placed them outside God's reach. The verse shows the first opponent requesting something from God and receiving it, which locates him inside the reach he supposed he had left.

**The Request and the Requests of the Pagans**
The verse has a close parallel in the behaviour the chapter described at its opening. The pagans asked the Prophet to hasten the punishment and to produce signs on demand (38:16). Iblīs asks God for time. Both are requests made to an authority the requester is defying.

The parallel extends to the form. Each request assumes that the one being defied controls what is being asked for, and each request concedes the control it ignores in the defiance. Iblīs asks for delay from the one whose command he refused; the pagans ask for a sign from the messenger whose message they reject.

The chapter records both and answers neither with a rebuttal. The answer in each case is a statement of what actually happens: the respite is granted and bounded, the punishment is deferred and fixed. The requests are heard, and the hearing changes nothing about the outcome.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:80

> Allah said, “You will be delayed

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sentence Left Incomplete**
The verse records the answer to Iblīs's request, and the answer is given in a form that requires the next verse to complete it. You will be delayed — and the sentence stops. The term of the delay is supplied in verse 81, and the two verses form a single statement.

The splitting is not accidental. The commentators note that verses 80 and 81 are repeated verbatim in al-Ḥijr (15:37–38), which means the division belongs to the Qur'an's own arrangement rather than to this chapter's composition. The same break occurs in both accounts.

The break has an effect on the reading. The granting is stated before its limit, and the reader holds the grant for a moment before learning what bounds it. The construction gives the grant and the limit equal weight.

**The Request Is Granted**
The first fact of the verse is that the request is answered affirmatively. Iblīs asked for respite and receives it, and the receiving is stated without qualification in this verse.

The granting has been much discussed. The commentators explain that the respite means Iblīs will neither die nor be punished until the appointed day, and the explanation accounts for the Qur'an's consistent presentation of him as active throughout human history.

The granting is also an exercise of the same authority Iblīs refused. The being who declined to obey a command is given what he asks for by the one he declined to obey, and the giving demonstrates that the refusal changed nothing about the relation.

The demonstration is aimed at the chapter's audience as much as at the narrative. The notables treated their rejection of the message as a withdrawal from an arrangement. The verse shows the first rejecter still asking, still receiving and still bound, which is the answer to that treatment.

**Why the Grant Is Given**
The Qur'an does not give a reason for the granting, and the absence of a reason has been read in several ways. The commentators note that from one perspective, while Iblīs misleads people, he is in fact bringing about the end God has decreed for those destined for the Fire.

That reading is supported by the wider account. God says to Iblīs in al-Isrāʾ: incite whoever you can of them with your voice, mobilise against them your cavalry and infantry, manipulate them in their wealth and children, and make them promises (17:64). The commands are permissive in form and describe an activity that serves a purpose Iblīs does not control.

The chapter here does not develop the point, and the omission keeps the focus on the grant itself. The reader is told that the respite is given and that it has a term, and the next verses will supply what Iblīs intends to do with it.

The restraint is consistent with the chapter's handling of the whole narrative. It gives the facts of the grant and leaves the theology of permission to the reader's own reflection, which is the same method it used with the two destinations and with the story of the litigants.

**A Grant That Is Also a Limit, and Its Place in the Argument**
The chapter has been describing a world in which the message is opposed, the believers are mocked and the transgressors demand that the punishment be hastened. The verse explains the condition of that world: it is a period of granted respite.

The explanation does not reduce responsibility, and the chapter is careful about this. Iblīs will state in the next verses that he has no power over God's sincere servants, and the Qur'an says elsewhere that he has no authority over the faithful (17:65). The respite creates an opportunity; it does not create a compulsion.
