The distinction is the chapter's account of temptation. Something is presented, and a choice is made. The presentation is permitted; the choice belongs to the person. The verse supplies the first half of that account.

The verse can be read as a concession and as a restriction at the same time, and the two readings do not conflict. Iblīs receives what he asked for, and what he receives is bounded by a day he does not choose.

The Qur'an's parallel formulation makes the restriction explicit. Al-Ḥijr gives the same two verses in identical words (15:37–38), and the identity indicates that the grant and its limit were transmitted together as one unit. The commentators note the repetition specifically.

The pairing is the chapter's model for every permission it describes. The transgressors are given time, the messengers are given a message, the believers are given provision, and each gift carries its term. The verse states the pattern in its clearest form.

**The Delay as a Test**
The commentators connect the respite to the testing of human beings. A world in which the first opponent remains active is a world in which the choice is real, and the reality of the choice is what makes the two destinations meaningful.

The chapter has already described those destinations in detail, and the description assumed a choice. The verse supplies the condition that makes the choice possible, and the supplying comes after the description rather than before it.

The order is significant. The reader learns what is at stake before learning how the stakes came to be in play. The chapter's method throughout has been to give the consequence first and the origin second, and this verse is the last instance of that method.

The method suits the subject. A test is understood properly only by someone who already knows what passing and failing mean, and the chapter supplies the meanings before it describes the test. The respite is the test, and the two destinations were its marking scheme.

**The Unfinished Sentence**
The verse's incompleteness is its most memorable feature, and the feature has a function. A grant stated without its limit could be taken as absolute; the break forces the reader to wait for the limit, and the waiting establishes that the limit is not an afterthought.

The next verse supplies the term — until the Day of the Moment Known — and the term is fixed by God rather than by Iblīs. The request asked for the day of resurrection; the answer gives the appointed day, and the change of formulation transfers the determination from the requester to the granter.

The transfer is the verse's quiet point. Iblīs asked for time, and time is given, but the measure of the time is not his. The chapter has been making the same statement about its opponents since its opening verses: what they have is on loan, and the term of the loan is set elsewhere.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:81

> until the appointed Day.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Term Is Named**
The verse completes the sentence the previous verse left open, and the completion is three words. You will be delayed — until the appointed Day. The grant is given and its limit is given, and the limit occupies the whole of the second verse.

The brevity of the verse is not a shortage of material. The chapter has been giving its most important statements in short form throughout, and the pattern holds here. The declaration of God's oneness was compressed into a single sentence; the condemnation of Iblīs into two; the term of his respite into three words.

The compression has an effect on the reader. A short statement cannot be argued with, and the verse does not invite argument. It states a date, and the date is the last fact the narrative needs.

**"Appointed" and What It Excludes**
The word used denotes something whose time has been fixed and recorded, and the fixing excludes several possibilities at once. The day cannot be brought forward, cannot be postponed, and cannot be renegotiated by the one who asked for the interval.

The exclusion is directed at the request that produced it. Iblīs asked for respite until the day of resurrection, and the answer gives him the appointed day. The substitution of terms is small in wording and large in effect: the requester named the occasion, and the granter names it again in a formulation that makes the naming His own.

The Qur'an uses the same word elsewhere for the fixed terms of created things, and the usage is consistent. A thing whose time is appointed has a term set by someone other than itself, and the verse applies that structure to the whole interval Iblīs has been given.

The application is the verse's theological content in a single word. Whatever Iblīs does inside the interval, he does as a creature with a term, and a creature with a term cannot be an independent agent. The oath he swears in the next verse is therefore made from inside a limit he has just acknowledged.

**A Day Known to God Alone**
The commentators connect the phrase to the description given in the parallel accounts, and they note that the day is known — fixed, recorded and certain — while remaining unknown to the one whose term it is. The two features are held together in a single word.

The combination is what gives the verse its weight. A term that is fixed but undisclosed cannot be planned around. Iblīs knows that his respite ends, and he does not know when the ending arrives relative to any moment he is living through.

The same structure governs the human situation the chapter has been describing. The Qur'an states repeatedly that the Hour is fixed and that its knowledge rests with God alone (7:187; 33:63), and the verse places Iblīs inside that condition rather than outside it.

**Two Verses Forming One Sentence, and What the Term Means**
The verse cannot be read alone, and its dependence on the previous verse is grammatical as well as rhetorical. The grant is stated in verse 80 and bounded in verse 81, and the two form a single declaration split across a boundary.

The split gives the limit an emphasis it would not carry as a clause. A reader encounters the grant, holds it for a moment, and then receives its boundary. The construction makes the boundary arrive as a separate fact rather than as a qualification.

The chapter uses the same device elsewhere. The description of the two destinations was built by pairing verses that complete one another, and the pairing is a consistent feature of the sūrah's composition.

The verse describes the boundary of an interval, and the reader is living inside the interval. Human history as the Qur'an presents it is the period Iblīs requested, and the verse gives its far end.

The giving is not a threat stated as one. The chapter does not say *you too have a term*. It states the term of the first opponent and leaves the reader to notice that his own situation has the same shape.

The noticing is the verse's purpose. A person who knows that a permission has a ceiling acts differently from one who believes the permission is open-ended, and the difference is the whole of what the chapter has been asking for since its opening verses.

**The Same Words in Al-Ḥijr**
The commentators note that verses 80 and 81 are repeated word for word in al-Ḥijr (15:37–38). The identity is exact, including the division between the two verses, which means the arrangement belongs to the Qur'an's own transmission rather than to this chapter's composition.

The repetition serves a function. The story of Adam is told in several places with variation in detail, and the variation allows each account to emphasise what its sūrah requires. The elements that do not vary are the ones the Qur'an wants fixed, and the grant with its term is among them.

Al-Aʿrāf gives the same grant in different words and adds the specification that the respite runs to the day of resurrection (7:14–15). The three accounts agree on the substance and differ in formulation, which is the normal pattern of Qur'anic repetition.

The pattern is worth naming because it answers an objection sometimes raised against repetition in the Qur'an. Where the wording is identical, the matter is being fixed; where it varies, the matter is being turned to face a different context. The verse belongs to the first group, and the fixity is why it can be this short.

**An Interval With Two Ends**
The verse supplies the second end of a period whose first end was given in the narrative of the expulsion. Between the command to leave and the appointed day lies the whole of human history, and the verse marks its far boundary.

The marking has a consequence for how the interval is understood. A period with two fixed ends is not a neutral stretch of time; it is an allowance. The verse is describing a gift with a term attached, and the term is what makes the gift a test.

The Qur'an uses the same structure for the human lifespan. Every soul has a term that cannot be advanced or delayed by a single hour (10:49; 16:61), and the verse applies the structure to the adversary as well. He is not outside the order he is trying to subvert.

The inclusion is the verse's quiet rebuke. Iblīs believed his standing set him apart from the arrangement governing other creatures; the verse places him inside it without comment. A being with a term is a creature, and a creature with a term has no ground on which to claim superiority over another.

**Why the Chapter Ends the Narrative Here**
The verse completes the story of Adam and Iblīs, and it completes it with a date rather than with an event. The narrative does not end with a resolution but with a term, and the term is what the following verses will address.

The choice is deliberate. An ending in resolution would close the matter; an ending in a term leaves it open, and the openness is the condition in which the chapter's audience lives. The verse leaves the reader inside the story rather than outside it.

That placement is the chapter's final rhetorical act. The narrative is not a story about the past but a description of the present arrangement, and the present arrangement has a date on it.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:82

> Satan said, “By Your Glory! I will certainly mislead them all

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Oath Sworn by God's Might**
The verse records Iblīs's response to the grant, and the response begins with an oath. He swears by God's might — the same attribute he was condemned under, and the same one the chapter named in its declaration (38:66).

The oath is a strange act in context. A being who has been expelled and cursed swears by the power of the one who expelled him, and the swearing acknowledges exactly what his refusal denied. The verse is showing an admission made in the form of a defiance.

The Qur'an's parallel account records the oath in slightly different terms. Al-Ḥijr has Iblīs saying *my Lord, for allowing me to stray I will surely tempt them on earth* (15:39), which frames the same intention as a response to the respite. This chapter's version leads with the oath, and the leading emphasises the appeal to divine might.

The emphasis suits this sūrah's argument. Its opponents measured power by wealth and tribal strength, and the chapter answers by locating might in God alone (38:66). The verse has the adversary appeal to that same might, which places him on the same side of the question as the chapter and against his own claim.

**I Will Mislead Them: Why the Intention Is Recorded**
The verb denotes causing someone to go astray, to lose the way, to err. It is not the word for compelling or forcing, and the distinction is the verse's most important feature.

The distinction matters for the chapter's account of responsibility. A being who compels removes the choice; a being who misleads leaves the choice intact and works on it. The verse describes the second, and the description is what makes the following verses coherent.

The Qur'an states the point explicitly elsewhere. Iblīs will say on the Day of Judgment that he had no authority over people, that he only called them and they responded (14:22). The verse here records the intention in the same terms the later confession will use.

The verse records an intention rather than an act, and the recording has a purpose. A reader who knows the plan can recognise its execution, and the recognition is a protection.

The chapter has been supplying protections of this kind throughout. It described the two destinations so that the reader would know what is at stake; it told the story of Adam so that the reader would know where the enmity began; it now states the adversary's declared intention so that the reader will know what to look for.

The stating is the opposite of the adversary's method. He makes things seem fair; the verse makes the making seem plain. The chapter does not disguise the intention or soften it, and the plainness is the answer to the disguise.

**"Them All"**
The scope of the intention is universal, and the universality is stated without qualification. Not some of them, not the weak among them — all of them. The claim is as broad as the population it concerns.

The breadth is what the next verse will narrow, and the narrowing comes from Iblīs himself. The structure of the two verses is a claim followed by its own exception, and the exception is the more informative of the two.

The claim also reveals something about the claimant. A being who has just been defeated announces an intention that exceeds his power, and the announcing is the arrogance named in verse 74 continuing after the condemnation. The verse shows the fault surviving its consequence.

The survival is the chapter's sharpest point about arrogance. Punishment did not correct it, information did not correct it, and expulsion did not correct it. The fault was never a mistake about facts, and the verse records it operating at full strength after every fact had been supplied.

**The Oath as a Strategy**
The verse describes an intention formed in response to a grant, and the response is immediate. Iblīs does not use the respite to reconsider; he uses it to plan, and the plan is stated in the same breath as the receiving of it.

The commentators note that the Qur'an describes this activity in several ways. Iblīs makes things seem fair to human beings (6:43; 16:63; 27:24; 29:38), whispers stealthily (114:4), deludes (31:33; 35:5; 57:14), enjoins indecency (2:268; 24:21) and makes false promises (4:120; 14:22). The verse is the origin of all those descriptions.

The listing is worth setting beside the oath. The oath is a single sentence; the activity it announces is described in a dozen ways across the Qur'an. The gap between the brevity of the intention and the variety of its execution is the chapter's account of how temptation actually works.

**What the Oath Concedes**
The oath swears by God's might, and the swearing concedes that the might is real and that it exceeds his own. A being who believed himself independent would not swear by another's power.

The concession is the same one visible in the previous verse's request. Iblīs asks God for time and swears by God's might, and both acts locate him inside a relation he has refused. The chapter has been making this point since verse 74: the refusal changes nothing about the dependence.

The point answers the chapter's opponents directly. The notables supposed their standing placed them beyond reach, and the verse shows the first being to suppose it swearing by the power he supposed he had escaped.

The oath is therefore a confession in the grammar of defiance. Its words claim independence; its form admits dependence. The chapter records both and lets the reader weigh them, and the weighing is the same one the notables were being asked to perform about themselves.

**The Oath and the Method**
The verse's oath is followed in the parallel accounts by a description of the method. Al-Ḥijr has Iblīs saying he will make things seem fair to human beings on earth and mislead them all together (15:39), and the two elements — beautification and misdirection — are the Qur'an's standard account of temptation.

The beautification comes first, and the order matters. Nothing is presented as evil; the evil is presented as attractive. The verse here states the aim, and the parallel account states the technique, and the technique is what the reader needs in order to recognise the aim being pursued.

The chapter has already shown the technique at work. The notables described devotion to their gods as loyalty to their fathers and their community (38:6–7), which is a fair-seeming presentation of a refusal. The verse names the mechanism that produced it.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:83

> except Your chosen servants among them.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Exception Made by the Adversary**
The verse records Iblīs narrowing his own claim, and the narrowing comes from him rather than from God. He has just sworn to mislead them all, and in the next breath he excludes a group.

The exception is the verse's central fact. A claimant who knows the limits of his power states them, and the stating is a form of knowledge. Iblīs knows that there are people he cannot mislead, and he knows it before he has tried them.

The Qur'an confirms the knowledge elsewhere. God says to Iblīs in al-Isrāʾ: you will truly have no authority over My faithful servants, and sufficient is your Lord as a guardian (17:65). The verse here has the adversary say what the other account has God say, and the agreement between the two is the point.

**"Your Sincere Servants"**
The phrase uses a form that denotes people made sincere, or people who are sincere, and the two readings carry different implications. On the first, the sincerity is conferred; on the second, it is achieved. The commentators note both, and the difference concerns where the protection comes from.

The reading that emphasises conferral locates the protection in God's act. The reading that emphasises achievement locates it in the servant's state. The Qur'an's usage elsewhere supports both: God chooses and guides, and the servant strives and is accepted.

The practical difference is small and the theological one is real. The verse attaches the exception to God — *Your* sincere servants — and the possessive makes the group His rather than theirs. The protection belongs to the relation, not to the quality.

The placement is reassuring for a reason the chapter has already given. Its account of David showed a man of the highest standing falling and returning (38:24), which means the relation can be damaged and repaired. A protection resting on an achieved quality would fail at the first lapse; a protection resting on the relation survives it.

**What Sincerity Means Here**
The word denotes a thing purified of admixture, and applied to a servant it means one whose devotion is unmixed. Nothing else is being served alongside God, and no other end is being pursued through the acts of worship.

The definition answers the situation the chapter has been describing. The notables of Makkah were not atheists; they worshipped, and the Qur'an records their claim that they worshipped their deities only to be brought nearer to God (39:3). Their devotion was mixed, and the mixture is what the verse's word excludes.

The exclusion is therefore available to the chapter's audience. The group Iblīs cannot reach is not defined by rank, wealth, knowledge or lineage — the categories the notables were using. It is defined by the absence of admixture, which is a condition any person can be in.

**The Exception as a Limit**
The verse states a boundary on the adversary's power, and the boundary is stated by the adversary. That is what makes it decisive. A limit declared by the one who would exceed it cannot be argued with.

The commentators draw the conclusion that God has permitted Iblīs to tempt people and lead the disbelievers astray, but has given him no authority over God's servants. The permission and the limit are both real, and the verse supplies the second.

The pairing explains the chapter's account of the world. Temptation is genuine, and it fails against a particular group. The failure is not because the temptation is weak but because the group is defined in a way the temptation cannot act on.

That definition is the verse's practical content. The group is not identified by strength of will, by knowledge or by immunity; it is identified by the absence of a second loyalty. A person with one object of devotion presents nothing for beautification to attach to, and the verse is describing a condition rather than a temperament.

**The Same Exception in Al-Ḥijr, and What the Verse Offers**
The parallel account gives the same exception in the same position, immediately after the oath to mislead (15:40). The wording differs slightly but the substance is identical, and the commentators note the correspondence.

Al-Aʿrāf frames the exception differently, with God stating that Iblīs will have no authority except over those who follow him from among the perverse (7:155 in a related formulation; cf. 15:42). The formulations agree on the group excluded and differ on who states the exclusion.

The agreement across accounts has a function. The limit on the adversary is not a local feature of one sūrah but a fixed element of the Qur'an's presentation, and the verse here states it in the form most difficult to dispute — the adversary's own words.

The verse identifies a group that is protected, and the identifying mark is sincerity. The chapter has been describing an audience that measures people by wealth, rank and tribal standing, and the verse replaces those measures with one.

The replacement is the chapter's answer to the objection raised in verse 6. The notables asked whether God had favoured the poor believers out of all people, and the verse answers by naming the quality that determines the favour. It is not standing; it is the absence of admixture in devotion.

The answer is also an invitation. The group is not closed, and its membership is not inherited. The verse describes a condition rather than a class, and a condition can be entered.

**The Exception Stated by the Wrong Party**
The most striking feature of the verse is who states the limit. God could have imposed the restriction from above, as He does in al-Isrāʾ (17:65); here the adversary states it himself, in the same sentence in which he announces his intention.

The self-statement makes the limit undeniable. A restriction declared by the one who would remove it cannot be treated as an optimistic interpretation offered by the believers. It is the adversary's own assessment of his reach, given before he has begun.

The assessment also reveals knowledge he could not have obtained by experience. He has not yet tested any human being under the new arrangement, and he already knows that a group exists which he cannot touch. The knowledge is not empirical, and the verse does not explain its source.

The unexplained source is worth leaving unexplained. What matters for the reader is that the limit is conceded by the party who would remove it, and the concession is stated before any test has been run. A limit admitted in advance is stronger evidence than a limit discovered afterwards.

**Sincerity Against Beautification**
The two verses together describe an encounter. One party beautifies and misdirects; the other is defined by an unmixed devotion that the beautification cannot act on. The verse supplies the second term of the encounter.

The reason sincerity defeats beautification is structural. Beautification works by attaching a desirable appearance to something the person already partly wants, and the attachment requires a mixed intention to fasten onto. A devotion with nothing else in it offers no fastening point.

The chapter has described the opposite case in detail. The notables' devotion was mixed with tribal pride, and the mixture is what made their refusal so durable. The verse names the alternative without argument, and the naming is the whole of its counsel.

The refusal to argue is consistent with the chapter's closing method. Its argumentative work finished long before this point; what remains is description and declaration. A reader who has followed the sūrah does not need to be persuaded that a mixed devotion is vulnerable, because he has watched one fail for eighty verses.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:84

> Allah concluded, “The truth is—and I ˹only˺ say the truth—

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Unfinished Statement**
The verse records God speaking again, and the speech stops before its content arrives. The truth is — and the sentence waits for the next verse. The construction is the same one the chapter used for the grant of respite, where the giving and its limit were split across two verses.

The splitting has a specific effect here. The assertion is stated twice before anything is asserted, and the double assertion makes what follows arrive with weight already applied to it. The reader is told that truth is being spoken before being told what the truth is.

The commentators describe the verse as enigmatic, and the description is accurate. Its literal form is *the truth, and I speak the truth*, and the two halves do not obviously connect. The connection is supplied by the readings.

**The Readings**
The commentators offer several, and they differ on whether the verse is a statement about God or a statement about what follows.

The first family of readings takes the verse as a continuation of God's words to Iblīs and understands it as a claim about the speaker — that He is the truth, or that the truth proceeds from Him, and that His speech is truthful in either case. On these readings the verse stands alone as a declaration.

The second family connects it forward, taking it to mean that God establishes the truth and speaks truly, with the reference being to the statement in the next verse. On this reading the verse is a preface to the threat, and its whole function is to guarantee that the threat will not fail. Two further variants within the first family locate the truth in God's possession rather than His being.

**Which Reading Is Stronger**
The forward-looking reading has the better claim, and the reason is structural. The verse is followed by a colon in every reading, and the content that follows is a statement about filling Hell. A preface that guarantees the certainty of what comes next fits that position; a free-standing declaration about God's nature would more naturally stand alone.

The declaration readings are not excluded, and they carry support from the Qur'an's usage elsewhere. God is described as the Truth in al-Ḥajj (22:6) and in al-Muʾminūn (23:114), and the description is established. The verse could be making that claim in compressed form.

The two options are not in real competition. A statement that God is the Truth and that He speaks the truth is also a guarantee of what follows, and the compressed form allows both functions at once. The commentators preserve the plurality because the wording supports it.

The preservation is itself instructive. Where the exegetes record several readings without settling them, the verse is doing more than one job, and the reader is expected to hold the readings together rather than choose. The chapter relies on that habit elsewhere, most clearly in its treatment of the two hands in verse 75.

**The Ambiguity Retained, and the Verse Before the Threat**
The verse is short enough that a single meaning could have been chosen, and the choice was not made. The retention of the plurality is characteristic of the Qur'an's handling of compressed divine speech, and it has an effect on the reader.

The effect is to slow the reading. A statement whose sense must be worked through cannot be passed over, and the verse is placed immediately before the most severe statement in the narrative. The slowing is deliberate.

The ambiguity also protects the verse from being reduced to one function. Read as a declaration, it is theology; read as a preface, it is rhetoric. The verse does both, and the doing is why the commentators did not settle it.

The verse's position is what gives it its force. It precedes a statement about filling Hell, and it guarantees the statement before the statement is made.

The guarantee is necessary because the statement is the kind that could be doubted. A promise about the future, made about a being who has just been granted a long respite, invites the objection that the respite may change the outcome. The verse removes the objection by asserting the speaker's relation to truth.

The assertion also answers Iblīs's oath. He swore by God's might; the verse responds with a statement about God's truth. The two attributes are the ones the chapter named in its declaration (38:66), and the narrative closes on the same pair.

**Truth as the Chapter's Theme**
The word connects this verse to the chapter's earlier statements. The message was described as a great news (38:67), and the disputing of the people of the Fire was confirmed as true (38:64). The verse is the third use of the same idea, and the three uses mark the chapter's structure.

The first use describes the message; the second confirms the account of the Fire; the third guarantees the threat. The progression moves from what is proclaimed to what will happen to what is certain to happen, and each step uses the same word.

The progression answers the chapter's opening objection. The notables called the message an invention and a lie (38:4–5), and the chapter answers at three points by naming truth. The final naming is attached to a threat, and the attachment is the answer's sharpest form.

The threefold naming also mirrors the chapter's threefold structure. Its beginning concerns the Book, its middle the two destinations, and its end the origin of the enmity between them. Each part closes on the word, and the closing ties the parts together without any of them having to summarise the others.

**Why Truth Is Attached to a Threat**
The attachment deserves a moment's attention, because it is unusual. Truth is more often attached in the Qur'an to promises, to guidance and to the description of God Himself. Attaching it to a declaration about Hell reverses the expectation.

The reversal has a purpose. A promise can be dismissed as consolatory; a threat is harder to dismiss, because the person dismissing it has an interest in its falsity. The verse places the guarantee where the interest in denial is strongest, and the placing is deliberate.

The same logic governs the chapter's treatment of the two destinations. The Garden was described with an emphasis particle (38:54) and the Fire with an exclamation (38:56). The verse completes the pattern by attaching truth itself to the second of the two.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:85

> I will surely fill up Hell with you and whoever follows you from among them, all together.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Statement, Addressed to the Adversary Directly**
The verse supplies what the previous verse withheld, and the content is a threat stated in the first person. Hell will be filled, and the filling is described with two groups named: Iblīs himself, and those among human beings who follow him.

The statement is the conclusion of the narrative that began at verse 71. Fifteen verses have covered the creation, the command, the refusal, the expulsion, the curse, the respite and the oath. The narrative ends with a declaration about where all of it leads.

The ending is not a resolution. Nothing is reconciled and no one repents. The verse closes the account by stating its outcome, and the outcome is a filling.

The statement is spoken to Iblīs, and the direct address has consequences. He is told what will happen to him and to those who follow him, and the telling happens before the respite begins.

The telling removes the possibility of ignorance. The chapter has been concerned throughout with what its audience knows and when they know it, and the verse applies the same concern to the adversary. He is informed of the outcome at the outset, which means his subsequent activity is conducted with full knowledge of where it ends.

The informing also answers the oath. Iblīs swore to mislead them all and excepted the sincere; the verse responds by naming what awaits him and the remainder. The two statements are matched, and the matching closes the exchange.

**The Same Threat Elsewhere**
The Qur'an makes the same statement in two other places, and the wording is close enough to indicate a fixed formulation. Hūd records that the Word of the Lord will be fulfilled, that Hell will be filled with jinn and humans all together (11:119). Al-Sajdah gives the same sentence as a word that has already gone forth (32:13).

The commentators note the correspondence and add that here and in al-Aʿrāf the promise is made directly to Iblīs rather than stated in the third person (7:18). The direct address changes the character of the statement: it is not a description of a decree but a sentence pronounced on the one hearing it.

The three occurrences together establish the threat as a settled element of the Qur'an's eschatology. It is not a local remark in one sūrah but a repeated declaration, and the repetition indicates that the filling is certain rather than contingent.

The certainty is what distinguishes this threat from the warnings earlier in the chapter. Those were addressed to people who could still avoid them; this one is addressed to a being whose course is already set, and it names an outcome that his activity will not alter. The filling includes him.

**"All Together"**
The phrase closes the statement, and its function is to prevent a reading in which the two groups are treated separately. Iblīs and his followers are filled into the same place at the same time.

The phrase also echoes the account of the angels' prostration, where they prostrated all together (38:73). The narrative began with a unanimous obedience and ends with a unanimous punishment, and the same phrase marks both. The echo is the chapter's way of measuring the distance between the two.

The word has a further implication for the followers. They are not admitted separately or ranked within the Fire; they arrive with the one they followed. The chapter has already described the leaders and followers disputing inside the Fire (38:59–61), and the verse states that the disputing parties share a single destination.

**What the Filling Implies**
The verb of filling implies a quantity and a completion. A vessel that is filled has a capacity, and the capacity will be reached. The verse is therefore not merely a threat but a statement about an outcome that is fixed in advance.

The Qur'an connects the filling to a word that has already gone forth (32:13; 11:119), which places it in the category of decreed matters. The chapter has used the same category for the term of the respite — the appointed day — and the two belong together.

The connection matters for the reader's understanding of the interval. The period in which the adversary operates is bounded at one end by the appointed day and at the other by a filling that has been decreed. The interval is real and it is contained.

The containment is the verse's reassurance, and it is stated without softening the threat. Nothing here suggests the filling will be gentle or partial; what it suggests is that the filling ends something. An interval with a decreed end is a period of testing rather than a permanent arrangement, and the verse is describing the close of a test.

**The Narrative's End and the Chapter's Turn**
The verse closes the account of Adam and Iblīs, and the closure is abrupt. There is no summary, no moral and no final exchange. The threat is stated and the narrative stops.

The abruptness is the chapter's method. It has been ending its sections this way throughout: the account of the destroyed peoples ends with the reason for their destruction, the account of the two destinations ends with the disputing of the people of the Fire, and the narrative of Adam ends with the filling.

The turn that follows is back to the Prophet. The chapter will give him three more verses to speak, and the speaking is the alternative the narrative has been preparing. The story of the first refusal is complete, and what remains is the message.

**Why the Story Is Told at the End**
The placement of this narrative has been noted by the commentators, who observe that it is the last account of the creation in the textual order of the Qur'an and most likely the first in the order of revelation. The two facts together explain the chapter's treatment of it.

A first-revealed account can be brief, because it establishes the outline. A last-repeated account can also be brief, because the outline is by then familiar. The chapter gives the story in fifteen verses and omits what the other accounts contain — the exchange with the angels, the Garden, the fall of Adam and Eve, the acceptance of his repentance.

The omissions are the chapter's selection, and the selection is governed by its subject. The sūrah is concerned with arrogance and its outcome, and it takes from the story exactly the elements that bear on that concern. The rest it leaves to the other accounts.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:86

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “I do not ask you for any reward for this ˹Quran˺, nor do I pretend to be someone I am not

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter Returns to the Prophet**
The narrative of Adam has closed, and the chapter turns back to the address that occupied verses 65 to 70. The command is the same — *say* — and the content is a statement about the Prophet's own position.

The return is significant. The chapter has now told the story of the first refusal, and it responds by having the Messenger describe himself. The description is not a summary of the narrative but an application of it: the one who brings the message is defined by what he does not claim.

The placement also answers the accusation recorded at the chapter's opening. The notables said the Qur'an was an invention and that the Prophet was seeking standing (38:4–8). The verse has him deny both, in his own voice, at the end of the sūrah.

**"I Ask No Reward of You"**
The denial concerns payment. The Prophet does not ask his audience for anything in return for delivering the message, and the denial removes the most common explanation for a claimant's behaviour.

The Qur'an uses the same formulation for five other prophets, and the repetition is exact. Nūḥ, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lūṭ and Shuʿayb each say *I ask no reward of you* in al-Shuʿarāʾ (26:109, 127, 145, 164, 180). The commentators note the correspondence and understand the phrase as referring to the delivery of the revelation.

Nūḥ states the principle most fully. If you turn away, he says, I have not asked you for any reward; my reward lies only with God, and I am commanded to be among those who submit (10:72). The sentence gives the reason for the denial: the reward comes from elsewhere, so the audience is not being charged.

The placement of the denial in this chapter carries an extra edge. It comes after the story of Solomon, who was given a kingdom no one after him would match (38:35), and after the account of Job, whose losses were returned doubled (38:43). A reader who has just read those accounts knows that God does reward His servants, and the verse distinguishes that reward from payment by an audience.

**Why the Denial Matters, and What the Message Is Not**
The commentators identify the absence of a demand for payment as a mark of true prophethood. A claimant who asks for reward has an interest in the claim being accepted, and the interest undermines the claim.

The point has a specific application in Makkah. The notables had offered the Prophet wealth, leadership and marriage in exchange for abandoning the message, and the reports of those offers were current among the audience. The verse answers them by denying that any such exchange was sought.

The denial also protects the message's reception. A person who is not paying can evaluate what he hears without calculating a cost, and the verse establishes the condition in which the message can be judged on its content. The chapter has been asking for exactly that judgement throughout.

The two denials together define the message by exclusion. It is not a transaction, and it is not a performance. What remains is the content, and the content has been given in the verses about God's oneness and the two destinations.

The chapter's structure makes the definition cumulative. The declaration in verses 65–66 said what the message proclaims; the description in verse 67 said what it is; this verse says what it is not. The three together complete the account.

The completion is placed at the end for a reason. A reader who has followed the whole sūrah arrives at the denials knowing what the message contains, and the denials then remove the two explanations his opponents were offering. What is left is the message itself.

**"Nor Am I Among the Pretenders"**
The second denial uses a word — *mutakallifūn* — that denotes people who impose a cost on themselves to appear as what they are not, or who claim what has not been given to them. The commentators gloss it as imposters, and also as those who impose themselves.

The denial is addressed to Quraysh specifically, and the commentators note that it reminds them of a known fact: he had never feigned a position, sought a rank that was not his, or claimed something he did not have. The denial appeals to their own knowledge of his life before the revelation.

The appeal is effective because it is verifiable. A man's history in a small city is public, and the Prophet's history included no claim of any kind until the revelation arrived at the age of forty. The verse asks the audience to check a fact they already hold.

**What the Word Imposes on the Person Using It**

The denial turns on a word worth holding onto, because it describes a cost rather than a lie. A *mutakallif* is not simply someone who is mistaken about himself; he is someone who takes a burden on himself in order to appear as what he is not. The root carries the sense of load, and the form carries the sense of straining under it.

That is a precise description, and it fits the men the chapter has been describing. Claiming a share in the distribution of revelation is a burden: it obliges a person to explain why the message went elsewhere, to defend a rank he cannot produce, and to keep the claim alive in public. The notables had been carrying that burden for years, and the verse names the carrying rather than the claim.

The naming is more damaging than an accusation of lying would be. A liar can be exposed and the matter ends; a person straining under a false position is described in the middle of the strain, and the description does not end until the position is dropped. The verse offers the dropping, and the offer is the point of the denial.

The distribution is what settles the question. Every feature the word describes is visible in the conduct of the men making the accusation — the contention over rank, the claim to the treasuries of mercy (38:9), the demands that the unseen be produced on schedule. None of it is visible in the conduct of the man receiving the accusation, whose life before the revelation contained no claim of any kind. The verse asks the audience to look at where the strain actually is.

**The Denial and the Chapter's Other Servants**
The verse belongs to a pattern the Qur'an establishes across its accounts of the messengers. Each of them is shown refusing payment, and the refusal is recorded in their own words rather than in a narrator's summary.

The recording has an evidential function. A claim that the prophets asked nothing could be made by anyone; the Qur'an places the claim in the mouths of the prophets themselves and repeats it five times in one sūrah alone. The repetition makes the claim part of the message rather than a comment on it.

The function matters for this chapter's audience. The notables were evaluating the Messenger by the standards applied to poets and soothsayers, both of whom were paid. The verse removes him from that market entirely, and the removal is the answer to the evaluation.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:87

> It is only a reminder to the whole world

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Nothing but a Reminder"**
The verse describes the Qur'an in a single phrase, and the phrase is restrictive. Nothing but — the restriction excludes every other category the audience had proposed. It is not poetry, not magic, not the speech of a man being taught, not an invention.

The restriction also excludes categories the audience had not proposed but might have assumed. The Qur'an is not a history, not a legal code in the first instance, not a book of prophecy about specific events. It is a reminder, and the naming determines how it is to be received.

The Qur'an describes itself this way repeatedly. Al-Anʿām, Yūsuf, al-Qalam and al-Takwīr all use the same formula (6:90; 12:104; 68:52; 81:27), and the consistency indicates that the description is a settled self-characterisation rather than a local remark.

**What a Reminder Presupposes**
A reminder is given to someone who already knows something and has stopped attending to it. The word therefore makes a claim about the audience: they are not being told something wholly new, but being called back to something they possess.

The claim is supported by the Qur'an's account of the primordial covenant, in which all human beings acknowledged God's lordship before their birth (7:172). The reminder calls back to that acknowledgement, and the calling is why the message can be recognised rather than merely learned.

The chapter has been operating on this assumption throughout. It does not argue its audience into belief; it presents the two destinations, tells the stories of the prophets, and asks the audience to notice what they already accept. The verse names the method.

The method explains a feature of the chapter that would otherwise look like evasion. The notables demanded proof and were given narrative. Narrative is the right instrument for a reminder, because a person recognises himself in a story before he assents to a proposition, and the recognition is what the verse is after.

**"For the Worlds"**
The scope of the reminder is universal, and the word used is the plural form that denotes all created worlds — human beings, jinn, and every community across time. The Qur'an is not described as a reminder for one people or one period.

The universality answers an objection the chapter recorded. The notables treated the message as a local matter, a claim by one man in their city about their gods. The verse places it in a different category entirely, and the placing is the answer.

The universality also connects to the narrative that has just closed. The adversary's oath concerned all human beings (38:82), and the reminder is addressed to the same scope. The two universals are matched, and the matching is the chapter's final statement of what is at stake.

**What a Reminder Does and Does Not Do**
A reminder does not compel. It presents, and the presentation leaves the response with the person reminded. The verse's description therefore contains the chapter's account of why the message does not force belief.

The Qur'an states the limit explicitly. The Prophet is told that he is not a controller over people (88:22) and that if God willed, all on earth would believe, so he is not to compel them (10:99). The reminder is the instrument, and the instrument has a defined reach.

The limit is also the audience's exposure. A reminder that is not compelling places the whole weight of the outcome on the person who hears it, and the chapter has been building toward that placement since its second verse.

The description excludes several things the audience wanted. A reminder is not a sign produced on demand, not a punishment brought forward, not a demonstration of power. Each of those was requested (38:16), and each is outside the category the verse names.

The exclusion is the verse's sharpest edge. The notables had set terms for belief, and the verse states that the message does not meet those terms because it is a different kind of thing. The refusal to meet the terms is not a failure but a definition.

The chapter has been making the same point through its structure. It gives narrative where signs were demanded, and description where proof was demanded. The verse names the category that explains the structure.

**The Verse in the Closing Sequence**
The verse is the second of the chapter's final three, and its position between the Prophet's self-description and the closing promise gives it a specific function. Verse 86 said what the Messenger is not; this verse says what the message is; the verse that follows says what will happen.

The sequence moves from the messenger to the message to the outcome, and the movement is the chapter's last argument. A messenger who asks nothing, bringing a reminder to everyone, about a matter that will be known — the three elements together leave the audience with no alternative explanation to offer.

The verse is also the shortest complete description of the Qur'an in the sūrah. Six words in Arabic, and the words have been the chapter's subject from its opening letter to this point.

**A Description That Answers the Opening**
The sūrah began with an oath by the Qur'an, full of reminder, and the notables responded that the message was an invention and its bearer a liar. The verse returns to the opening description and states it again, without qualification and after everything the chapter has said.

The return is not a repetition but a confirmation. The opening made the claim; the intervening eighty-six verses supplied the material; the verse restates the claim with the material behind it. A reader who has followed the chapter receives the restatement as a conclusion rather than as an assertion.

The chapter's use of a single opening letter is also relevant. Ṣād is one of the muqaṭṭaʿāt, and this sūrah is one of four named after its opening letter. The naming places the Book itself at the head of the chapter, and the final verse returns to the same object. The frame is closed on the Qur'an rather than on the audience.

The closing on the Book is the chapter's final claim about itself. It began by swearing on the Qur'an and ends by describing the Qur'an, and everything between — the prophets, the destinations, the story of Adam — is held inside that frame. The audience is addressed throughout, but it is not what the sūrah is about.

**Reminder Without Coercion**
The description settles a question the chapter's audience kept raising. They asked for signs that would compel belief — angels brought down, the Book sent in pieces, the punishment produced on demand (38:16; cf. 17:90–93). A reminder is not that kind of instrument.

The verse therefore answers the demand by reclassifying the thing demanded from. The message was never going to produce compulsion, because compulsion is not what a reminder does. The demand was addressed to a category the message does not belong to.

The reclassification is the chapter's final word on its opponents' method. They had been evaluating the revelation against standards drawn from magic, poetry and soothsaying. The verse gives it a category of its own, and the category determines what can be asked of it.

The determination cuts both ways. A reminder cannot compel, and it therefore cannot be blamed for failing to compel; but a reminder also cannot be dismissed, because dismissing it does not change what it recalls. The verse leaves the audience with an instrument that offers them nothing and requires everything.

---
## Sūrah Ṣād 38:88

> And you will certainly know its truth before long.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Promise of Knowledge**
The chapter's final verse is a statement about the future, and the statement concerns knowledge rather than events. The audience will know, and what they will know is the truth of what has just been described as a reminder.

The framing is significant. The chapter does not end with a threat, a promise of reward or a command. It ends with an assertion that the audience's present position will be replaced by knowledge, and the replacement is certain.

The certainty is carried by two particles in the Arabic — a prefixed letter of futurity and an emphatic particle — and the combination produces a statement that admits no qualification. Not probably, not eventually for some; certainly, and for all.

The universality is the part that bears on the audience. The notables had treated the message as a claim they could decline, and declining left them where they were. The verse removes that option by making the knowledge unavoidable: the question is not whether they will find out but when.

**"Before Long"**
The phrase denotes a time that is near, and the nearness is relative rather than measured. What is long for a person is short in the scale the verse is using, and the commentators differ on which scale is meant.

The phrase has a function beyond its literal sense. It removes the assumption that the interval is open-ended, which is the assumption the chapter has been contesting since the notables asked for the punishment to be hastened (38:16). They believed the delay meant the warning was empty; the verse says the delay is short.

The shortness also answers the respite narrative. Iblīs was given time until the appointed day, and the verse tells the audience that from their side the same period reads as brief. The two perspectives on one interval are the chapter's final contrast.

**Two Readings of When**
The commentators give two identifications of the moment, and both are supported by the wording. The first takes it as the knowledge that comes after death and after the Resurrection, when what the Qur'an said is seen to be true.

The second takes it as the victory and spread of Islam, which the audience would witness in history rather than in the hereafter. On this reading the knowledge arrives within the lifetime of some who heard the verse.

The two readings are not in conflict, and the wording accommodates both. The chapter has described a Day of Reckoning that cannot be postponed and a reminder addressed to all worlds; the knowledge of its truth comes at the first encounter with either. The commentators preserve both because the verse does not choose.

**The Certainty and the Present, and What the Verse Leaves**
The verse's force depends on the audience's current state, and the state is one of not knowing. They have heard the message, disputed it, mocked its bearer and demanded signs. The verse tells them that the dispute will end in knowledge.

The telling is not a threat of punishment but a statement about certainty. A person can be punished without being convinced; the verse promises conviction, and conviction is the more severe of the two because it arrives without the possibility of acting on it.

The chapter has already described that arrival. The people of the Fire are shown asking where the believers are, and continuing to ask without understanding (38:62–63). The verse is describing the same condition from the other side of the interval.

Three things remain when the verse ends. A message that asks nothing of its hearer, a reminder addressed to every world, and a certainty that its truth will be known before long.

The three are the chapter's conclusion, and they are given without elaboration. No summary is offered, no final exhortation is added, and no further argument is made. The chapter stops where its material stops.

The stopping is itself a statement. A book that is a reminder does not need to argue for its own acceptance; it states what it is and when its truth will be evident, and leaves the interval to the reader. The interval is the only part of the matter in which anything can still be done.

**The Chapter's Last Word**
The sūrah ends on a future tense, and the ending is a choice. A chapter that began with an oath by the Qur'an and moved through prophecy, eschatology and the story of Adam closes by telling its audience that they will know.

The closing returns the chapter to its opening subject. The first verses concerned the Qur'an and the arrogance of those who rejected it; the last verse concerns the same Qur'an and the certainty that its truth will be known. The circle is closed on the same object.

The closing also places the reader inside the verse. The audience addressed is not a historical group only; the promise is made to whoever hears the reminder, and the hearing is the condition of the knowing. The verse is the chapter's final application of its own description of itself.

**The Knowing and the Two Destinations**
The verse connects to the chapter's central section in a way worth stating. The two destinations were described in detail, and the description was presented as information about a future event. The verse confirms that the information will become knowledge, and the confirming is the guarantee of the description.

The connection also explains why the chapter gave the description at all. A reader who will come to know the truth does not need to be told in advance; the telling is what makes the difference between knowing in time and knowing too late. The verse names the alternative it is trying to prevent.

The people of the Fire were shown in the same state. They were asking questions whose answers they could no longer act on (38:62–63), and their questions are the form the promised knowledge takes when it arrives after the interval closes.

The verse therefore offers knowledge in two forms and invites the reader to choose between them. The same facts can be known now, while they can still be answered, or later, while they cannot. The chapter has spent eighty-eight verses making the facts available in the first form.

**The Interval as the Chapter's Subject**
The verse's promise locates the reader in a period, and the period is the whole of what remains. Everything the chapter has described lies on one side or the other of its end, and the reader stands inside it.

That location is the chapter's final achievement. Eighty-eight verses have moved from an oath, through prophecy and kingship and suffering, through the two destinations and the story of the first refusal, to a single sentence about a date that has not arrived.

The sentence leaves the reader with nothing to dispute and everything to do. The message has been delivered, its character has been stated, its truth has been guaranteed, and the time in which it can be answered is still running.

That is the whole of the chapter's demand. It does not ask the reader to resolve the dispute with the notables, to settle the exegesis of the Adam narrative, or to rank the patriarchs. It asks him to notice that the interval is open, and to act inside it.
